| 7 | CaCO₃ 0.10 g + HCl 1 M, 3.0 mL | Fizzing; solid dissolves completely (acid in excess) | Eye |
| 8 | HCl 6 M + NaOH 6 M | Nothing visible; tube gets hot (about 40 °C rise) | Eye, touch or thermometer |
| 9 | CoCl₂ 0.5 M + AgNO₃ 0.1 M | White AgCl precipitate; liquid stays pink (Ag⁺ is limiting) | Eye |
| 10 | Mg 0.012 g + HCl 3 M, 5.0 mL | H₂ bubbles; ribbon dissolves; tube warms (about 11 °C rise); trapped gas pops with a lit splint | Eye, lit splint, touch |

This table is the readable summary; each station's structured `expected` record is the test oracle.

The eye is a tool at every station (corrected on 2026-10-07, as the screens were built). The table first gave stations 6, 8 and 10 only their other tools, and the record followed it to the letter, so those three never asked what is seen. A learner always looks at the tube: nothing to see is itself the observation at stations 6 and 8, and the bubbles and the ribbon that dissolves are at station 10. Every station now asks for the solid, the colour of the liquid and the bubbles, beside what its other tools show. The stored entry's version stayed 1: the lab had no page before this, so no run had been stored.

Station notes:

- **2 and 3:** the graded net ionic equation is the simple carbonate (M²⁺ + CO₃²⁻ → MCO₃). An expanded note says the solid that really forms is a basic carbonate, such as Cu₂(OH)₂CO₃.
- **5:** the no-reaction control; the engine must not invent a product.
- **6:** the litmus tool has two positions. In the liquid it turns blue from excess NaOH alone, which proves nothing; held moist in the vapour it shows ammonia. Sniffing directly instead of wafting triggers a safety note. As built, "Smell the tube directly" is a button of the station's tools wherever wafting is a tool, which is station 6 alone; it is never done, and a press is answered with the record's caution, which ends by pointing to the waft tool. No vigorous bubbling is shown: ammonia is very soluble.
- **8:** only detectable by temperature. The touch tool carries a caution that the tube is hot and the contents corrosive. The thermometer gives the idealised rise and says which of the question's answers that is, as the touch does (added 2026-10-07): a rise of about 40 °C alone left warm or hot to a guess.

### 8.3 Learner input and checking

**Observations** are recorded with structured choices, not free text. The form shows only the fields the station's tools can observe; other fields are neither asked nor graded. An observation is correct when every shown field equals the station's `expected`. As built, each field is a group of radio buttons, and one button records and checks them all. A field that a tool shows is disabled until one of its tools has been used, with the tool to use under it; a check made while such a field still waits says that some questions are not open yet and that each says under it what to do first (2026-10-07: it had said to choose an answer for each question, which cannot be done for one that is not open). The eye's three fields wait in the same way, each for a look at its own part of the drawn tube: the bottom for the solid, the liquid for its colour, the liquid's surface for the bubbles (added 2026-10-07, after the owner used the page; section 8.5). In the engine a look is a use of the eye, stored with the station as a tool's use is, and an observation of a part never looked at is refused. The field for the solid asks what solid is in the tube once any reaction is over (reworded 2026-10-07 from "Solid in the tube after mixing", which some read as asking what went in: at stations 7 and 10 the powder and the ribbon dissolve, and the answer is "no solid"). Feedback names the fields to look at again and never what they should be; a correct observation is shown with its fields disabled and a "Change" button, as an entry made in the titration lab is.

**Equations.** For each station the learner enters molecular, complete ionic, and net ionic equations with a species palette: they pick species offered for that station (including distractors), set coefficients and states, and place each on the reactant or product side. The palette always offers "No reaction", which is the correct answer at station 5. An equation is correct when the species on each side match the curated equation, each state is in that species' accepted list (NH₃ accepts aq or g), and the coefficients are proportional. No free-text equation parsing. As built, each of the three is a builder with no dialog and nothing typed. It was rebuilt on 2026-10-07: the first one asked of each species chosen which side it goes on, in two buttons, and gave each term a row of controls of its own, and the owner, who is not a chemist, found it hard to read and asked for the equation as empty slots filled from a bank of tiles, as a language lesson has a bank of word tiles. The equation is now a row of slots with the plus signs and the arrow drawn between them, the reactants left of the arrow and the products right of it, and over it the station's palette as a bank of tiles. One slot is the active one, drawn with a thick outline and named as active: a tile that is pressed goes into it, in place of whatever it held, and the next empty slot becomes the active one; a press of a slot makes that one active. A tile is not used up, since a spectator stands on both sides, and the same species twice on one side is refused. A tile can also be dragged, with a mouse or a finger, from the bank onto a slot, from one slot to another, where two full slots change places, and out of the row, which empties its slot. Nothing is done by dragging alone: Backspace or Delete on a slot empties it, and the arrow keys move along the slots. The term last put in or pressed has, in one line between the bank and the row, its coefficient, stepped from 1 to 9 (the curated equations need 3 at most; the engine itself takes up to 99), a choice of state, and a way to take it out; in its slot it is drawn as it is written, "3OH⁻(aq)", the coefficient only where it is more than 1. (Until 2026-10-17 the row came first, then that line, then the bank: at 768 px wide a tile that put the row onto a second line moved the bank about 50 px down under the hand. The bank and the line now stand over the row, as the types of reactions lab has them, by the shared builder's `toolsFirst`.) In Guided, where the station has a reaction, each side has as many slots as the correct equation has terms: a scaffold, as the hints are. The engine's view of the station hands the page those two counts and nothing else of the equation. In Challenge at all times, and in Guided at the station where nothing reacts, a side is its terms and one empty slot after them, so that the row gives neither the count of terms nor that there is no equation to write. A new term is written as dissolved, (aq), which is the learner's to change: that is a choice of the page, made so that an ion costs one press, and it means the state of a solid, a liquid or a gas is always the learner's own act. The thirty equations of a faultless lab take 246 presses in Guided and 273 in Challenge, where the first builder took 378. The engine takes an equation at any time, before the tube is mixed too, so the builders are on the station from the start. "Check" enters the equation; the feedback says what kind of thing is wrong (ions in a molecular equation, a compound not split, a spectator left in, the substances or their sides, a state, the balance, or an equation where none is wanted and the reverse) and never the answer.

**Guided mode** shows hints and the expected observations up front. **Challenge mode** hides them until the learner records their own, then checks. As built, the hints are over the three builders, and the expected observations are under the observation form, which is there once the tube is mixed, in a list that is closed until the learner opens it and is named "What a correct observation holds" (2026-10-07: the list had stood open over the form, and the owner found that the page then said the same thing three times; Guided still offers it from the start, but no longer shows it unasked). In Challenge a sentence in their place says when they come, and they come once an observation has been recorded, right or not, the list closed as it is in Guided. The mode can be changed at any time. The shell's two sentences beside the mode's radio buttons are the titration lab's, so this lab hands the shell its own.

The lab is completed when all ten stations have correct observations and equations. Attempts are unlimited. Progress is saved per station: as built, with each action the engine takes (a mix, a part of the tube looked at, a tool used, an observation checked, an equation checked), as section 10 says. A completed lab takes no more entries; the one reason is then said once at the head of each station, where it is the description of the button of each of the station's four entries (2026-10-07: it had stood under each entry's button, four times on a station), and Restart starts a new run.

### 8.4 Acceptance

- For each station, the engine's output agrees with the station's `expected` record: precipitate ID, gas ID, temperature class, remaining coloured ions; and its fired rule and product IDs agree with the curated net ionic equation. Station 5 returns no reaction.
- The limiting reagent matches the oracle: OH⁻ at station 1, Ag⁺ at station 9, the solid fully consumed at stations 7 and 10.
- Computed temperature rise: 35–45 °C at station 8; 8–14 °C at station 10.
- Every curated equation balances in atoms and charge, and no net ionic equation has a species unchanged on both sides.
- The equation checker accepts doubled coefficients and rejects a distractor species.
- `describeStationResult` (the text description of what is seen) is tested for every station. Since 2026-10-07 the page does not show it: it shows what is seen in one part of the tube when that part is looked at. Those three sentences are tested for every station too, and are held to this description, to the drawing and to the station's `expected` record.

### 8.5 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-07, straight through, as the titration lab's screens were (section 13), on the shell that lab made. The route is `#/lab/aqueous`, loaded lazily; Home and the list of labs link it with its status.

- **Lab tab: one station at a time.** A list of the ten stations says in words, beside a mark, how far each has come: not started, started (the tube mixed or an equation entered), observed (the observation correct), done. "Station 3 of 10" is over the station's heading, and Previous and Next under it are named for the stations they go to. Where the tab is narrow the list folds behind a button, as the primer's topics do; the two are one component. A station that has been shown stays in the page, hidden, so that what was chosen or built there and not yet checked is still there on coming back. The ten are never on the page together: the owner found a crowded page hard to use on a tablet.
- **A station is a short column of cards.** The tube: its two reagents as tiles, each with its name, what it looks like in its bottle, its amount and its hazards, and "Mix"; then a drawn test tube on a white card, as the titration flask is, with the liquid in its colour, a layer of solid where one forms and bubbles where a gas comes off, which the learner explores (the next point). The tools, where the station has any but the eye: a button for each way of applying one, and a message line that says what the tool shows. The observations. The three equation builders. The station's notes, once the station is done. The station's Sources. The tools and the observations are there once the tube is mixed. The notes first came as soon as the observation was correct; they were moved on 2026-10-07, in the review of the phase, because a note may name a product or give an equation outright (the control's says that the answer to all three equations is "No reaction", and those of stations 2 and 3 give the net ionic equation that is checked), and that is what the three builders are still to ask for, in Guided and in Challenge alike.
- **The tube is explored** (2026-10-07, after the owner used the page). The page had said what is seen three times over: in a sentence beside a small drawing, in the list of what to expect, and in the choices of the questions. The owner asked for a larger picture that is explored with the pointer, in place of the sentence, and for the expected observation to be closed. The tube is now drawn 300 px tall where its card has the room, and what is in it lies in three sections stacked as they are drawn: the surface of the liquid, where bubbles rise and break; the liquid; and the bottom, where a solid settles. Each section is a real button laid over the drawing, 58 px or more tall, the bottom with no solid in it and the surface with no bubbles too. A mouse pointer over a section, keyboard focus on it or a tap looks at it: the section is outlined, and a callout beside it says what is seen there and nothing else, the colour always in words. The callout goes when the pointer or focus leaves, at Escape, and on a touch screen when another section or anywhere else is tapped; its words are also put in a status line for a screen reader. No sentence on the page says the whole observation. Beside the picture are one line that says what to do, which is the picture's description, and a list of the three parts, each with a mark and the words for whether it has been looked at; a section not yet looked at has a dashed edge. Looking at a part opens its question (section 8.3) and is stored, so a reload keeps it. A completed lab stores nothing more and can still be looked at.
- **What a reagent looks like before the mix** (2026-10-07, at the owner's request) is said on its tile in words, "Looks: pink solution", and the liquid of a coloured solution is drawn in its bottle in that colour. A solution has the colour the record stores for its one coloured ion, which is the colour that ion gives a mixed tube; one with no coloured ion is "colourless solution" and its bottle is drawn as before; the powder and the ribbon have the appearance the record stores for them, in words alone. At the stations where a coloured ion is left over, the bottle's word is the word for the liquid afterwards. That was accepted: it is what a bench shows, the primer's card on spectator ions asks for the two to be compared, and the question about the liquid stays closed until the liquid is looked at.
- **The drawing** is worked from the engine's result, and the words for each part from the drawing and that result. The colour a solid is drawn in is the drawing's own value for the name the record stores; the name, which the bottom of the tube says when it is looked at, is what has a source. The bubbles rise three times, for under five seconds in all, and only where the reduced-motion setting allows; under it they are drawn at rest.
- **Three panels float over the page** and can be moved, by dragging or with the arrow keys: the solubility table, a real table with a row for each positive ion and a column for each negative ion, which says of a pair the record does not hold that it is not listed; the ions of the bench (2026-10-07), a real table with a row for each of the sixteen ions that a bottle gives or an equation of a station holds, with its name, its formula, its charge, whether it is one atom or a group, its colour in solution in a word and the bottles that hold it, and under it a second table of what is not an ion and is written whole (water, the three gases and the two bottles that are a solid), all of it read from the record, which for this stores a name with a source for each of those ions and molecules; and the calculator the titration lab has. The calculator's panel became a shared component for this. One cell of the table carries a footnote's sign beside its word (2026-10-07): ammonium with hydroxide, stored as soluble, which is the pair that reacts at station 6. A line under the table says that the two make no solid but do react, giving ammonia, and names the primer's card on gases. A panel that is open is held inside the window when the window is made smaller, as it is while it is dragged (2026-10-07). A panel is placed by an estimate of its height and, once it is drawn, again by the height it has (2026-10-07): the panel of ions is taller than a window 768 px high allows, and then scrolls inside itself.
- **Hit areas.** The controls that are drawn smaller than 44 px (the buttons that open the panels, a disclosure's summary, the list's button, Previous and Next, a panel's grip and close button) have a hit area of 44 px each way laid over them by the shell's stylesheet, which draws nothing and takes no room (2026-10-07). It is the shell's, so the titration lab has it too, and is laid out as before.
- **Record tab:** a table of the ten stations, with the observation and the three equations as they were last checked, each equation in one line, and whether each is correct; and, once all ten are done, the line that says the lab is complete, which the Lab tab says too.
- **The shell.** This lab hands the shell no docked notebook: what is entered is on the stations, and the bench has the Lab tab's whole width. No guide is handed either.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is chosen in the observation form or built in a builder and not yet checked, the station shown, which part of the tube has its callout open, and what the message lines last said. A reload opens on the first station that is begun and not done.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the hand checklist of section 13 was run by script, by keyboard, in headless Chromium, and by nobody at a keyboard. The record of the build is `docs/research/phase-4/build-record.md`.
- **The review of the phase** (2026-10-07) was made by an AI coding assistant that had not built the lab. It ran a whole lab in Challenge to completion by keyboard and, in a browser that emulates a touch screen, by tap, and made the changes dated 2026-10-07 in this section, with three to the primer: the card on the solubility table describes the grid the page draws and says where it opens from; the card on the three forms of an equation says what a precipitate is where the word first stands in the cards read first; and the card on the case where nothing reacts names the base that meets an ammonium ion beside the three cases that need an acid. What it found, did and left is the last section of the record of the build.

## 9. Mechanism timeline

### 9.1 Model

A mechanism is authored choreography. The scene state is a pure function `state(mechanism, t)` of the scrub position `t` in [0, 1]: atom positions, bond orders, electron-pair paths, active species, energy cursor, camera target, and explanation text. Scene components draw from `state` only, with no smoothing between frames, so scrubbing in either direction gives identical frames. The learner-controlled camera is the one exception.

**Data.** A mechanism has a fixed actor list (one stable `atomId` and element per atom for the whole timeline) and a keyframe at every step boundary, giving every actor's position and the bond list with orders. `state` interpolates positions between keyframes with a named easing; a bond whose order changes is drawn as partial. Keyframes are generated by a build script from a small authored file that places library species and maps library atom indices to actor IDs. Only the water molecules that take part in a step are explicit; bulk solvent, hydration shells, and the bubble stage are a non-atomistic backdrop.

As built, a step also has keyframes inside it wherever its choreography needs one: the timeline has fifteen, of which seven are at chapter boundaries. A keyframe gives, besides the positions and the bonds, what the actors make up there (each library species with its actors in its record's atom order, and each monatomic ion), which of those species are held together as one named unit (the two ions of one unit of the solid, until it has come apart), and what the camera frames (a point and a radius). The authored file names, for each keyframe, one actor of each species the camera is to hold, and the build gives the camera the smallest sphere that holds the centre of every atom of those species, so that nothing framed is cut by the edge of the picture. An actor can name the chapter it enters at: the record still places every actor at every keyframe, and the scene leaves out what has not been added yet (revised at the deep review of the phase, 2026-10-08, with what follows on units and on names). The authored file (`docs/research/phase-5/choreography.json`) places a species by three anchors, where one of its atoms is, which way a second points and which side a third is on, and not by a matrix: the move is a rotation and a shift, so no distance or angle inside the species changes. A species stays where it was put until one of its atoms is placed again. The build refuses a choreography in which a species is twisted out of its library shape at a chapter boundary, in which the scene's charge changes, in which a step's bonds broken and formed are not the difference between the bond lists at its two ends, in which two atoms sit on or pass through each other, or in which an actor enters apart from the rest of its species or its unit, or after a chapter that is about it.

**Step record:** time range on the track, bonds broken, bonds formed, electron paths, `deltaH` and `deltaG` (standard-state, each cited or marked as derived), optional `activationG` (cited, or the marker "schematic"), camera target, explanation text at `intro` and `expanded` depth, real timescale label, optional prediction question.

As built, the record differs from that list in these ways. An energy is stored with its value, whether it is worked out directly from cited values or derived, the method (formation values, an acid constant, a remainder, or the Eyring equation) and the sources it rests on; the build works each one out from the cited values in `docs/research/phase-5/mechanism.json`, and none is typed in. `deltaH` is null for a step that has no enthalpy of its own, and the next step's then names both steps as the ones it covers. `activationG` can also be a derived value, which step 3's is. A bond that is there before and after a step with another order is listed apart from the bonds broken and formed. Each end of an electron path is a lone pair on one atom or a bond between two. The camera is in the keyframes, not in the step. The timescale label is stored with its source, or with the reason it has none. A step names the change its formation values were taken over, and carries its own notes. Every chapter, a bookend and the prologue too, has its range, its two depths of text and the list of actors it is about.

**Features:** chapters with deep links (`?step=proton-transfer`); a non-linear track with each chapter's real timescale as a label; camera auto-framing that the learner can override and that resumes on release; beaker bookends at the start and end. The timeline is a native range input and announces on chapter change only.

**Modes.** The mechanism has the same Guided/Challenge selector as the labs. Challenge pauses before key steps to ask "What happens next?"; Guided skips the pauses. The mechanism is completed when the learner has reached `t` = 1. Beside the mode is "Start again", which asks first and then puts the run back at the start of the track with nothing answered, in the mode it was in (added at the deep review, 2026-10-08: clearing all progress in Settings was the only way before).

`state` gives the atoms that are in the scene at `t` and the species they make up there, each marked where the chapter is about it, and the electron paths only during the move of a step in which its bonds change. The species are named from the bonds as they are at `t`: a bond counts once it is more than half formed and until less than half of it is left, so a species becomes another half-way through the move in which its bonds change, and not at the keyframe that ends it (as first built it was the species of the last keyframe passed, and a hydronium ion was called one until the last frame of the proton's move). A unit of the solid is held by no bond and is one thing by the same rule: until the move that parts its two ions is more than half done. The state's energy cursor is for drawing: between two levels a curve is a drawing choice, so nothing prints the cursor's value (section 9.4).

### 9.2 HCl + NaHCO₃ → NaCl + H₂O + CO₂

Solid NaHCO₃ is added to hydrochloric acid. Track order: beaker bookend, prologue, steps 1–4, beaker bookend.

- **Prologue** (chapter `prologue`, deep-linkable, labelled "before mixing", no energy values): what is already in the bottle. Hydrochloric acid is fully ionised: H₃O⁺ and hydrated Cl⁻.
1. **Dissolving:** NaHCO₃ separates into hydrated Na⁺ and HCO₃⁻. Timescale: seconds, depending on particle size and stirring.
2. **Proton transfer:** H₃O⁺ gives its proton to HCO₃⁻, forming H₂CO₃. Timescale: picoseconds to nanoseconds.
3. **Decomposition:** H₂CO₃ splits into H₂O and dissolved CO₂ through a water-relayed proton shift. Timescale: tens of milliseconds; the slow step. (As built the label reads "tens of milliseconds; the slower of the two steps that change bonds": steps 1 and 4 are labelled in seconds, beside which "the slow step" read as a mistake. Changed at the deep review.)
4. **Gas escapes:** dissolved CO₂ gathers into bubbles and leaves; Na⁺ and Cl⁻ remain as spectators. Timescale: seconds.

**Energies.** The overall process is NaHCO₃(s) + H⁺(aq) → Na⁺(aq) + H₂O(l) + CO₂(g), with ΔH = +31.4 kJ/mol and ΔG = −42.4 kJ/mol at 298 K from the NBS tables.

- In the NBS tables, "H₂CO₃(aq)" means CO₂(aq) + H₂O. That entry must not be used for the true H₂CO₃ molecule shown in steps 2 and 3.
- Step 2 uses the cited true pKa of H₂CO₃. Step 3 is the NBS total for H⁺ + HCO₃⁻ → CO₂(aq) + H₂O minus step 2, labelled as derived.
- If no separate enthalpy for true H₂CO₃ can be cited, the enthalpy curve shows steps 2 and 3 as one segment.
- A barrier with no citation is drawn schematically and labelled "not to scale".
- Step 1's ΔG is slightly positive at standard state. It carries the note "slightly uphill at 1 M; it dissolves because the acid removes HCO₃⁻ as it forms". (As built, since the deep review: "Slightly uphill when both ions are at 1 M. It dissolves all the same: the ions start far more dilute than that, and the acid removes HCO₃⁻ as it forms." The first wording read as if the solid would not dissolve in water without the acid, which it does.)

The energy panel plots free energy as the main curve with enthalpy alongside. Its note reads: heat is absorbed in dissolving the solid and releasing the gas; the reaction proceeds because escaping gas increases entropy. This leads into Gibbs free energy.

Monatomic ions (Na⁺, Cl⁻) are drawn as spheres sized by cited ionic radii: each at the same fraction of its ionic radius as an atom's ball is of its van der Waals radius, a quarter, so that every ball in the scene is at one scale (as first built an ion was drawn at its whole ionic radius beside atoms at a quarter of theirs, and a sodium ion looked as large as a hydrogen carbonate ion; changed at the deep review, 2026-10-08). Polyatomic species use the library's 3D records.

The literature values for true H₂CO₃ are identified and cited before the mechanism phase's plan is written.

**As built.** `docs/research/phase-5/sources/README.md` says how each value was read, what is worked out from what, and what could not be sourced. In short:

| Step | ΔG, kJ/mol | ΔH, kJ/mol | Barrier |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 Dissolving | +2.325, from the NBS tables | +18.70, from the NBS tables | none: not one move over a barrier |
| 2 Proton transfer | −19.92, from the pKa of the carbonic acid molecule, 3.49 (Pines and others, 2016) | none of its own | schematic: drawn 10 kJ/mol high, a drawing parameter, and labelled not to scale |
| 3 Decomposition | −16.419, derived: the NBS total for steps 2 and 3 less step 2 | −7.64 for steps 2 and 3 together, from the NBS tables | about 66 kJ/mol, derived by the Eyring equation from a lifetime of about 60 ms |
| 4 Gas escapes | −8.379, from the NBS tables | +20.291, from the NBS tables | none |

The steps sum to +31.351 and −42.393 kJ/mol, and nothing was tuned to that. The pKa was measured at 20.5 °C and is used at 25 °C with no correction. Step 3's barrier is a value, so it is drawn to scale and labelled as derived, where the list above foresaw only a cited barrier or a schematic one. No enthalpy of the carbonic acid molecule is cited, so the enthalpy curve shows steps 2 and 3 as one change. The timescale labels of steps 2 and 3 rest on the same paper; those of steps 1 and 4 have no source and are stored with that reason. The radii of the two ions are Shannon's for six neighbours, checked through a transcription and not in the paper. One water molecule is in the scene from the first chapter, because it has a part in step 3; no other water is drawn. The solid is not in the scene until step 1 begins: "Before mixing" shows the acid alone, as a close group, and the unit of the solid comes down into the picture from above it. The sodium ion parts from the hydrogen carbonate ion by an ångström and a half within step 1 and drifts on during step 2. The water made in step 2 moves off upward during step 3. In step 3 one O–H group of carbonic acid turns to face the relaying water in four moves of 45 degrees before the two protons move, in one move. In step 4 the carbon dioxide leaves up and to the right, where nothing else is, and is out of the picture before the step ends. Where the atoms are between and around the reacting species is this project's choreography and not a computed or measured path; the first chapter's text says so, and step 3's says it of how the hydrogen crosses.

The energy panel's note is the record's own and is longer than the sentence above: it also says what enthalpy, entropy and free energy are, and that a change runs by itself when G falls. It is followed by the sum of the four steps and by what standard conditions are.

### 9.3 Acceptance

- **Continuity:** at every step boundary, `state` just before and just after gives the same atom positions within a small tolerance. This is of every atom that is in the scene on both sides: an atom that is added at a boundary is where its keyframe has it from that boundary on, and nothing leaves the scene at one.
- **Bond bookkeeping:** each bond in a step's bonds-broken exists before the step and each bond in bonds-formed does not; each step's bond list at its end equals the list at its start, minus bonds broken, plus bonds formed. The set of atoms and the total charge are identical at every chapter boundary, and every species' connectivity there matches its library record. This is of the record, which places all fourteen actors at every keyframe; the scene does not draw the six of the solid before it is added, and what it leaves out it leaves out as whole species.
- **Energies:** the summed step `deltaH` and the summed step `deltaG` (steps 1–4) equal +31.4 and −42.4 kJ/mol within 0.5 kJ/mol.
- **Deep links:** each chapter's link opens at that chapter's start.

### 9.4 The page as built

The page is `#/learn/hcl-nahco3`, loaded lazily. The Learn page lists it with its status, and Home and the search link to it. This section says what was built and where it differs from 9.1 and 9.2; `docs/research/phase-5/build-record.md` gives the reasons.

- **The head** has the title, the mode in one line that opens, "Start again" beside it once a run is stored, the button of the energy panel, the periodic table in a new tab, and the notices of section 10. There are no tabs, no primer and no Start: the page opens on the timeline.
- **The scene** is drawn from `state(mechanism, t)` and the element table at every render, with nothing eased or kept between two positions. Atoms of a molecule are balls of the explorer's size and colours, bonds are sticks, and a bond whose order is between two whole numbers is its whole lines and one dashed line, thinner and more broken the less of it there is. The two ions are balls at the same scale, with the symbol and charge written beside them once each is a species of its own, in ink that reads in either theme; one helper line under the scene says that every ball is drawn much smaller than the atom or ion it stands for, so that the bonds can be seen. An electron pair on its way is a curved arrow with two dots on it: a flat mark on the picture, drawn over everything else, which starts at the edge of the atom a lone pair is on or at the middle of the bond the pair comes from, ends at the middle of the bond the pair becomes or at the edge of the atom it stays on, and bows round that atom's ball. All the arrows of a move are shown together, five in step 3. The two bookends are a drawn beaker, laid over the scene, whose powder and bubbles move with the timeline.
- **A molecule or an ion is named** in a callout when it is pointed at or tapped in the scene. A list beside the scene gives the same names, those that take part in the chapter first; focusing or tapping a name puts the callout on its molecule in the scene. Until the solid has come apart it is one thing with one name, "unit of solid sodium hydrogen carbonate, NaHCO₃", in the callout, the list and the scene's description, so that the page does not answer step 1's question; before it is added it is not listed at all.
- **The camera** frames what the keyframes give: at a chapter's start, the species the chapter is about, whole, in a sphere no wider than 4.5 Å (as first built, up to 11). Step 1 is the exception: the solid is out of the picture when it begins and comes into it. A learner can turn and zoom it with a pointer, and their view is kept as the timeline moves: the direction they look from, and how much closer or further than the chapter's own distance. What the camera looks at stays the chapter's, so the action is followed. A "Reset view" button, shown once the scene has been turned or zoomed, gives the chapter's own view back. 9.1 says the framing resumes on release; as first built it came back whenever the timeline moved, and the owner, using it, found that a zoom lost at every move of the slider could not be worked with (2026-10-08).
- **The timeline** is under the scene: Play or Pause, Previous and Next named by the chapter each goes to, a native range input over the whole track in steps of 0.005, and the seven chapters marked under it, each as wide as its share of the track. A share is too narrow for a name, so a step's mark shows its number, and the real timescale of the step the timeline is in is one line under the track; a screen reader is given every chapter's name and timescale in the list. Play takes one minute for the whole track, which is a pace for the eye and says nothing of the reaction. Previous in the first chapter goes to the start of the track and Next in the last goes to its end, which is where the timeline is completed. Under the reduced-motion setting there is no Play, and one line says why.
- **The chapter in words** is one chapter at a time: its heading, its short text, the longer text in a closed disclosure, and a link to the chapter that can be followed or copied.
- **The energy panel** floats over the page, as the labs' calculator does, and stays open while the timeline moves. It draws free energy as the heavier, whole curve and enthalpy as the thinner, broken one, each named on the chart, with a cursor where the timeline is, the four steps marked, and in words on the curve which barrier is not to scale and what is derived. Under the curves is what the step the timeline is in changes, with where each value is from, and the note of 9.2 on step 1. Closed until they are opened are a table of all four steps, the panel's notes, how each value was worked out, and the sources. A step's notes are in this panel and not with the chapter's words, since each is about an energy. While Challenge keeps any step's name back, the panel gives figures and no words about them: the notes, how each value was worked out and the sources each answer a question, and wait, with one line that says so, until every question is answered.
- **No figure off a drawn curve is printed.** The cursor marks a place. Every energy the page or the scene's description gives is a step's own whole change or a sum of whole steps. The description gives a step's own change, at the start of a chapter the total of the steps before it, and after the last step the total of all four; between steps 2 and 3 it gives no total of enthalpy.
- **Challenge** stops Play and Next at the start of a step whose question is not answered. The answers are buttons, and one press answers; the feedback and the chapter follow. A radio button was not used, since an arrow key chooses one. The slider is never held. Until a step's question is answered or the learner has gone into the step, its name is kept back wherever the page would show it, and while a question waits the page also keeps back the step's real time, which species the step is about, and that sentence of the scene's description. The first answer to a question is the one kept, until the learner starts again, and whether it was right is worked out on reading. Reaching the end is said once, in Challenge with how many predictions were right.
- **A deep link** opens at its chapter's start and saves nothing. The place is the run's own from the learner's first action, when the address drops the link, so that a reload comes back to where the learner was.
- **The scene in words** (`describeMechanismState`) is in a closed disclosure under the timeline and is the picture's description for assistive technology. At a bookend, where the page draws a beaker and no atoms, the words are the chapter, the picture of the beaker and, after the last step, the totals. It changes with the position and is not a live region: only the chapter is announced. In a browser with no WebGL the frame holds these words in the scene's place and the rest of the page is as it is; the beaker is still drawn.

## 10. Progress

Guest-only. Progress is stored in `localStorage` as JSON. Each lab, the mechanism, and preferences have their own entry with its own version, so a change to one resets only that one.

- **Per lab:** seed, mode, status, recorded readings and observations, answers, and the current trial's engine state as plain data. Titration progress is saved after every learner action and every stream addition, so a reload resumes the current trial at the delivered volume, with the stopcock closed. The titration entry holds the seed and what the learner did and typed; what the seed fixes and what is judged from it (pass or fail, percent error, causes) are worked out on reading, so a stored record cannot disagree with its seed, and a change to the model's values raises the entry's version. Nothing is saved until the learner starts a lab, so opening its page leaves the home page saying not started. Aqueous progress is saved with each action the engine takes. Its entry, `lab:aqueous`, holds the mode, the status and, for each of the ten stations, whether the tube is mixed, which tools have been used, the observation last checked and the equation last checked in each form; there is no seed, since the stations are fixed. Whether an observation or an equation is right, and whether a station is done, is worked out on reading and stored nowhere, so a change to what a station expects raises the entry's version. What is chosen or built and not yet checked is not stored, and neither is the station shown. The flame test lab's progress (section 15) is saved in the same way, with each action the engine takes. Its entry, `lab:flame`, holds the mode, the status, what the loop carries now, the closing question's answer as last given and, for each of the six stations, what the loop carried when it was last held in the flame there, which of the flame and the strip's lines and bands have been looked at since, and the observation last recorded with what the loop carried when it was made; there is no seed. Whether an observation counts, whether it is right and whether a station is done are worked out on reading, so a change to a flame's colour, to a line or a band or its rank, to the answer ranges or to the closing question raises the entry's version. What is chosen and not yet checked, the entry of the list that is shown, whether the spectroscope is being looked through and a warning that is showing are not stored. The calorimetry lab's progress (section 16) is saved in the same way, with each action the engine takes. Its entry, `lab:calorimetry`, holds the seed, the mode, the status and, for every run of each of the three parts in order, the step of the logger at which the lid was put on and at which the stirrer was started, whether the logger has been started, the step after whose reading the change was started, how many steps the logger has taken, which readings have been looked at, the worksheet's entries as they were made, and whether the worksheet has been checked. The masses and temperatures of a run, which metal the piece is, every reading of the logger, whether an entry follows, whether a run counts and why not, and whether a part is done are worked out on reading from the seed and from what was done, and stored nowhere, so a change to the draw, to the cup's model, to a value a result is held to or to the stored shape raises the entry's version. (One such change was made with the version left at 1: on 2026-10-10 a typed temperature came to be held to the logger's exactly, where a tenth of a degree either side had been taken. The lab had not been released, so there was no stored run to judge differently.) What is typed and not yet entered, the part that is shown, the reading last looked at and a warning that is showing are not stored. The Group I cations lab's progress (section 17) is saved in the same way, with each action the engine takes. Its entry, `lab:cations`, holds the seed, the mode, the status and, for every run of each of the two parts in order, the list of what was done in it: the reagent added, the bath, the centrifuge, the litmus paper, a look at a part of the tube, what was recorded as seen, a tile put in a place of the flow chart or taken out of it, the check, the pouring off, and the ions named. Which ions a sample holds, what each tube holds and shows, which step a run has reached, whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, whether a place holds what belongs in it, whether a run counts and why not, and whether a part is done are worked out on reading from the seed and that list, and stored nowhere, so a change to the draw of the unknowns, to a rule of the tubes, to the bench's amounts, to a look or a choice, to the flow chart or to the stored shape raises the entry's version. A lab that is complete still takes actions, so that part 2 can be run again with a new unknown; its status stays completed. (One such change was made with the version left at 1: on 2026-10-10 a typed temperature came to be held to the logger's exactly, where a tenth of a degree either side had been taken. The lab had not been released, so there was no stored run to judge differently.) What is chosen and not yet recorded, the part and the entry of its list that are shown, which tile is held, and a warning that is showing are not stored. The Le Chatelier's principle lab's progress (section 18) is saved in the same way, with each action the engine takes. Its entry, `lab:le-chatelier`, holds the mode, the status and, for every run of each of the four systems in order, the list of what was done in it: the prediction, each press of the change, a look at a part of the tube, what was recorded as seen, the answer, and the check. There is no seed, since nothing in this lab is drawn by chance. Which disturbance a run has reached, what each tube shows, whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, whether an answer is right, whether a run counts and why not, and whether a system is done are worked out on reading from that list, and stored nowhere, so a change to a disturbance's portions, to what a tube shows at a stage, to a right answer, to the choices, to the order of the disturbances or to the stored shape raises the entry's version. A lab that is complete still takes actions, so that a system can be worked again; its status stays completed. What is chosen and not yet recorded, the system and the entry of its list that are shown, and what a message line last said are not stored. The clock reaction lab's progress (section 19) is saved with each action the engine takes, but for the pour, which the page keeps to itself while it plays the watch and saves together with the stop: a run that is left with its watch running has nothing stored of it. Its entry, `lab:clock-reaction`, holds the seed, the mode, the status and, for every attempt at each of the eight runs in order, whether the starch is in, where the flasks stand, whether the thermometer has been read, whether the flasks are poured, and how the watch was stopped: by hand, with what the watch read and how long after the change the hand was, in true milliseconds, or at the change; and for every working of each of the two notebooks, its entries, the places on the line that have been looked at, and whether it has been checked. The room's temperature and the baths', the time of every run, whether a run counts, whether an entry follows and whether a part is done are worked out on reading, so a change to a value the model times or judges with, or to the stored shape, raises the entry's version. What is typed and not yet entered, the part and the run shown, a watch that is running, whether the watch is to be stopped at the change, and what a message line last said are not stored.
- **Mechanism:** mode, status, last position, and the first answer given to each prediction, saved with each action the engine takes. Playing is shown frame by frame and written where a frame enters a chapter and where playing stops, however it stops, the page being left or closed among them (as first built it was written at every frame; changed at the deep review, 2026-10-08). A page closed in a way the browser gives no notice of comes back at the start of the chapter it was playing in. Whether an answer was right is worked out on reading, so a change to the track or to a question's answers raises the entry's version. Nothing is saved by opening the page or by following a deep link.
- **Preferences:** theme and reduced motion (each System, On, or Off; System is the default and follows the operating system), valence display level.

Stored records are plain data with no browser-specific types, so the same shapes can later be written to a database unchanged. If an entry fails to parse or has an unknown version, that entry starts fresh and the old value is kept under a backup key instead of being deleted; a later broken value gets its own numbered backup key and never replaces an earlier one. Where the store is too full to take the backup, the old value stays under the entry's own key and nothing is saved over it: that entry's progress lasts for the session only, with the notice below showing, until a save finds room to make the backup first. The home page shows an entry that is not JSON, or is of another version, as not started. It reads an entry's version and status and nothing else, because holding a record to a lab's own shape would mean loading the lab, which the home page does not do (section 4). So an entry of the right version that the lab's page will refuse is shown as the status it claims, in progress or completed, until that page has been opened; the page sets it aside, and the home page says not started from then on. That is accepted (Gate A, 2026-10-05). A lab page that starts fresh because its entry was set aside says so, in a status paragraph, on that visit, until the page is reloaded or a new run is started: that a saved run could not be opened in this version, that it was kept, and that the export includes it (added at the deep review, 2026-10-05; before, the learner saw a lab that had never been started and no word of the run). A lab page open in two tabs of one browser takes the record the other tab saves at the moment it is saved, with its stopcock closed, so that neither tab saves an old record over a newer one; a value the page's version cannot read is left where it is, and an entry removed in the other tab leaves this one not started (added at the deep review). Two actions made in two tabs within the moment the browser takes to tell the other are still last save wins. If the browser refuses a save part-way through a session, the settings page and each lab page say that progress will last only for that session: until the page is reloaded or the tab is closed, as the notice has said since 2026-10-17 (before, it named only the closing of the tab, and a reload empties the session's store as well). The home page shows the same notice, and says that progress is saved in this browser only while it is (added 2026-10-17). The notice stays while any entry's latest save was refused; a later successful save of a different entry does not clear it, and "Clear my progress" does (built with the titration lab, the first activity that saves more than preferences).

A settings page offers "export my progress" (downloads the JSON) and "clear my progress".

## 11. UI and accessibility

As in the original doc: calm, card-based layout; the 3D viewport or lab bench in the centre; in the explorer the atom and bond list sits to its right and the inspector runs across the page below it, its cards side by side where there is room, with the text description and the element details open by default; a lab page in three tabs, primer, lab and record (section 7.1), with the working part of the lab notebook beside the bench where the page is wide enough for both and below it otherwise, and the record of trials on a tab of its own (the aqueous lab has no docked notebook: its Lab tab is the ten stations, one at a time, and its Record tab the table of what was recorded at each; section 8.5; the flame test lab's is the same, with six stations and a closing question; section 15.6; the calorimetry lab's Lab tab is its three parts, one at a time, each with its worksheet in the part's own column, and its Record tab every run of each part; section 16.7; the Group I cations lab's Lab tab is its two parts and, of the part shown, the five steps and the entry that closes a run, one at a time, and its Record tab every run of each part; section 17.8; the Le Chatelier's principle lab's Lab tab is its four systems and, of the system shown, its disturbances and the entry that closes a run, one at a time, and its Record tab every run of each system; section 18.8; the clock reaction lab's Lab tab is its three parts and, of the part shown, a run or a notebook, one at a time, and its Record tab every attempt at each run and every working of each notebook; section 19.8); the mechanism's timeline under its scene, with the chapter's words beside the scene where the page is wide enough and below otherwise (section 9.4); light and dark themes; Inter with tabular numerals; design tokens as CSS variables in the Tailwind theme; a Ctrl+K command palette over molecules, labs, and the mechanism.

The theme and the reduced-motion setting in effect (the stored preference, or the system's) are applied before first paint by a short inline script in `index.html`, so that the dark theme never flashes light. The build writes that script from the app's own definition of the preferences entry (its key, version and allowed values), and a test holds its decision to the app's for every kind of stored value; missing, corrupt or unreadable storage follows the system, and storage that can be read but refuses writes keeps the stored preferences, in the script and in the app alike. `color-scheme` is set for both themes, so the browser's own controls match.

Accessibility is designed toward WCAG 2.1 AA:

- Full keyboard operation. Explore works through the DOM atom and bond list (section 6.3). Lab tools are DOM controls, and none depends on timing or on holding a key down. The timeline is a native range input.
- Colour is never the only signal: every colour observation also has a text label, and the titration plume has a status line, which also names the flask's colour. In the aqueous lab each part of the drawn tube says what is seen there in words when it is looked at, the list beside it says in words which parts have been looked at, a station's progress, a correct entry and one that is not are each words beside a mark, and the solubility table's cells are words. In the flame test lab the colour of the drawn flame and of each line and band on the strip is said in words when it is looked at, a line or a band not yet looked at has a dashed edge and is listed in words as not looked at, and an observation that does not count says so in a sentence. In the calorimetry lab the cup's state is said in words beside its drawing, a reading of the graph says its time and its temperature in words when it is looked at, a point that has been looked at is filled and one that has not is open, with a key under the graph that says which is which, a step of the cup that has been taken carries a tick, and whether an entry follows and whether a run counts are words beside a mark. In the Group I cations lab each part of the drawn tube says what is seen there in words when it is looked at; a liquid and a solid are each under a bracket with the word for which it is, a solid that has settled is drawn with an edge and a grain, and one that has not as specks with an edge under a bracket of its own, so that a coloured liquid is not told from a solid by its colour; a part not yet looked at has a dashed edge and is listed in words as not looked at; the place of the flow chart that is chosen has a whole edge and one that is not a dashed one, and a tile that is held is marked as pressed; and whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, whether a place holds what belongs in it and whether a run counts are words beside a mark. In the Le Chatelier's principle lab, which turns on the colours of its liquids, each part of the drawn tube says what is seen there in words when it is looked at, so each of the six colours a liquid can have is told by its word, and the one the record does not name by the words the record has for it; a liquid and a solid are each under a bracket with the word for which it is, and a solid is drawn with an edge and a grain, so that whether a white solid is there is not told by a colour; the bath a tube stands in is told by what is drawn in it, bubbles and steam or blocks of ice, and by a word beside it, and the line beside the picture says where the tube stands; a part not yet looked at has a dashed edge and is listed in words as not looked at; the tube the learner is on, of the four that stand together in system 4, is marked as current, in bold, behind a pointer and under a whole edge; and how a system and a disturbance stand, whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, whether an answer is right and whether a run counts are words beside a mark. In the clock reaction lab, whose one event is a liquid that turns dark blue, the look of the liquid is drawn in a colour and written in the record's words under the flask, said in a sentence beside it, and, at the moment it changes, said once to a status line; the three baths are told apart by what is drawn in them (blocks of ice, steam, or neither) and by the name under each; a step of the flasks that has been taken carries a tick; the watch is figures; a place on the line that has been looked at is filled and one that has not is open, a run's point is a diamond, and the key under the graph says which is which; and whether a run counts, whether an entry follows and whether a notebook counts are words beside a mark. The titration lab's colour roles (section 7.1) are an added cue on text or a mark that already says the same; their values were chosen for a text contrast of at least 4.5 to 1 against the background each sits on in both themes, and `docs/for-institutions.md` gives what was measured.
- Text descriptions of 3D scenes and station results come from pure functions (`describeMolecule`, `describeStationResult`, `describeMechanismState`) tested in Vitest. The Group I cations lab's description of a tube in words (17.7) is `tubeText`, among that lab's messages, and is tested in the same way for every tube a run can show. The Le Chatelier's principle lab's (18.7) is its own `tubeText`, tested for each of the thirty-eight tubes the lab shows. The clock reaction lab's descriptions in words of the flasks and of the graph (19.7) are `flasksText` and `describeGraph`, among that lab's messages, and are tested in the same way.
- The meniscus text alternative gives the two bracketing graduations and which fifth of the gap the meniscus is in, not the reading itself. Each position spans 0.02 mL, so the text alone is enough to pass the ±0.02 mL check. It is available to every learner.
- Reduced motion follows the operating system unless the learner overrides it.

Target screens: 1366×768 first, scaling up. Tablets fully supported. On viewports narrower than 768 px, Explore and Learn work; a lab page keeps its three tabs, with the primer on the first, the notebook of any saved run (read-only) on the Record tab, and on the Lab tab, in place of the bench, a notice that the bench needs a window at least 768 px wide and that progress stays in the browser it was made in; the bench is not shown, and the page has no mode choice, Start or Restart. A page zoomed to 200% on a 1366 px screen is narrower than that, so the bench is not shown there either, and the notice says that zooming out brings it back. As built, the read-only notebook of the narrow page is the record of trials with the recorded HCl molarity over it: each trial's readings, titre, molarity, result and feedback. The line that says a lab is complete, with the true molarity, is in the working part of the notebook, which the narrow page does not show. The narrow page also says so when the browser is not keeping progress (section 10). The aqueous lab's narrow page is the same: the primer, the notice in place of the stations, and on the Record tab the table of a saved run, which there does include the line that says the lab is complete. The flame test lab's narrow page is as the aqueous lab's, and so are the calorimetry lab's, the Group I cations lab's, the Le Chatelier's principle lab's and the clock reaction lab's.

An About page carries the PubChem/NLM acknowledgment and NCBI disclaimer, the NIST and other citations, the MIT licence, and the statement of independence from any school.

## 12. Documentation for institutional reviewers

Valence is made for any learner, not for a particular institution. The repository is expected to become public, and a school or college may still want to review it before pointing students to it. The documentation therefore includes one clearly labelled section for each department that might do so, written as plain statements so the decision is easy. The sections are institution-agnostic: none names or targets a specific school.

- **IT and security:** static site, no server, no login, no install; what the browser stores; how to self-host; browser requirements. On a shared computer, saved progress is visible to the next person using the same browser profile; the docs say so and point to "clear my progress".
- **Legal and privacy:** no accounts, no personal data collected, no analytics, nothing transmitted to any server, so the app creates and holds no education records. FERPA, COPPA, and GDPR are addressed in those factual terms. Licence and third-party data attributions.
- **Accessibility services:** what has been tested against WCAG 2.1 AA and what has not.
- **Finance and procurement:** free, MIT-licensed, no contract, no vendor, no support agreement.
- **Faculty and curriculum:** which labs exist, what each teaches, how answers are checked, where every chemistry value is sourced.
- **Lab safety:** Valence supplements bench labs and does not replace safety training; each bottle's hazard information says what it classifies. For the titration lab's bottles that is the concentrated or the pure substance, from one supplier's safety data sheets: hydrochloric acid at the strength the sheet names, and sodium hydroxide and phenolphthalein as solids. A note with its own source, or two, says what a safety data sheet gives for the solution at the strength used, and never makes the solution sound safer than its sheet does.

Rules: state only what is true and tested when written. The docs never say "FERPA compliant" (that determination belongs to the institution) and make no WCAG conformance claim before an audit. They keep the statement that Valence is independent, with no vendor relationship. Each section is written as its features land.

## 13. Testing

- Vitest unit tests for every engine model, the derive functions, the describe functions, and the validator, written before the corresponding UI.
- A phase is done when its automated acceptance list passes and every requirement in its section has been checked by hand in the running app, using a checklist written into that phase's plan. The checklist includes a keyboard-only walkthrough of the phase's features.
- No end-to-end framework in v1. A script used for a browser check is throwaway and stays out of the repository.
- CI runs the linter, the tests, the type check, the production build and the data scripts on every pull request and on every push to the main branch, and fails when a generated file differs from what the scripts write.
- The titration phase's plan has two gates besides its tasks. Before any screen is built, a reviewer who has not seen the designs checks the stored constants against their sources, re-derives the model's results, and reads every sentence a learner will see, and the owner reads the primer and the hazard notes. When the page is whole, the hand checklist is run in the browser, and the owner reads the meniscus and judges the stream, the pink and the layout. As run (the owner's instruction of 2026-10-05, when the engine, the data and the wording were built and the first gate had been held): the screens were built straight through, with one deep review of the whole branch at the end in place of a review after each task. The second gate was not held as a stop. Scripts drove the whole page by keyboard for what a person cannot see by looking (that every control can be reached and used, focus, names and states, that words and picture agree, reduced motion, that nothing is lost on a reload), and nobody ran the hand checklist by hand. The owner reads the meniscus and judges the stream, the pink and the layout in one look at the close of the phase. The first gate's findings were all fixed before any screen was built, but the gate was not closed first: the owner's reading and two of the owner's questions were still open, and go to that look and to the review. The plan records what was run and what was not, and `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was tested and by whom. This is how the titration phase was run; it does not change the rule above for the phases that follow, which is the owner's to restate.

## 14. Build phases

1. **Foundation.** App shell, navigation, tokens, themes, hash routing, progress storage, settings and About pages, chem-data types and validator, seeded RNG, lint rules.
   - Done when: the validator rejects each of its four failure conditions; the lint rules reject a React import and a `Math.random` call in `engine/`; progress survives a reload.
2. **Explorer.** Section 6, plus the command palette over molecules (later phases add their entries). Built as two plans: 2A, the data and derived values (elements, molecule files, derive and describe functions, MO data); then 2B, the 3D viewer, inspectors, measure tool, valence levels, and palette. Done when 6.5 passes.
3. **Titration lab.** Section 7, including the shared lab shell (the page frame with the mode switch and three tabs: the primer, the lab with slots for a guide, a bench and a notebook, and the record) reused by phase 4, and the narrow-viewport lab page. Each lab owns its notebook and its check: phase 4 checks observations and equations, this lab one number, and nothing is shared ahead of need. The endpoint acceptance test is the first task. Done when 7.4 passes.
4. **Aqueous reactions lab.** Section 8. Done when 8.4 passes. As built: the data, the engine with 8.4 as its tests, the primer and the chemistry wording first, then the screens straight through (section 8.5), with what the two labs' pages share made shared as it was needed: the floating panel, the paged list, the button that stays focusable when it cannot be used, and a shell that takes a lab with no docked notebook and a lab's own words for its modes. 8.4 passes as automated tests. The hand checklist was run by script and not by a person, and the one deep review of the phase comes after the screens.
5. **Mechanism timeline.** Section 9. Done when 9.3 passes. As built: the sources, the record, the pure model with 9.3 as its tests, the wording and the stored progress first, then the page straight through (section 9.4), reusing the explorer's 3D shapes and the labs' mode switch, floating panel, notices and message line. 9.3 passes as automated tests. The hand checklist was run by script in one browser and not by a person, and the one deep review of the phase comes after the page.

Each phase gets its own implementation plan. The documentation sections in section 12 are written alongside the phase that makes them true.

## 15. Flame test lab (added after v1)

Asked for by the owner on 2026-10-08, when the periodic table's emission spectra were extended to every element and it became plain that a flame's colour is not simply the atom's strongest lines. It is the first lab beyond the two of v1, and section 3's "the other 16 labs" is one fewer. It uses the lab shell of sections 7 and 8 (Primer, Lab, Record; Guided and Challenge) and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

### 15.1 Set-up

Six solutions, each 0.1 M and each a chloride: barium chloride, calcium chloride, copper(II) chloride, potassium chloride, sodium chloride and strontium chloride. One metal loop, a spot plate with a well of each solution, a burner's flame, distilled water, and a hand spectroscope with a wavelength scale from 400 to 780 nm. The learner observes the six; there is no unknown to identify, and no cobalt glass (the primer says in a sentence what it is for). Nothing is seeded: the lab is the same every time.

### 15.2 At a station

One salt at a time, in the shell's list with Previous and Next. A station is a short column of cards:

1. **The bottle**: the solution, what it looks like, its hazards.
2. **The loop**: "Clean the loop" (rinsed in distilled water and held in the flame until the flame shows no colour), then "Dip the loop" in the station's well. The loop's state is said in words.
3. **The flame**: "Hold the loop in the flame". A drawn flame takes the colour. The learner looks at it as they look at the tube of section 8: pointing at it, focusing it or tapping it says its colour in a callout, and that look opens the first question.
4. **The spectroscope**: "Look through the spectroscope" shows a strip against its scale with what the flame gives: sharp lines and broad bands, each where its source puts it. Each is looked at in the same way, and its callout says where on the scale it is, its colour, and whether it is a line or a band. Looking opens the other questions.
5. **What you saw**, in choice boxes: the colour of the flame; where on the scale the brightest thing is (ranges of wavelength to choose from); and whether that brightest thing is a sharp line or a broad band.

Guided has the hints, and what a correct observation holds, each in a closed disclosure (the hints stood open at first; the same five at every station were tiring to read, and they were closed at the review of 2026-10-09). Challenge shows neither until the learner has recorded an observation of their own that counts: one made with a clean loop (as first built, any recorded observation brought them, so a careless dip and a guess bought the answers; changed on 2026-10-08, before the screens). Feedback says which answers are not right and never the right one.

**The technique that matters is a clean loop.** A loop carries what it was last dipped in until it is cleaned, and a loop that has only been handled carries a trace of sodium. In the flame every salt on the loop shows: the spectroscope has the lines and bands of each, and sodium's yellow hides the other colours of the flame. An observation made with a loop that was not clean does not count, whatever was recorded, and its feedback names the cause; the station is done again with a clean loop. In Guided a warning is raised before a loop that is not clean is dipped, with a way to go on anyway, as the titration lab's warnings are. The size of the handling trace is a model parameter, made large enough to show, and the primer says so.

When all six are done, one closing question in both modes: all six bottles are chlorides, so what gives each flame its colour? The lab is complete when the six observations are correct, each made with a clean loop, and the closing question is answered correctly.

### 15.3 Data and model

For each salt, stored with sources that were read: the colour of its flame (a name, and a colour to draw it in); and what is seen in the spectroscope, as a list of features, each a sharp line (one wavelength) or a broad band (a range), with its colour, how prominent it is among the salt's features, and what gives it: the metal's atom, or a molecule formed in the flame, named. Atomic lines are NIST's, from tables saved as the emission spectra's are. Where a band's limits cannot be read from a source, the feature says where the source puts it and that it is approximate. Hazards follow 5.2: the supplier's sheets by default, the most restrictive official document where one is stricter, cited; disposal is left to the laboratory's own rules and local regulations.

The engine is pure and holds no chemistry of its own: the loop's contents, what the flame and the spectroscope show for them, the check of an observation, and the lab's stored progress (mode, per station what was looked at and recorded and whether the loop was clean, the closing answer, status).

Each station's notes, shown once it is done, say what gives the light there, and for calcium, strontium, barium and copper that the flame's colour comes mainly from molecules, whose light that element's card of the periodic table (linked) does not show. As first written the notes, the primer and the closing question's feedback said that the flame is not the colour of the lines on the card. The cards do not bear that out: six of the nine lines on calcium's card are orange or red and its flame is orange-red, and three of the six on strontium's are red and its flame is red. Since the review of 2026-10-09 each says what is so: the card shows lines of the atom alone, the atom's line of the strip among them where the strip has one, and what colours the flame is mostly the molecule's light, which the card does not show.

As built, the record and the engine differ from 15.2 and from the paragraphs above in these ways, each for the reason given; `docs/research/flame/README.md` says how every value was read and what could not be sourced.

- **A pair of lines is one feature.** Two lines of one salt closer than 5 nm are stored and shown as one sharp line, the stronger with its partner beside it: sodium's two yellow lines and both of potassium's pairs. A hand spectroscope does not separate them, and the 5 nm is a parameter of the model. The callout says that it is two lines too close together to tell apart.
- **When the questions open.** A look at the flame opens the first question. The other two open only when every line and band on the strip has been looked at, since which is the brightest cannot be said before all have been seen.
- **What a callout says.** Where a line or a band is, its colour, which of the two it is, and also how bright it is beside the others on the strip, so that the brightest can be told without seeing the picture. It never says what gives it: that is the station's notes', once the station is done.
- **Brightness is the instruments', not the eye's.** A feature's rank follows what its sources' instruments recorded, or the relative intensity NIST lists, and never a table of measured intensities, of which none was found. Potassium's deep red pair is ranked first on that ground, at the edge of what an eye sees. The primer says so.
- **Where on the scale.** The second question is answered from six ranges that cover the scale with no gap, each boundary at least 5 nm from the brightest feature of every salt.
- **A band without limits.** A band whose sources give only where it is brightest is stored at those positions, marked approximate, and drawn 5 nm wider on each side; its callout says that its limits are not given.
- **What the strip leaves out.** Bands a source names and gives no place for (those of SrCl, of copper oxide), or that are spread over most of the scale or lie inside another (BaO, BaCl), are not on the strip. Each is said in a sentence at its station, shown with the notes.
- **A loop that carries several salts.** With sodium among them the flame is sodium's yellow, from a source. Without sodium the flame is called by the colour of each salt, in the order they came onto the loop, and is drawn in each side by side: the model's own rule. On the strip every salt's lines and bands are shown as that salt alone gives them, and the model does not rank one salt's light against another's: a callout then says "one of the brightest". Each line and band is counted along the strip by its middle, so that a line that stands inside another salt's band is counted where its button lies (at first each was counted by where it begins, and on such a loop the count, the Tab key and the buttons disagreed). The primer says that what such a flame is drawn and called as is this lab's rule.
- **The trace on a handled loop** shows as fully as a dip in the sodium chloride solution.
- **The closing question** has five choices: the chloride, the gas, the loop, the metal, the water. A wrong one is told why it cannot be the answer and can be changed; attempts are not limited, there or at a station.
- **One more saved table.** The emission spectra's tables stop at 700 nm, so one more response of the NIST database, for potassium from 700 to 780 nm, is kept with this lab's sources.
- **Hazards.** The rule of 5.2 as the owner refined it on 2026-10-08: the stricter document wins where what it says is about the substance. Each bottle's note is three short paragraphs and can rest on more than two documents.
- **What is stored.** The loop's contents now; for each station what the loop carried when it was last held there, the looks made since, and the observation last recorded with what the loop carried when it was made. Whether that loop was clean is worked out on reading.

### 15.4 Primer

Short topics in the shell's paged primer, those to read first marked: what a flame test is; light from atoms (an electron falling between levels gives light of one wavelength: a line); molecules in a flame (broad bands, and the four metals whose flame colour is mostly theirs); the loop (cleaning it, what an unclean one does, sodium everywhere, and cobalt glass in a sentence); the spectroscope and how to read its scale; safety (the burner, the salts, and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules).

### 15.5 Acceptance

- For each salt, a clean loop gives the stored flame colour and the stored features and nothing else.
- A loop that was not cleaned gives the features of everything on it; with sodium on it the flame is sodium's yellow; an observation made so does not count and its cause is named; Guided warns before the dip.
- Every stored feature has a source; every atomic line's wavelength is the saved table's digits.
- No state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on; the stored record round-trips; Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the learner records an observation that counts.
- The descriptions in words of the flame and of the spectroscope's view are tested for every salt.

### 15.6 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-08 and 2026-10-09, straight through, as the aqueous lab's screens were (section 13), on the shell and the station pieces of sections 7 and 8. The route is `#/lab/flame`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it with its status.

- **Lab tab: one entry at a time.** A list of seven: the six stations, each with how far it has come in words beside a mark (not started; started, once the loop has been held in the flame there; recorded with a loop that was not clean; recorded, not correct yet; done), and the closing question, which says that it opens when the six are done. Previous and Next are named for where they go. The list is the shared paged list. It is beside the station only from 58rem of bench, later than the primer's topics, so that the station beside it still has the strip's full width; below that it is behind its button.
- **A station is a short column of cards,** each there when the engine has a use for it. The bottle: the solution's tile, with its name, what it looks like in words, its bottle drawn with its label and, for the one coloured solution, its liquid in that colour, and its hazards, whose note is drawn as three short paragraphs. The loop: a drawn loop, its state in words in a status line, "Clean the loop" and "Dip the loop", and a sentence that says what the one step of cleaning is. The flame, once the loop carries the station's solution: "Hold the loop in the flame", then the drawn flame. The spectroscope and the three questions, once the loop has been held. The notes, what the strip leaves out, a link to the card of the station's metal on the periodic table, in a new tab, and the station's Sources, once the station is done and not before: each of them says, or names a paper that says, what gives the light.
- **Guided's warning** is drawn in the loop's card when a loop that is not clean is dipped. It takes focus, as the titration lab's does, and is the same component. "Go back and clean it" puts focus on Clean; "Dip it anyway" dips and puts focus on Dip. Any other press on the loop's or the flame's card drops it, and so does a change to Challenge. Once dropped it is raised again only by Dip: as first built a station kept that its warning had been raised and drew it whenever a dip there would warn, so a warning left unanswered came back by itself after a change of mode and back, or when the loop, cleaned at another station, carried another salt again (put right at the review of 2026-10-09).
- **The flame is explored.** It is drawn about 200 px tall on a dark card in both themes, in the colour the record stores for what is on the loop, with the burner's own pale cone inside it and its edge drawn, so that a flame of no colour would still be a flame; a loop that carries two salts and no sodium gives a flame drawn in both colours side by side. One button lies over it.
