A mouse pointer over it, keyboard focus on it or a tap looks at it: a callout beside it says its colour in words, the same words go to a status line for a screen reader, and the first question opens. The flame flickers five times, for under five seconds, where the reduced-motion setting allows, and is still under it.
- **The strip is explored.** "Look through the spectroscope" shows, on a dark card, a black strip over a scale from 400 to 780 nm with a mark every 20 nm and a number at every 100 nm and at 780. A broad band is a stretch that fades at its two ends, drawn short of the strip's top and foot. A sharp line is a thin stroke at its wavelength, the strip's whole height and so taller than any band, with a dark gap either side of it and its edges drawn on the screen's own pixels; every line is drawn over every band. Each is in the colour of its wavelength, the brightest of a salt's drawn whole and the others fainter by rank: 1, 0.75 and 0.55 of full strength. (As first built a line was a stroke with a glow about it, as tall as a band, and barium's line, 3.5 nm from the end of its band, read as the band's bright edge. Changed at the review of 2026-10-09, and the primer's card on the spectroscope says how the two are drawn, as a rule of the simulation.) A button lies over each, as tall as the strip and at least 44 px wide where the picture is at its narrowest; where two would overlap, the space between them is split, and a line beside a band has its button off-centre. A look says, in a callout over the strip that stays inside the card, where the line or band is, its colour, which of the two it is and how bright it is beside the others. The gap between the strip and the callout is part of the button, so a pointer moved up onto the callout does not lose it. Until it has been looked at, a line or a band is named, in its button and in the list under the strip, by its place in the count from the violet end and by nothing else; the page never carries the record's own name for it, which says what gives it.
- **The strip's width.** The spec asked for a strip that scrolls sideways in its card below about 560 px. As built the picture is drawn down to 440 px before it scrolls. The narrowest window that shows a bench, 768 px with the menu open, leaves it 447 px, and an upright tablet of 820 px leaves it 499: at 560 both would have had to be scrolled to find potassium's deep red line, at the far end. At 440 px the marks of the scale are about 21 px apart and every button is still 44 px wide.
- **A colour the screen can show.** The shared function that turns a wavelength into a colour fades toward 780 nm, as the eye does, and would draw potassium's pair near 766 nm almost black. On the strip a wavelength beyond 740 nm is drawn in the colour of 740 nm, so that the line the lab calls the brightest can be found. The primer says that a real eye sees that light faintly.
- **Nothing on the strip is too dim to find.** Whatever is drawn on the strip has a contrast of 3 to 1 or more with the strip's black, as a ratio of luminances. Where the colour of a wavelength at its rank's strength falls short of that, the colour is moved toward white by the fewest of 64 steps that reach it, and the strength stays the rank's. That is so for calcium's violet line and strontium's blue one, ranked third (1.5 and 2.2 to 1 as first built), for potassium's violet pair (1.9), for its deep red pair (3.0 where it was 2.96) and for the violet end of copper's band (2.5 at its middle). They are paler than the colour of their wavelengths, and still violet, blue and red. Added at the review of 2026-10-09 and measured from screenshots at four window sizes in both themes: every line 3.0 to 1 or more over two columns of pixels or more.
- **The questions** are three groups of choice boxes, each disabled, with what to look at first under it, until its looks are made, and one button, "Check observations". They are the aqueous lab's observation form, made a shared component. An observation made with a loop that was not clean is told that it does not count and why, as a reason and with no tick or cross, and a "Clean the loop" button under it cleans the loop and puts focus on Dip. A new hold of the loop is a new thing to look at: the spectroscope is closed again and the form is empty, and an observation made at an earlier hold stays the station's, in the list and in the record, until another is recorded. Where the loop has been cleaned or dipped again since it was held, the flame's card says that its pictures are of the loop as it was held.
- **Hints.** In Guided the five hints are over the questions, in the card they help with, in a closed disclosure named "Hints", and what a correct observation holds is under the questions in another. In Challenge neither is in the page until an observation that counts has been recorded, and a sentence says when they come; they are closed there too. (The hints stood open until the review of 2026-10-09.)
- **The closing question** is the list's last entry: one group of five choice boxes and "Check answer", there and disabled, with the reason under it, until the six stations are done.
- **Record tab:** a table of the six stations, with the three answers as last recorded and whether the observation counted and is correct; the closing question with the answer last given and whether it is right; and, once the lab is complete, the line that says so, which the Lab tab says too.
- **The descriptions in words** of the flame and of the whole strip (15.5) are tested for every salt. The page shows the first, as the flame's callout. It does not show the second, which names the brightest thing outright: it shows what one line or band is when that one is looked at, as section 8.5 does with the tube.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is chosen and not yet checked, the entry shown, whether the spectroscope is being looked through, a warning that is showing, and what the message lines last said. A reload opens on the first station begun and not done, and on the closing question once the six are done.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the hand checklist of section 13 was run by script, by keyboard and by emulated touch, in headless Chromium, and by nobody at a keyboard. The review of 2026-10-09 was held the same way, by an AI coding assistant that had not built the screens. No chemist has read the record, the notes or the primer.

## 16. Calorimetry lab (added after v1)

Suggested by the owner on 2026-10-09. It is the second lab beyond the two of v1. It is built without the owner's department notes for it: its three parts are the usual ones of a first-year coffee-cup calorimetry lab, and each is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell (Primer, Lab, Record; Guided and Challenge), the notebook and typed entries of section 7, the pictures that are explored of section 8, and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

### 16.1 Set-up

A coffee-cup calorimeter: two foam cups, one inside the other, a lid with a hole, a stirrer, and a temperature probe whose readings a logger plots against time, one reading every 15 seconds. Three parts, each with an idea of its own:

1. **Neutralisation.** 50.0 mL of 1.00 M hydrochloric acid and 50.0 mL of 1.00 M sodium hydroxide solution. The heat given out for each mole of water formed.
2. **Dissolving.** A weighed portion of solid ammonium nitrate, about 4 g, in 50.0 mL of water. The heat taken in for each mole dissolved.
3. **A metal's specific heat.** A weighed piece, about 50 g, of an unknown grey metal (aluminium, iron, zinc or tin, by the seed), heated in boiling water whose temperature is read, then put into 50.0 mL of water in the cup. The metal's specific heat, and from it which metal it is.

A run's starting temperatures, masses and the bath's temperature are seeded, so that no two runs share their numbers; the same seed and the same actions give the same run. The seed is drawn in the app.

### 16.2 A run

A part is a short column of cards:

1. **The bench**: what is used, with its hazards, and the balance's reading where something is weighed.
2. **The cup**: drawn, with its state in words. "Put the lid on", "Stir", "Start the logger", then the one action that starts the change (pour in the second solution; add the solid; add the hot metal), and "Run on" to let time pass, 30 seconds at a press. No clock runs: time is a count the engine is handed. In Guided a warning is raised before the change is started with the lid off or the stirrer still, with a way to go on anyway.
3. **The graph**: temperature against time as the logger draws it, a point for each reading. The graph is the picture that is explored: pointing at a point, focusing it or tapping it says its time and its temperature, and a look at the readings before the change and at the highest or lowest after it opens the notebook's first entries. It is also said in words.
4. **The notebook**: a worksheet of entries in the order they are worked out. For parts 1 and 2: the starting temperature; the highest or the lowest temperature reached; the change in temperature; the heat the cup's contents took in or gave out, in joules; whether the reaction gave heat out or took it in (a choice); the moles; and the heat for each mole, in kJ/mol, with its sign. For part 3: the heat the water took in; the specific heat of the metal, in J/(g·°C); and which metal it is (a choice).

A temperature typed must be the reading the logger shows, to the tenth of a degree. (As first specified a tenth of a degree either side was taken as well. Changed by the owner on 2026-10-10: a look at a reading gives it to 0.1 °C, so nothing is estimated, and with the tenth either side a run could have every entry taken and then not count; section 16.7 says more.) Every other entry must follow from the learner's own earlier entries, correctly rounded, as the typed molarity of 7.3 must. Guided checks each entry as it is made and has the relation it uses ready in a closed hint; Challenge checks them together at the end. Feedback says which entry does not follow and never the value.

**A run counts** when its temperatures are the logger's, each entry follows, the lid was on and the cup was stirred throughout, and its result is within the tolerance of what the model gives for that run. A run that does not count says why. One run that counts completes a part; a part can be run again, with new numbers. The lab is complete when all three parts are.

### 16.3 Model

The engine is pure and deterministic. The heat of a run is the moles reacted or dissolved times a stored enthalpy, or for the metal its mass times its stored specific heat times its fall in temperature. The cup's contents warm or cool as their mass times the specific heat of water, with solutions taken as 1.00 g/mL: the same idealisation the learner's own arithmetic uses, said as one in the primer. The temperature approaches its end value over a mixing time, short when stirred and long when not; and it exchanges heat with the room, slowly with the lid on and several times faster with it off, so that the curve reaches a peak and drifts back. The learner takes the peak. The calorimeter itself takes up no heat, and the hot metal reaches the cup at once and dry: both idealised, and the primer says what a careful bench does about each.

Magnitudes are model parameters with rationales, chosen so that a careful run is within tolerance at every seed and each technique error alone (the lid off; not stirred) puts a run outside it at every seed, as 7.3's are. No cited constant is adjusted to make that so.

### 16.4 Data

Enthalpies of formation from the NBS tables, as elsewhere; the specific heats of the four metals from a source that can be cited, given for a gram as worked out from the value for a mole; molar masses; the specific heat of water. Hazards by 5.2: the 1 M acid and base carry the records of section 8's lab; solid ammonium nitrate and the four metals get their own; boiling water and a hot piece of metal are in the primer's safety topic.

### 16.5 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: temperature and heat; giving out heat and taking it in, and the sign of the change; specific heat, and heat as mass times specific heat times the change in temperature; the calorimeter and what it assumes; reading the graph; from heat to heat for each mole; a metal's specific heat; safety.

### 16.6 Acceptance

- For seeds 1 to 100, a careful run of each part counts, and its result is within tolerance of the stored value: the neutralisation's heat for each mole, the ammonium nitrate's, and the metal's specific heat, which also picks the right metal out of the four.
- For those seeds, with the two temperatures the logger's own, a careful cup counts whichever way the rounding rule lets each entry after them be worked: no run whose technique is right and whose every entry follows fails on the tolerance alone. (Added on 2026-10-10.)
- Each technique error alone makes a run not count at every one of those seeds, and its cause is named.
- An entry that does not follow from the learner's own numbers is refused or marked, among them a result with the wrong sign and one cut off where it should be rounded.
- The same seed and actions give the same run; the stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on; Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the entries are made.
- The descriptions in words of the cup and of the graph are tested.

### 16.7 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-09, straight through, as the flame test lab's screens were (section 13), on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8 and 15. The route is `#/lab/calorimetry`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it with its status. Where the page is other than 16.2 has it, the point below says so in its first words.

- **Lab tab: one part at a time.** A list of the three parts, each with how far it has come in words beside a mark (not started; cup under way; worksheet under way; run did not count; done). Previous and Next are named for where they go. The list is the shared paged list, beside the part from the same width as the primer's topics and behind its button below that. Over it, a line says that the lab is complete once a run of each part counts.
- **A part's head.** Which run of the part this is and how far the part has come, the part's title, what the part finds, and a line that says the part is done once a run of it counts. What the part finds is shown in both modes, from the start. **Other than 16.1's wording:** the sentences for parts 1 and 2 do not say which way the heat goes, since that is an entry of those two worksheets: the heat for each mole of water formed when an acid and a base are mixed, and the heat for each mole of ammonium nitrate that dissolves in water. (As first built the two sentences said "given out" and "taken in", and Challenge was not shown what a part finds until the part was done. Changed at the review of 2026-10-09.)
- **A part is a column of cards,** each there when the engine has a use for it: the bench and the cup from the start; the graph once the logger is started; the notebook once the change is started; and a card named "This run" once there is a run to leave behind (the change started, the logger full, the worksheet checked, or the part done). **Not as short as 16.2 says:** with the cup worked and four entries made, part 1 measured between 2,300 and 2,410 px long and part 3 between 2,500 and 2,650 px at the six window sizes tried, which is a little over two screens of a tablet held upright and more than three of a window 768 px tall. (Measured at the review of 2026-10-09, with the bench's lines at the head of the notebook; as first built, 85 to 195 px less.)
- **The bench.** What is used, as a short list in words, with what the balance reads where something is weighed and, in part 3, what the thermometer in the boiling water reads. The acid, the base and the ammonium nitrate each have the shared bottle tile, with the formula on the label and the hazards closed. The water has no tile: the record holds no hazards for it. Part 3 has the table of the four metals the piece may be, each with its specific heat, and under a heading that says why, a tile for each of the four, which is the metal's name, its symbol and its hazards: a piece of metal is in no bottle. Once a run of part 3 counts, the heading names the metal the piece was and one tile is left; the table keeps all four. Each line of the list is as true once the change is started as before it, since the list stands for the whole run and is said again in the notebook: the second solution "is poured in" and the piece "is heated in boiling water" (as first built, "ready to pour in" and "is in boiling water"; reworded at the review of 2026-10-09).
- **The cup.** Drawn as a cut-away in the colour of the page's text, so that it follows the theme, and hidden from a screen reader: two cups one inside the other on the bench, the liquid, the lid on the cup or lying on the bench beside it, the stirrer with marks about its ring while it is going, the probe wired to the logger, whose screen shows a dot and a level row of dashes once it is started, and beside the cup what is to go into it: the second solution's beaker, the salt's weighing boat, or the beaker of boiling water with the piece in it. Once the change is started the liquid stands higher, the beaker or the boat is empty, and the piece lies in the cup. Nothing in the drawing says what the temperature does. The stirrer bobs four times, for under four seconds, as the drawing first shows it going, where the reduced-motion setting allows, and is still under it. Beside the drawing a status line says the same state in words. Under it are four buttons: the lid, the stirrer, the logger, and the one action that starts the change. A step that has been taken carries a tick for as long as the run is shown, so a run that did not count for want of the lid shows it.
- **Run on is over the graph. Other than 16.2,** which has it among the cup's buttons: a press of it shows in the graph and nowhere else, and in the cup's card it stood a screen away from the points it adds. What one press does is said beside it. Where the logger is full and the curve has not turned back (the change started at the logger's last reading, or not at all), Run on and the notebook say the same thing: that the worksheet cannot open and the part is to be run again. (As first built Run on said to fill in the worksheet and the notebook said to run on. Put right at the review of 2026-10-09.)
- **Guided's warning** is drawn in the cup's card when the change is started with the lid off or the stirrer still, and is the shared component: one group, named by what it warns of, both where both are skipped. It takes focus, and again when the same press raises it again. "Go back" puts focus on the first step that was skipped; "Start the change anyway" starts the change and puts focus on the button that started it. Any other press of the cup drops it, and so does a change to Challenge; once dropped it is raised again only by the press that raised it, and it is not stored. The lid's warning, and the reason given for a run that did not count for want of the lid, say that the cup trades heat with the room through its open top. (As first built they said that the cup loses heat there, and that heat left through it: the cup of part 2 is colder than the room and takes heat in. Reworded at the review of 2026-10-09.)
- **The graph is explored.** It is drawn in the colour of the page's text on the card's own colour, 480 by 280 of its own units, scaled as a whole and no wider than 512 px, so that it is not taller than a short window has room for with the worksheet under it: temperature up the side on marks of whole degrees, time along the bottom in whole minutes, a point for each reading with a thin line through them, and a dashed line just after the reading the change followed. The time axis spans two minutes at the least and ends on a whole minute with room after the latest reading; the temperature axis takes in every reading with half a degree or more over and under. A button lies over each reading: a column the plot's whole height, the columns meeting, so that a pointer or a finger anywhere on the plot is on the reading nearest to it. A mouse pointer over a column, keyboard focus on it or a tap looks at it: the point is ringed, a callout at the head of the picture says its time and its temperature, the same words go to a status line for a screen reader, and the look is stored the first time. A point that has been looked at is filled and one that has not is open, and a key under the graph says which is which and what the dashed line is. **Other than the flame test lab's pictures,** the callout does not go when the pointer or the focus leaves: it stays until another reading is looked at or Escape is pressed, so that it can still be read while the worksheet is typed in. It stands in room the picture keeps at its head, over no point of the graph, and inside the picture wherever its reading is.
- **The graph by keyboard and by touch.** The graph is one stop of the Tab key, on the reading last looked at, or on the first until one has been. The left and right arrow keys move one reading back and on, Home and End go to the first and the last, and moving looks at each reading as focus reaches it; a line beside the graph says what the arrow keys do. Where the picture is at its narrowest a column is 44 px wide or more only while the logger has taken eight readings or fewer, and nine readings measured 33 px each there, so two buttons under the graph, a finger's size each, step one reading back and one on. A logger run to its last reading has 241 columns, each under 2 px wide in the narrowest window, and is still walked by the keys and by those two buttons.
- **The graph in words** (16.6) is in the page, in both modes, behind a disclosure that is closed. **16.2 says only that the graph is also said in words:** the description names the starting temperature and the highest or lowest outright, which are the first two entries of the worksheet, so it is not laid open beside the picture. It says what the logger has drawn so far and nothing of what it will draw, and reading it opens no entry: an entry opens on a look.
- **The notebook** is a numbered list of the worksheet's entries in the order they are worked out, with the shared calculator's button over it. Between the two, under the words "From the bench", the bench's own lines are said again, with the four metals and their specific heats as one line more in part 3: by the fifth entry the bench's card is 1,840 to 2,220 px up the page from the entry that waits, at the six window sizes tried, and these lines 610 to 680 px. They hold nothing that the bench does not show from the start, and no state. (Added at the review of 2026-10-09.) Until the curve has turned back the list is not drawn, and one sentence says what opens it. From then on one entry waits at a time. Those above it are made: each a disabled field that shows the entry, or disabled choice boxes with the one chosen, beside "Change". The one that waits has its field, a finger tall, or its choice boxes, with "Enter", how the entry is written under the field, and in Guided the relation it uses in a closed disclosure. **Other than the titration lab's notebook,** the entries still to come are their names alone and have no field, so that the list stays short. The first entry waits for a look at a reading before the change and the second for a look at the highest or the lowest, each with the reason under its field. A refusal takes the place of the line that says how the entry is written, as an alert. In Guided an entry that is made says beside a tick that it follows; in Challenge nothing is said of an entry until the worksheet is checked, and "Check the worksheet" is under the list, where it says what is missing until every entry is made. Changing an entry empties the entries under it, as the engine has it, and the list says so once two entries are made. A choice that is emptied in that way is asked afresh, with nothing chosen and nothing said of it. Enter in a choice box makes the entry and leaves focus on its button, which then reads "Change"; a change of a choice that Guided refuses stays open, with the refusal, until another choice is entered or the change is cancelled. (As first built the choice that went stayed chosen, with "Follows" still beside it in Guided; Enter in a choice box dropped focus to the page; and a refused change closed on the old choice with the refusal beside it. The last two were the shared form's, and were put right there at the review of 2026-10-09.) No refusal sends the learner to the relation, since a worksheet that has been checked and Challenge's have none to open, and part 2's moles, which come from a mass, are not told that millilitres are not litres, as they were when first built. The two entries that may begin with a minus sign ask for a keyboard that has one. Once the run is over, or the lab complete, the list is read and not typed in: each entry as its value beside its label, with whether it follows and, where it does not, what kind of slip it looks like, and never the value.
- **The two temperatures are the logger's own. Other than 16.2 first had it,** which took a temperature within 0.1 °C of the logger's. The reading's tolerance is a parameter of the record, and was 1 tenth of a degree; over seeds 1 to 100, with a careful cup and every entry worked correctly from the temperatures typed, one temperature a tenth off left 2 runs of part 3 in 100 outside the 5%, and both a tenth off, toward each other, left 68 of part 2 and 42 of part 3 outside it, each with every entry told in Guided that it follows. The owner set it to nothing on 2026-10-10. In Guided a temperature a tenth off is now refused at once, with the sentence that was there for any other reading; in Challenge it is taken, as everything is, and marked when the worksheet is checked. With that, over the same seeds, every chain of entries the rounding rule allows from a careful cup counts (16.6): 1.57% low in part 1, 2.89% at the most in part 2 and 3.08% in part 3; and over every setup the draw can give, tried once by a throwaway search, 3.52% at the most, for the tin piece. The 5% was not changed. The stored entry's version was left at 1, since the lab had not been released and no stored run could be judged differently.
- **This run.** Whether the worksheet has been checked, and then that the run counts or that it does not and why, beside a tick or a cross; focus goes there when the worksheet is checked, since the controls it was on are gone, and the ring that shows it stands off the words. Once the lab is complete the sentence for a run that did not count does not say to run the part again. (Added at the review of 2026-10-09. The review also added a sentence for a run that did not count for its result alone, with the cup worked rightly and every entry following, which said to look at the two temperatures, since one typed 0.1 °C off the logger's was then taken and could be enough. It was taken out on 2026-10-10, when a typed temperature came to be held to the logger's exactly: no such run comes to that any more.) "Run this part again" is under it, with what it does said beside it: the new run is a column of its own, with nothing typed or said in the one before carried over, and focus goes to the part's heading. Once a run of the part counts, and not before, a closed disclosure named "Notes on this part" holds the figure the result was held to, the record's notes on the part and, for part 3, how the metal's figure in the table was worked out.
- **Sources** are at the foot of each part from the start. **Other than the flame test lab's,** which come when a station is done: nothing in this list says a figure a result is held to, and part 3 lists all four metals whichever the piece is. The list has 4 px of room under it, so that the hit area of its closed disclosure does not lie under that of Previous, in the row below: it measured 40 px tall in parts 2 and 3 as first built, and 44 after.
- **Record tab:** one table with a group of rows for each part: the part with how far it has come, then every run of it in order with how it came out (not checked yet, does not count, or counts beside a tick), and under a run what was entered, each entry beside its label, and why it did not count. It shows nothing of what a result is held to. A run's reasons are given there as they are on the Lab tab, but for the words that send the reader to the worksheet's marks: the record has no worksheet beside it. Once the lab is complete the line that says so is over the table, as it is on the Lab tab.
- **What was made shared.** The labelled field of the titration lab's notebook is now `src/app/lab/Field.tsx`, as it was, with three things it can be asked for: a line that says how an entry is written, a finger's height, and a keyboard with a minus sign. The titration lab's markup is unchanged.
- **What Challenge is kept from before a run of the part counts:** the figure a result is held to, which metal the piece is, the notes, each entry's relation, whether an entry follows until the worksheet is checked, and how far off a run that did not count was. Automated tests search the Lab tab's markup for each at every step of a careful run of each part up to the check, and after a check that does not count; and, at every step up to the check, for any word of which way the heat goes outside the two choices of the entry that asks it and the line that says how a signed entry is written. The Primer tab is not kept back in either mode, and its safety card says that the cup of part 2 gets colder than the room.
- **The descriptions in words** of the cup and of the graph (16.6) are both in the page: the first beside the drawing, the second behind its disclosure.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is typed and not yet entered, the part that is shown, the reading last looked at and its callout, a warning that is showing, whether the calculator is open, and what the message lines last said. A reload opens on the first part begun and not done, with its cup, its graph and its entries as the stored record has them. A part that is not the one shown has no calculator, so that no panel floats over the page with no button of its own in view; going to another part closes it.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the hand checklist of section 13 was run by script, by keyboard, by mouse and by emulated touch, in headless Chromium, by the AI coding assistant that built the screens and by nobody at a keyboard. `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. The review of the phase was held on 2026-10-09 in the same way, by an AI coding assistant that had not built the screens: what it found and did, and what it left, is the last section of `docs/research/calorimetry/README.md`. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device. No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer.
- **Room over the hint of an entry that is chosen, since 2026-10-12.** Found by the builder of the endothermic and exothermic lab and measured by that lab's reviewer, by script in headless Chromium at 768×1024 and at 1280×800, on the entry for which way the heat went: the hint's line stood 18 px under "Enter", and open, its hit area, which reaches 24 px up from its line, answered for the lowest 7 of that button's 44 rows of pixels (133 of 836 points asked). The page now asks the shared worksheet for 12 px of room over that hint, as section 26.6 has it: the line is 30 px under the button, and no point of "Enter" answers for anything else. The same for the entry that names the metal.

## 17. Group I cations lab (added after v1)

Asked for by the owner on 2026-10-09, the first of a set of labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions. Those directions say what is set out for a lab and not what a learner does, and some of what they say is out of date: by the owner's word the labs and their outcomes are what matters, and the particulars are corrected and brought up to date here. So the procedure is the usual scheme for these three ions, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell (Primer, Lab, Record; Guided and Challenge), the test tube that is explored and the observation form of section 8, and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

A real bench of this lab holds lead, mercury, silver and chromate. That is the case for a virtual one, and the primer's safety topic says so.

### 17.1 Set-up

Qualitative analysis: finding which ions a solution holds by what they do, not how much there is. The three ions are silver, Ag⁺; lead, Pb²⁺; and mercury(I), Hg₂²⁺. They are the ones whose chlorides do not dissolve in cold dilute acid, which is what sets them apart from every other common metal ion.

On the bench: hydrochloric acid, 6 M; distilled water and a bath of boiling water; acetic acid, 6 M, and potassium chromate solution, 0.5 M; ammonia solution, 6 M; nitric acid, 6 M; blue and red litmus paper; a centrifuge. The samples are made from 0.1 M solutions of silver nitrate, lead nitrate and mercury(I) nitrate.

Two parts:

1. **The known.** A sample that holds all three ions, worked through the scheme so that every positive result is seen once.
2. **An unknown.** A sample that holds one, two or all three of the ions, which of the seven by the seed. The learner works it through the same scheme and says which ions it holds.

### 17.2 The scheme

Five steps, each on a tube the step before it left:

1. **Precipitate the group.** Hydrochloric acid is added to the sample: a white solid, the chlorides of whichever of the three are there. The tube is centrifuged and the liquid poured off.
2. **Take the lead out.** Hot water is added to the solid and the tube is heated in the bath: lead chloride dissolves in hot water and the other two do not. The tube is centrifuged and the hot liquid poured off into a second tube.
3. **Confirm lead.** Acetic acid and potassium chromate go into the second tube: a yellow solid, lead chromate, if lead is there. The chromate colours the liquid yellow to orange whether lead is there or not, and with no lead the liquid stays clear: a coloured liquid is not a yellow solid, and the tube's picture marks which is which.
4. **Part silver from mercury.** Ammonia is added to the solid left by step 2. Silver chloride dissolves, as a colourless ion of silver with ammonia. Mercury(I) chloride does not dissolve: it turns grey to black, which confirms mercury(I). The tube is centrifuged and the liquid poured off into a third tube.
5. **Confirm silver.** Nitric acid is added to the third tube, a little at a time, until litmus shows the liquid is acid: a white solid, silver chloride come back, if silver is there.

### 17.3 A run

A part is the five steps, one at a time. A step is a short column of cards:

1. **The tube**, drawn, with its layers marked as liquid or solid, and explored: pointing at a layer, focusing it or tapping it says what is seen there (never what it is). The step's actions are beside it: add the step's reagent, heat, centrifuge, pour off, test with litmus, each offered when the engine has a use for it.
2. **What is seen**: choice boxes, opened by looking, as section 8's observation form: is there a solid, what colour, what the liquid looks like.
3. **Where each ion went** (part 1 only): a flow chart of the scheme with empty places, filled from a set of tiles, one step's worth after each step: which of the three is in the solid, which in the liquid, and as what. In part 2 the finished chart is beside the work as a map, once part 1 is done.
4. **Which ions** (part 2 only, after step 5): three boxes, any of which may be ticked. A naming of none is refused and asked again: no sample holds none of the three.

Guided warns before a technique error and lets the learner go on, checks each observation and each placing as it is made, and has a hint ready in a closed disclosure. Challenge warns of nothing and checks a step's observations together when asked.

**A run counts** when the technique was right, each observation matches what the tube showed, and, in part 2, the ions named are the ones the sample holds. A run that does not count says why. One run that counts completes a part; part 2 can be run again, with a new unknown. The lab is complete when both parts are.

Three errors of technique, each real and each its own cause:

- **Pouring off without centrifuging.** The solid goes over with the liquid, and the tube poured into is cloudy.
- **Pouring off the water of step 2 without heating it.** Only part of any lead chloride has dissolved; the rest stays in the solid and is still there, white, after the ammonia of step 4.
- **Judging step 5 before the liquid is acid,** which is recording what is seen there before litmus paper has shown it acid. Until then no solid forms, whether silver is there or not.

Each makes the run not count wherever it is made, whatever the sample holds, so that neither a warning nor a verdict tells what an unknown holds: the water of step 2 poured off cold does not count for a sample with no lead, nor step 5 judged early for one with no silver.

The net ionic equation of each step is shown in the notes once the step is done, with its source. It is not built by the learner: section 8's lab is where equations are written.

### 17.4 Model

The engine is pure and deterministic. A tube holds whole-number amounts of what is dissolved in it and of each solid. Each action changes them by a stored rule that has a source: the three chlorides form with hydrochloric acid; lead chloride dissolves in hot water, and in cold water as far as its solubility allows; lead chromate forms with chromate; silver chloride dissolves in ammonia and comes back with acid; mercury(I) chloride with ammonia gives a grey to black solid. Where a stored number can check a rule (an ion product against a solubility product at the bench's amounts; the lead chloride against what the water added can hold, hot and cold; the acid added against the ammonia it has to neutralise), the build makes the check and stops on a disagreement.

Simplifications, each said in the primer: a pouring off is taken to be clean, where a careful bench washes each solid; the amounts are fixed by the bench, not chosen.

### 17.5 Data

Solubility products from the Gibbs energies of the NBS tables, as elsewhere, or from another source that can be cited; the solubility of lead chloride in cold and in hot water; the formation constant of the silver ion with ammonia; what each solid and each liquid looks like; what mercury(I) chloride does with ammonia; molar masses. Hazards by 5.2 for every bottle on the bench and for the three nitrates.

### 17.6 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: what qualitative analysis is; solids from solutions, and what a solubility product says; the three chlorides; parting ions by a difference (hot water); complex ions (silver and ammonia); mercury(I) and what ammonia does to it; confirming tests, and a yellow liquid against a yellow solid; centrifuging and pouring off; reading the flow chart; safety.

### 17.7 Acceptance

- For the known and for each of the seven unknowns, a careful run shows at every step what its ions give and nothing else, and counts; naming its ions completes part 2, and a naming of none is refused.
- Each error of technique alone makes a run not count wherever it is made, whatever the sample holds, and its cause is named; Guided warns first.
- Every rule and every stored value has a source; the build's checks of rules against numbers pass.
- The same seed and actions give the same run; the stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the observations are recorded, and nothing on the page says which ions an unknown holds before the learner has named them.
- The description in words of every tube is tested.

### 17.8 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-10, straight through, as the calorimetry lab's screens were (section 13), on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8, 15 and 16. The route is `#/lab/cations`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it, as "Group I cations", with its status. Where the page is other than 17.3 has it, or other than the labs before it, the point below says so in its first words.

- **Lab tab: two parts, and one entry at a time.** A navigation named Parts has a button for each part, with its title and how far it has come in words beside a mark (not started; steps under way; ions to be named; run did not count; done); the one shown is marked as current. Those words are grey on the page's ground and the text's own colour on the grey ground of the part shown, or under the pointer; so are the number and the note of an entry in the shared paged list (changed at the review: grey on that ground measured 4.35 to 1 in the light theme, in words 12 px tall). **Other than the calorimetry lab's parts,** which are the entries of the paged list: here a part has a list of its own. Of the part shown, the shared paged list has six entries: the five steps, each with how it stands in words beside a mark (not reached yet, under way, done), and the entry that closes a run, which is the whole flow chart in part 1 and the naming of the ions in part 2. One entry is shown at a time, and Previous and Next are named for where they go. The list is beside the entry from the same width as the primer's topics, and behind its button below that. Until anything is done in either part the tab says at its head what the lab is, and until a part's run is begun the part says what it is for and that the lab is complete once a run of each part counts; from then on those lines are gone and the room is the step's.
- **A step's head.** Which run of the part this is, which step of the five, and how it stands; the step's title; what is done at it, in both modes, which names no colour and nothing of what will be seen; and in Guided the hint, in a disclosure that is closed. At steps 1, 2 and 4 the task has the centrifuge before the record (changed at the review, 2026-10-10: as first built it had the record first and then the centrifuge and the pouring off, and in part 1, once the chart was filled, the centrifuge beside the tube was 925 to 1,037 px over the pouring off at 768 by 1024, more than the window shows, so that a learner who followed the task went up the page and came down again). The engine takes either order, as before.
- **A step is a short column of cards,** each there when the engine has a use for it: the tube from the start; what is seen, once the step's reagent is in; in part 1 the flow chart, with it; and under the cards what ends the step. A step not reached yet shows its task and no card. A step that is done shows its tube as it was when the step was checked, with a sentence that says so, what was recorded there as lines to be read, in part 1 the chart with what the check found, and its notes.
- **The tube is explored.** It is the tube of section 8.5, made a shared component: drawn 320 by 260 of its own units on a white card in both themes, with a real button laid over each of its two parts, the liquid and under it the bottom, where a solid settles. A mouse pointer over a part, keyboard focus on it or a tap looks at it: the part is outlined, a callout beside it says what is seen there and nothing of what it is, the same words go to a status line for a screen reader, and the look is stored the first time. Both looks open the questions. Anything that moves the solid, the centrifuge among them, empties the looks, and the tube is looked at again. A part not yet looked at has a dashed edge, a line beside the picture says of each in words whether it has been looked at, and the name of its button says so too. That line is there once the step's reagent is in: before it, a look says what is there and is not kept, as the engine has it, and there is nothing yet to record. The bottom's button is 58 units tall and the liquid's 58 or more, from a little over the liquid's surface, and both are there whatever the tube holds, so a look at the liquid of a tube with none in it finds that out. A step that is done can still be looked at, and stores nothing.
- **A liquid is told from a solid by more than its colour.** A liquid is drawn in its colour up to its surface, under a bracket with the word "liquid". A solid that has settled is drawn at the bottom with an edge and a grain over it, under a bracket with the word "solid"; the grain is the ink's, and light over the grey to black solid, on which the ink would not show. A solid that has not settled is drawn as specks, each with an edge, all through the liquid, under one bracket with the words "solid in the liquid". So at step 3 a sample with no lead shows a smooth yellow to orange liquid under one bracket, and one with lead a solid with its edge, its grain and its own bracket under that liquid. The colours are the drawing's own values for the record's words; by those values the yellow of the solid and the yellow to orange of the liquid differ by 1.6 to 1 as a ratio of luminances, and a white solid and a colourless liquid by 1.2 to 1, which is why neither is left to the colour. Every solid is drawn at one height, since the engine gives no amounts of solid. A liquid stands higher for each thing that goes into its tube, not to scale: the least a step works with is drawn at about six tenths of the height of the most, so that a liquid over a solid is deep enough to be seen.
- **The rack** is in the picture, in the room beside the tube over where the callouts open: the three tubes drawn small, each holding what it holds now, each over its name, the step's own on a marked ground with its name in bold. It is drawn for the step that is being worked. The picture of a step that is done is of its tube as it was, has no rack, and is named for that and not as a tube drawn in a rack (the name was put right at the review). The tube described in words (17.7), for each of the three by name, is in a disclosure that is closed, since it says the whole of what the picture shows: beside the picture's foot where the card is narrow, and under the other words where it is wide.
- **The step's actions. Other than 17.3,** which has them all beside the tube. The buttons that add the reagent, heat the tube (step 2), test with litmus paper (step 5) and centrifuge are beside the picture: a column beside it where the card is narrow, in rows beside it with the card's words where it is wide. The check, which Challenge asks for, and the pouring off are under the step's cards. The pouring off ends a step and can be made only once what is seen is recorded and checked, which in part 1 is under the flow chart, a screen below the tube; under the cards it is where the learner is when it can be done. A button that cannot be used now stays focusable and says why when pressed. One that names a thing done that cannot be done again carries a tick; the nitric acid of step 5 goes in up to four times, and its button has none until the tube is full. What has been done to the tube so far is said in a status line under the picture, and what litmus paper shows is said there.
- **Guided's warning** is the shared component, raised under the button that would make the slip: under the pouring off for a solid that is still spread through the liquid and for the water of step 2 poured off cold, both in one group where both apply, and under the questions for step 5 recorded before litmus has shown the liquid acid. It takes focus, and again when the same press raises it again. "Go back" puts focus on what was skipped, the centrifuge, the bath, or the litmus paper or the acid; the way on sends the same action again. Any other press of the step drops it, and so does a change to Challenge; it is not stored.
- **What is seen** is the shared form of choices, with two questions: the solid in the tube (none, or its colour) and the colour of the liquid. Both are closed until both parts of the tube have been looked at, and what opens them is said once, under the first; the shared form takes an empty reason for a question that waits for what the one over it waits for. In Guided an observation that is not what the tube shows is not taken, and the form says which part to look at again and never what it should be; one that is taken is shown in its boxes, disabled, beside "Change", and said to be what the tube showed. In Challenge whatever is recorded is taken, shown the same way, and said to be not checked yet. Once the step is checked the form is gone and the observation is lines to be read, with a cross on an answer that is not what the tube showed and what the check found beside a mark; while the pouring off is still to come, the card says why nothing more is taken. A mode changed to Guided with an observation recorded in Challenge holds that observation to the tube.
- **The flow chart of part 1. Other than 17.3 in how it is drawn:** a tree of nested lists, not a drawing with arrows. Each of the seven places is under the place it is made from, which the page works out from the tube each step is worked in: a step worked in a tube an earlier step was worked in goes on from that step's solid, and a step worked in a new tube from the liquid last poured off. So the liquid of step 2, with step 3's solid under it, is over step 2's solid, with step 4's two places and step 5's under them. Until what is seen at a step is recorded, the chart's card is one line that says what opens it. Then it is the whole tree: the step's own places to be filled, each a button, and the others a line each, with what they hold. The places are filled from nine tiles under the tree, each named for what it is, its formula and whether it is a solid or dissolved: a tile is tapped and then its place, or the place and then each tile; where one place alone is open it is the chosen one from the start; the keyboard does the same with the same buttons, and nothing is dragged. A tile is not used up, and a tile in an open place has a button that takes it out. In Guided a tile that does not belong is refused, each open place says how many tiles belong in it, and the step is checked by itself when the last one goes in. In Challenge anything is taken and no count is given. Once the step is checked each of its places says in words beside a mark whether it holds what belongs in it. The whole chart is the last entry of part 1's list, to be read.
- **The map of part 2. Other than 17.3,** which has the finished chart beside the work: it is under the ways on of every entry of part 2, in a disclosure that is closed, once a run of part 1 counts, and a sentence stands there until then. At the widths a tablet has there is no room beside a step, and the map names what each thing of the scheme is, which is what a learner is to work out. It is the scheme's and marks no place right or not.
- **The entry that closes a run.** In part 1 the whole chart. In part 2 the naming: three boxes, any of which may be ticked, there and disabled with the reason under them until the five steps are done, and said once for each run. A press with no box ticked is asked again and sends nothing: the engine refuses a naming of none, since the naming is said once and no sample holds none of the three (as first built this was the page's own rule and the engine would have taken such a naming; the rule was made the engine's at the review, with its sentence among the lab's messages). Under either, a card named "This run" says that the run is not finished yet, or that it counts, or that it does not and why, and for an unknown what the sample held, which the engine hands over only then; it takes focus when the run ends. "Run this part again" is under it, with what it does beside it: unavailable until a run is begun, and still taken once the lab is complete, as the engine has it. A new run is a column of its own, on its first step, with nothing chosen or said in the one before carried over.
- **The notes and the net ionic equations** of a step are in a disclosure that is closed, once the step is done and not before, since they say what each thing is; in part 2 a line over the equations says that each is for an ion the sample may or may not hold. Until then a line says that they come.
- **Under the ways on, on every entry:** the sources of the step shown, closed, from the start, since the list is the scheme's whatever a sample holds and no label in it names a substance; the bench; and in part 2 the map. **Other than the calorimetry lab's bench,** which is the first card of a part: the bench is a disclosure that is closed, one place that every step's page has, and it stays as it was left from step to step. It holds what stands on the bench as a list in words, a bottle's tile with its hazards closed for each of the five reagents, and under a heading that says why all three are shown, one for each of the three solutions the samples are made from. The water has no tile. The sources, the bench and the map are each a line of words 20 px tall under a hit area of 44, which reaches 12 px over and under a closed one and 24 px over an open one: they stand 36 px apart and 28 px under the ways on, and a step's notes have 4 px more under them, so that no hit area lies over another control (changed at the review: 8 px apart, a tap on Sources with the bench open under it closed the bench).
- **Focus** is not left on the page itself. Where the control that had it is gone (the form of a step that Guided has checked, the last tile, the check), it goes to the pouring off, or to the line that says the step is done, which is always in the page and empty until then. The parts, the list, Previous and Next and "Run this part again" move it to the heading of the entry shown. A tile's button that takes it out leaves focus on its place.
- **Nothing on the page moves.** There is no animation in it, so the reduced-motion setting has nothing to stop.
- **Record tab:** one table with a group of rows for each part: the part with how far it has come; every run of it in order with how it came out; under a run each step at which something was recorded, with what was recorded and what the check found, or that it is not checked yet; in part 1 whether every place checked so far holds what belongs in it; in part 2 the ions named and, once they are named, what the sample held; and why a run did not count. Once the lab is complete the line that says so is over the table, as it is on the Lab tab.
- **What was made shared.** The tube that is explored is `src/app/lab/Tube.tsx`: it is told its picture's name and height, what is in it (a liquid with where its surface is, or none; a solid at the bottom; a solid spread through the liquid; bubbles), the words beside its brackets and the sections to lay buttons over, and draws what it is handed in the room beside the tube. The aqueous reactions lab's markup is unchanged. The shared form of choices takes an empty reason, as said above.
- **What Challenge and an unknown are kept from.** Before a step is done: its notes and its equations, in either mode. Before a step is checked, in Challenge: whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, the hint, and how many tiles belong in a place. Before an unknown's ions are named: which it holds. Automated tests draw the Lab tab at eleven points of a careful run of each part in each mode and search its markup for the notes and equations of every step that is not done; and draw it at sixteen points of each of the seven unknowns of a seed and hold two pages whose tubes have shown the same things to being the same, to the byte. What the page stores is the engine's record, which holds what was done and nothing of what a sample holds. The Primer tab is not kept back in either mode.
- **Two things were built other than this section was first written,** each the builder's, of 2026-10-09, with its reason in the research record (`docs/research/cations/README.md`, which lists seven more). Sections 17.2, 17.3 and 17.7 said that the liquid of step 3 is yellow, and that a slip of technique makes a run not count wherever it changes what is seen; at the review of 2026-10-10 they were put right to what was built. The liquid of step 3 is "yellow to orange": one page read calls a chromate solution yellow and another calls the liquid over the solid orange, and the stored word takes in both, so the choice the page offers for the liquid is "yellow to orange". And a slip of technique makes a run not count wherever it is made: a warning or a verdict that turned on what the sample holds would say what an unknown holds.
- **How long a step's page is,** measured in headless Chromium, in Guided, with the menu open, over the five steps of both parts. At 768 by 1024, where 959 px of the window are under the tab bar once it is at the window's top: from the line over a step's title to the foot of the card of questions, which is the tube, its buttons and its questions, 922 to 962 px while the questions wait for the looks and 876 to 916 once they are open; the two parts' buttons and the list's button stand 126 px over that. A whole step's page, from that line to the foot of its last line, is 614 to 698 px before its reagent is in; in part 2 940 to 1,024 with the questions open and 740 to 760 done; in part 1, where the chart is under the questions, 1,042 to 1,126 with the questions open, 1,699 to 1,881 while the chart is being filled, which is the longest it gets, and 1,114 to 1,246 done (measured again at the review, on the build it left: a step that is done is 4 px longer than it was first built, for the room under its notes). At 1280 by 800, with 735 px under the tab bar: 792 to 826 and 752 to 782 px to the foot of the questions, and 1,509 to 1,659 for a step of part 1 while its chart is being filled. So a step with its tube, its buttons and its questions is about one window of an upright tablet, and the chart of part 1 is a second. The review measured what drawing fewer places of the chart would save, 224 to 414 px of a step with only its own places, and left the chart whole: the research record's last section has the figures and the reasons.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is chosen and not yet recorded, the part and the entry shown, which tile is held and which place is chosen, the callout of a look, whether a disclosure is open, a warning that is showing, and what the message lines last said. A reload opens on the part whose run is begun and not over (in a lab that is complete, on a part that is being run again), at the step that run has reached, or at the entry that closes it once the five steps are done.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the hand checklist of section 13 was run by script, by keyboard, by mouse and by emulated touch, in headless Chromium, by the AI coding assistant that built the screens and by nobody at a keyboard. The one deep review of the lab was held on 2026-10-10 by an AI coding assistant that had built none of it, by script in the same browser and by reading: the last section of the research record says what it found, changed and left, and the points above say where the page is other than it was first built. `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device. No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer.

## 18. Le Chatelier's principle lab (added after v1)

The second of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used: the lab and its outcomes are kept, the particulars brought up to date). The directions list the bottles; the procedure is the usual one for those bottles, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell, the test tube that is explored and the observation form of section 8, and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

### 18.1 Set-up

Le Chatelier's principle: a system at equilibrium that is disturbed shifts in the direction that undoes part of the disturbance. The lab disturbs four equilibria, each in a test tube, by adding something, taking something away, heating or cooling, and has the learner say beforehand which way each will go, see what happens, and say which way it went.

On the bench: lead nitrate solution, 0.3 M; hydrochloric acid, 0.3 M, 6 M and 12 M; methyl violet indicator solution; solid cobalt(II) chloride; zinc nitrate solution, 0.1 M; magnesium nitrate solution, 0.1 M; sodium hydroxide solution, 6 M; ammonia solution, 6 M; distilled water; a bath of boiling water and a bath of ice water.

Nothing in this lab is drawn by chance: the same actions give the same tubes.

### 18.2 The four systems

Each system is headed by its equilibrium, written one way and kept that way, so that "left" and "right" mean something. Each has a short list of disturbances, done in order, each from the state the one before left.

1. **A solid and its ions: lead chloride.** Lead ions and chloride ions against solid lead chloride. Hydrochloric acid, 0.3 M, is added to the lead nitrate solution a portion at a time, three portions in all, and the tube is looked at after each: nothing after the first, a white solid after the second. The tube is heated, and the solid dissolves; cooled, and it comes back; water is added, and it dissolves. The number of portions is fixed and said beforehand: an addition that stopped when the solid came would say what the tube shows before it is looked at.
2. **An indicator: methyl violet.** The indicator's yellow acid form against its violet form and a hydrogen ion. Hydrochloric acid, 6 M, is added three drops at a time, twice, and the violet goes through a colour between to yellow; sodium hydroxide, 6 M, three drops at a time, twice, takes it back. The colour in the middle of the indicator's range is stored as "between blue and yellow" and has no name: the table read gives the change as yellow to blue, and no source read names what lies between. Three drops at a time keeps every tube clear of the two ends of the range, where a number could not decide the colour.
3. **A complex ion: cobalt.** The pink ion of cobalt with water against the blue ion of cobalt with chloride. Cobalt(II) chloride in hydrochloric acid, 12 M, is blue; water is added, one portion, and it turns violet, with both ions in the liquid; the violet tube is heated, and it turns blue; cooled in ice water, it turns pink. These are the changes the demonstration read shows, in the liquid it shows them in: the tube is not diluted on to pink before it is heated.
4. **Two hydroxides: zinc and magnesium.** Each metal ion with hydroxide ions against its solid hydroxide. A drop of sodium hydroxide gives a white solid with each. The solid is then shared among three tubes: acid dissolves both; more sodium hydroxide dissolves zinc's and not magnesium's; ammonia dissolves zinc's and not magnesium's. Zinc's hydroxide dissolves in more base and in ammonia because a second equilibrium, that of a complex ion, takes the zinc ion away; magnesium has none.

Systems 1 to 3 above, and the last card and the last rule of 18.3, are as the lab was built on 2026-10-10. `docs/research/lechatelier/README.md` says where that is other than this section first had it, and why.

### 18.3 A disturbance

A system is its disturbances, one at a time. A disturbance is a short column of cards:

1. **The equilibrium**, as written, and what is about to be done.
2. **Before**: which way will it shift (toward the left side as written, toward the right, or not at all)? One choice, recorded and never judged: it is there to be compared with what happens.
3. **The tube**, drawn and explored as in section 8, with the one action (add, heat or cool; for an addition by drops, one press for each portion, and the tube is looked at as often as the learner likes on the way).
4. **What is seen**: choice boxes, opened by looking: the liquid's colour, whether there is a solid.
5. **Which way it went**: one choice, checked. For the four tubes of system 4 that get more sodium hydroxide or ammonia, two of each metal, the question is instead what took the metal ion away: hydroxide, ammonia, or nothing. At zinc's two tubes the second equilibrium did; at magnesium's two the answer is that nothing did, and the solid is still there.

Once a disturbance is done, a note says what happened and why, with the learner's own prediction beside it.

Guided checks each answer as it is given and has a hint ready in a closed disclosure. Challenge takes whatever is recorded and checks a system's answers together when asked; a system that is not right is worked again. In Challenge the answer to the last card is given once: giving it ends the disturbance and opens its note, and what was recorded of the tube can be changed until then.

**A system counts** when each observation matches what its tube showed and each direction is right. There is no error of technique that spoils a run here: every change in this lab can be taken back, which is the point of it. The lab is complete when all four systems count.

### 18.4 Model

The engine is pure and deterministic, and holds whole-number amounts. What a tube shows after a disturbance comes from a stored rule with a source, and where stored numbers can decide it they do: whether lead chloride is present, from the amounts and its solubility cold and hot; the indicator's colour, from the hydrogen ion left after the acid and the base added and the indicator's stated range; whether a hydroxide is present, from the amounts and the constants of its equilibria. The cobalt system is held by rule and not by number: its colour at each of the lab's states (in concentrated acid, diluted, hot, cold) is a stored observation with a source, since no constant read covers 12 M acid. The amounts of each addition are model parameters with rationales, chosen so that what the numbers give is what the usual procedure shows; no cited constant is bent to that end.

Which disturbance gives off or takes in heat is from cited enthalpies where they can be read.

### 18.5 Data

Solubility products, formation constants and enthalpies from the NBS tables, as elsewhere, or from another source that can be cited; lead chloride's solubility cold and hot (section 17's); methyl violet's range and its colours; the colours of the two cobalt ions; what each tube shows, with a source. Hazards by 5.2 for every bottle; records another lab already holds for the same bottle are shared.

### 18.6 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: what equilibrium is (two changes at one rate, not no change); Le Chatelier's principle; adding and taking away; heating and cooling, and which way gives off heat; diluting; a solid and its ions; indicators; complex ions and colour; a second equilibrium that takes an ion away; safety.

### 18.7 Acceptance

- For each system, working its disturbances in order shows at each one what the stored rule gives and nothing else, and the system counts when each observation and each direction is right.
- Every rule and stored value has a source; where numbers decide what a tube shows, the build checks them at the lab's amounts and stops on a disagreement.
- A prediction is recorded before the action it is about and can be neither changed after it nor judged.
- The stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the learner's answers are recorded; a disturbance's note is shown only once it is done.
- The description in words of every tube is tested.

### 18.8 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-10 and 2026-10-11, straight through, as the Group I cations lab's screens were (section 13), on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8, 15, 16 and 17. The route is `#/lab/le-chatelier`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it, as "Le Chatelier's principle", with its status. Where the page is other than 18.3 has it, or other than the labs before it, the point below says so in its first words.

- **Lab tab: four systems, and one entry at a time.** A navigation named Systems has a button for each system, four to a row at every width, with its number, a short name and how far it has come in words beside a mark (not started; under way; to be checked; run did not count; done); the one shown is marked as current. The short name is what stands after the colon of the record's name ("Lead chloride"); the whole name heads the system until its run is begun, with what the system is and that the lab is complete once all four count, and heads the entry that closes a run. Of the system shown, the shared paged list has its disturbances, each with how it stands in words beside a mark (not reached yet, under way, done), and after them the entry that closes a run. One entry is shown at a time, and Previous and Next are named for where they go. The list stays behind its button until the page has 928 px, as the flame test lab's does, so that a disturbance has the width for two columns; from there it is beside the entry.
- **A disturbance's head.** Which system and which run this is and how the disturbance stands; and its title, with which of the system's disturbances it is.
- **A disturbance is three cards, not five, and each comes when the one before it is worked. Other than 18.3,** which has five cards in a column. The first is the equilibrium as written: the equation, what stands on each side with the colour that tells it, for cobalt a third line that says what a liquid holding both looks like, and what is about to be done; with the prediction under it, in the same card. The second is the tube. The third is what is seen, there once the prediction is made, with the question of the disturbance under it in the same card, there once what is seen is recorded. So what is on the page is what can be worked now, and a card not yet come is not drawn as a closed one. A disturbance not reached yet shows what will be done at it and no card.
- **An entry that is made folds to a line. Other than the labs before it,** whose forms show a recorded answer in its boxes, disabled. A prediction or an observation that is recorded is one or two lines beside "Change", and the boxes come back when Change is pressed. Once the change has begun the prediction is a line under the equilibrium, with the sentence that says it is fixed and no heading of its own. This is what keeps a disturbance to about one window of an upright tablet (below).
- **Two columns where there is room. Other than 18.3's one column.** Where a disturbance has 640 px, the tube's card stands on the left for the whole height and the other two cards on the right, one over the other, so that the picture is beside whatever is being answered. Narrower, the three are one column in the order they are worked. The order in the page itself, which the Tab key and a screen reader follow, is the order of working at every width. The rule follows the width the disturbance has, never the window's: with the menu open a window 768 px wide leaves it 497 px and one column.
- **The prediction** is one question with three choices, recorded with a button, changed until the change begins, and fixed after it. Nothing is said of it and no mark is drawn on it, then or later: where it stands again, with the notes and in the record, it stands as it was made. The button that makes the change says, if it is pressed first, that a prediction comes first and is not marked.
- **The choices of the two questions of direction name the way alone,** "Toward the left side", "Toward the right side", "Not at all", as 18.3 has them. As first written the lab's messages added to each what stands on that side; the card of the equilibrium stands over both forms and says it once, and the three then fit one row. Each of the two that names a side is described, for a reader who is not shown where the card stands, by the card's line for that side: with "Toward the left side" a screen reader is given "Left side: lead ions and chloride ions, dissolved." The third names no side and has no description.
- **The tube is explored.** It is the shared tube of 17.8, with a button laid over each of its two parts, the liquid and under it the bottom: a pointer over a part, keyboard focus on it or a tap looks at it, a callout beside it says what is seen there in words and nothing of what it is, the same words go to a status line for a screen reader, and the look is stored the first time. A part not yet looked at has a dashed edge, a line beside the picture says of each in words whether it has been looked at, and the name of its button says so too. An edge has a light line about it, since the ink it is drawn in does not show on the blue liquid: measured on the picture, the ink is 1.1 to 1 against the blue and the light line 7.1 to 1. The line is the shared tube's, and so every lab's. Each press of the change empties the looks. The picture is drawn 320 px wide where its card is the left column, and 280 px where what goes with it is beside it in a narrow card; a part is then 50 px tall and 66 wide.
- **The one action** is a button beside the picture, named for what it does and how much goes in. For an addition by portions the same button adds the next, the line under it says how many of how many have gone in, and the tube can be looked at after each; once all are in the button carries a tick, and a further press is refused with the reason. A button that cannot be used now stays focusable and says why when pressed.
- **A liquid is told from a solid by more than its colour,** as in 17.8: a liquid under a bracket with the word "liquid", a solid at the bottom with an edge and a grain under a bracket with the word "solid". Systems 1 and 4 turn on whether a white solid is there, and a white solid on a white card under a liquid with no colour is all but one colour with what is about it (1.2 to 1), so it is the edge, the grain and the word that tell it.
- **The colours of the liquids** are the drawing's own values for the record's six words, chosen to differ in how light they are as well as in hue: from the darkest, blue (#1d3ea6), violet (#8f58d1), the colour between blue and yellow (#00ae96), pink (#f8b4ce), yellow (#f6d93f), and the liquid with no colour (#e8edf2). The middle of methyl violet's range has no name in the record: it is drawn half way between the drawing's blue and its yellow in lightness, chroma and hue, a look at it says "between blue and yellow in colour", and the choice a learner records is those words. Measured on the screen, in both themes, each is the value given; as ratios of contrast, the colours one system shows differ by 2.0 to 1 (blue and violet), 2.8 (violet and pink) and 5.4 (blue and pink) for cobalt, and by 1.7 (violet and the colour between), 2.0 (the colour between and yellow) and 3.3 (violet and yellow) for the indicator; the least between any two of the six is 1.2, yellow against pink and against the liquid with no colour. None is left to its colour: the word is said at every look. The tube stands on a white card in both themes.
- **The bath** is drawn behind the tube while the tube stands in one: a beaker about its foot with water in it, bubbles through the water and steam over it and the words "boiling water" beside its rim, or four blocks of ice at the water's surface and the words "ice water". The water is one colour in both. The inside of the glass is drawn white over the bath, so that what is in the tube is judged against white all the same, and the brackets stand clear of the beaker. A tube is still in the bath it was left in when the next disturbance opens, and the line beside the picture then says so; a tube that something is added to is back in the rack, and is drawn with no bath.
- **How high the liquid stands** follows how much is in the tube, as far as its own system fills one, and not to scale: a millilitre or more raises the surface plainly, and a drop or two raises it by a unit or two of the picture, or by none. That a portion went in is said in words.
- **What has gone into the tube, and the tube in words. Other than 17.8 in what the disclosure holds.** What the tube held to begin with and what each disturbance before this one did to it (18.3 does not ask for it) is in a disclosure that is closed, beside the picture, with the description of the tube in words (18.7) under it, which says where the tube stands in the two systems that have a bath. It is closed since it says the whole of what the picture shows.
- **The tubes of system 4.** The card of the tube is headed by the tube's name, which says which metal and whether it is the first tube or the share for acid, for more sodium hydroxide or for ammonia, where the other systems' says "The tube". Under the heading the four tubes of the metal in hand are listed, each by what it is for ("First tube", "For acid", "For more sodium hydroxide", "For ammonia") and as wide as its words, in a list named for the metal ("The four tubes of zinc"): one row where the card is as wide as an upright tablet's, and two in the narrow card of the left column. The learner's own is marked as current: in bold, under a whole edge, behind a pointer. The other tubes are not drawn: the engine hands over what a tube shows only while its disturbance is being worked or once it is done.
- **What is seen** is the shared form of choices, with two questions: the solid in the tube (none, or a white solid) and the colour of the liquid, of six. Until the change is complete and both parts of the tube have been looked at since, the card is one line that says which of the two it waits for. In Guided an observation that is not what the tube shows is not taken, and the form says which part to look at again and never what it should be; one that is taken is said beside a tick to be what the tube showed. In Challenge whatever is recorded is taken and said to be not checked yet, and can be changed until the question is answered. A mode changed to Guided with an observation recorded in Challenge holds that observation to the tube: the form says which part to look at again, and the question under it takes no answer until the observation is what the tube shows.
- **The question of the disturbance** is one question, checked: which way the equilibrium went, or at the four tubes of system 4 that get more sodium hydroxide or ammonia what took the metal ion away, whose three choices name the thing alone ("Hydroxide ions", "Ammonia", "Nothing did") and stand in one row; how the ion is held is the notes' to say. In Guided an answer that is not right is refused with a sentence that says how to look for it and not which answer is right, one sentence for a question of direction and another for what took the ion away. In Challenge the answer is taken, once, and the page says so beside the button. Answering ends the disturbance.
- **Guided's hint** is in a disclosure that is closed, under the question that is open: under the prediction until the change begins, and under the question of the disturbance once what is seen is recorded. Between the two there is none on the page. Challenge has none.
- **A disturbance that is done shows its notes first and outright. Other than 18.3 in where they stand,** which has them after the cards.
