The line that says the disturbance is done, then the notes in a card of their own, over the three cards and not under them: they are what the disturbance is for. Over the notes stand the learner's prediction, as it was made, and the answer, with what a check found of it beside a mark, or that it is not checked yet; a second change in the tube, where the record has one, is under the notes with its own heading. Focus goes to that card when the question is answered. Under it are the equilibrium, the tube as it was left, which can still be looked at and stores nothing, and what was recorded as lines to be read, each with a cross where a check found it not to be what the tube showed. Until the disturbance is done a line at its foot says that its notes come.
- **The entry that closes a run,** which 18.3 does not have: named "This system", with the system's whole name; how the run stands or came out, beside a mark, with each reason a run does not count; the check, in Challenge, unavailable until every disturbance is done, and in Guided only for a run whose last answer was given in Challenge, since Guided checks a system by itself; and "Work this system again", with what it does beside it, unavailable until a run is begun and still taken once the lab is complete. How the run came out takes focus when the check is made. A new run is a column of its own, on its first disturbance, with nothing chosen or said in the one before carried over.
- **Under the ways on, on every entry:** the sources of the disturbance shown, closed, from the start, since no label in the list names what a tube will show; and the bench. The two stand 36 px apart and 28 px under the ways on, as the Group I cations lab's do, so that the hit area a finger tall over each line of words lies over nothing else. The bench is a disclosure that is closed, one place that every entry has, as the Group I cations lab's is: what stands on the bench of the system shown as a list in words, and a bottle's tile with its hazards closed for each of the lab's ten bottles, whichever system is shown, two to a row where the bench has 672 px. A bottle's label carries its formula over its strength; the solid's its formula in two lines; and the indicator's, which the record gives neither, the word for what it is. The water has no tile.
- **Focus** is not left on the page itself. Recording an entry leaves it on the button that then reads "Change"; answering the question puts it on the notes; the check puts it on how the run came out; the systems, the list, Previous and Next and "Work this system again" move it to the heading of the entry shown.
- **Nothing of the lab's own moves.** Its stylesheet has no animation and no transition, and the baths are drawn at rest. The app's buttons keep the short transition of the app's own style, which the reduced-motion setting stops, as on every page.
- **Record tab:** one table of three columns with a group of rows for each system: the system with how far it has come; every run of it in order with how it came out; under a run each disturbance at which something was entered, with the prediction, what was recorded and the answer as lines of one cell, and what the check found of the observation and of the answer, or that the run is not checked yet; and why a run did not count. A prediction is never marked. Once the lab is complete the line that says so is over the table, as it is on the Lab tab.
- **What was made shared, or changed in the shell.** What turns a tube an engine shows into what is drawn of it (how tall the picture is, the two sections that are looked at, where a liquid's surface stands, the colour each word is drawn in) is `src/app/lab/tubeDrawing.ts`, lifted from the Group I cations lab, whose own file hands it on under the same names; that lab's markup is unchanged. The shared tube takes something to draw behind it, with the inside of its glass kept white, and a place for its brackets. The shared form of choices takes a way to show recorded answers as lines beside "Change". No other lab passes any of the three. Three more things of the shell were changed at the review, for every lab: the light line about the edge of a part of the tube, which had been a rule of this lab's stylesheet; a choice of the shared form can be described by something on the page, which this lab alone uses; and an entry of the shared paged list is a finger tall where it is one line with no note under it, as six of this lab's primer's are, which had measured 32 px.
- **What Challenge is kept from.** Before a disturbance is done: its notes and the second change in its tube, in either mode. Before a system is checked, in Challenge: whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, whether an answer is right, and the hint. Before a part of the tube is looked at, in either mode: what is seen there, outside the closed disclosure of the tube in words. Automated tests draw the Lab tab at ninety-three points of a careful run of the four systems in each mode and search its markup for the notes of every disturbance that is not done and for what a look at the tube in hand would say; and draw every entry of a system that holds a wrong observation and a wrong answer, not yet checked, and find no mark on it. What the page stores is the engine's record, which holds what was done and nothing worked out from it. The Primer tab is not kept back in either mode, and gives answers the lab asks for, as 18.6 has it and the research record says.
- **How long a disturbance's page is,** measured in headless Chromium over the seventeen disturbances in both modes, from the line over a disturbance's title to the foot of its last card. The figures are those of the review of 2026-10-11, after its changes. At 768 by 1024 with the menu open, where the page is 497 px wide and one column and 959 px of the window are under the tab bar once it is at the window's top; the systems' buttons and the list's button stand 162 px over a disturbance. For the first three systems: 805 to 895 px before the prediction, with the line under the cards; 689 to 737 with the change made; 919 to 967 with the two questions open; 944 to 966 at the question of the disturbance in Guided and 952 to 974 in Challenge; and 870 to 1,014 done, the notes first. For system 4, whose card of the tube lists four tubes: 835 to 931 before the prediction; 719 to 775 with the change made; 949 to 1,005 with the two questions open; 974 to 1,030 at the question where it asks which way, and 1,014 to 1,042 where it asks what took the metal ion away; and 943 to 1,080 done. So a disturbance of the first three systems is one window of an upright tablet, to within 15 px, at every point of working it, and one of system 4 is up to 83 px longer than that window, at the question of magnesium's two tubes that ask what took the ion away. As first built those pages were up to 1,214 px, 255 px longer than the window: the review named the three choices of that question by the thing alone, so that they stand in one row where they stood in three, listed the metal's tubes in one row where they took three or four lines, and put Challenge's sentence that an answer is given once into one line. With the menu closed, where the page is 649 px wide and two columns: 663 to 737 before the prediction, 576 to 662 with the change made, 747 to 793 with the two questions open, and 892 to 946 at the question for the first three systems and 892 to 978 for system 4. At 1280 by 800, where the page beside the list is 753 px wide and two columns and 735 px are under the tab bar: 604 to 690, 576 to 662, 667 to 773, and 692 to 826 at the question; at 1024 by 768, with 703 px under the bar, the same, measured in Guided.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is chosen and not yet recorded, the system and the entry shown, the callout of a look, whether a disclosure is open, and what the message lines last said. A reload opens on the system whose run is begun and not over, the earlier of two, at the disturbance that run has reached, or at the entry that closes it once its disturbances are done; in a lab that is complete, on a system that is being worked again. The record does not hold which system was shown.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the hand checklist of section 13 was run by script, by keyboard, by mouse and by emulated touch, in headless Chromium, by the AI coding assistant that built the screens and by nobody at a keyboard. `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device. No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer. The one deep review of the lab was held on 2026-10-11, by an AI coding assistant that had built none of it, working by script in headless Chromium and by reading; the last section of `docs/research/lechatelier/README.md` says what it found, changed and left.
- **Where the page is now other than it was first built,** each by the review of 2026-10-11 and each said in its place above: the light line about a part's edge is the shared tube's; a choice that names a side is described by the card's line for that side; the three choices of what took the metal ion away name the thing alone; the metal's four tubes are listed by what each is for, in one row; Challenge's sentence that an answer is given once is one line; a wrong answer to what took the ion away has a refusal of its own; Guided takes no answer while an observation that Challenge recorded is not what the tube shows; the sources and the bench stand apart, since a tap on "Sources" with the bench open under it closed the bench and opened nothing; the number and the progress on the button of the system shown, and "Not checked yet" over a disturbance's notes, are in the text's own colour, since the grey of the page's lesser words measured 4.35 and 4.49 to 1 on the grey grounds they stood on; and an entry of the primer's list is a finger tall.

## 19. Clock reaction lab (added after v1)

The third of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used). It is the first of two labs on how fast a reaction goes; the second, the iodination of acetone, is built next on the same engine. The directions list the bottles; the procedure is the usual one for them, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell, the notebook and typed entries of sections 7 and 16, the graph that is explored of section 16, and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

### 19.1 Set-up

Bromate ions, iodide ions and acid make iodine, slowly. A small, known amount of thiosulfate in the flask takes up the iodine as fast as it forms, and starch is there to show iodine. Nothing is seen until the thiosulfate is used up; then the next iodine meets the starch and the liquid turns blue at once. The time that takes says how fast the reaction was going.

On the bench: potassium iodide solution, 0.010 M; sodium thiosulfate solution, 0.0010 M; potassium bromate solution, 0.040 M; hydrochloric acid, 0.10 M; starch solution; distilled water; two flasks, one for each half of a mixture; a stopwatch; a thermometer; baths of ice water, cool water and warm water.

Three parts:

1. **Five mixtures at room temperature.** Each differs from the first in the amount of one thing, or of all, and is timed.
2. **The rate law.** From the five times: the rate of each run, the order in each of the three reactants, and the rate constant.
3. **Temperature.** The first mixture again at three other temperatures; from the four rate constants, the activation energy.

### 19.2 A run

The bench sets out the mixture: so many millilitres of each solution in each flask, 50 mL in all. A run is a short column of cards:

1. **The mixture**, as a table.
2. **The flasks**, drawn, with what has been done in words. Starch is added to one flask. In part 3 both flasks stand in a bath, and the thermometer is read, before they are mixed. Then one flask is poured into the other and the watch started, as one action.
3. **The watch and the liquid.** The watch runs and the liquid is clear and colourless until, all at once, it is blue. The learner stops the watch. The page plays the time faster than a clock would; the engine reads no clock and is handed the time. The time recorded is the time the model gives with the learner's own delay added, in true seconds, as a hand on a real watch adds it. A learner may instead have the watch stopped at the change: that run counts as any other, and the record says which way it was timed.

Two errors of technique, each real: **no starch**, so that nothing ever shows; and, in part 3, **mixing before the flasks have come to the bath's temperature**, so that the time belongs to no one temperature. Guided warns before the pour; a run made so does not count and says why.

A run can be made again; the times of the runs that count fill a table that the other two parts work from.

### 19.3 The notebook

The arithmetic is done once by hand and then by the notebook, which says that it is doing so: a learner who has worked one row is not made to work nine.

- **Part 2.** For the first run the learner works out the concentration of each reactant once the two flasks are mixed, and the rate, which is the bromate used up by the time of the change, a sixth of the thiosulfate, over the time. The notebook then fills the same four figures for the other runs. The learner gives the order in each reactant, a choice of 0, 1 or 2, from the two runs the question names; then the rate constant of the first run, with its unit chosen, and the notebook fills the rest and their mean.
- **Part 3.** The notebook fills each temperature's rate constant, its natural logarithm and one over the temperature in kelvin. A graph of the logarithm against one over the temperature, with the straight line through the points, is explored as section 16's is; the learner reads two places on the line, works out its slope, and from it the activation energy.

Entries are typed and checked as section 16's are: each must follow from the learner's own earlier entries, rounded as the primer says; Guided checks each as it is made and has its relation in a closed hint; Challenge checks them together.

A part counts when its runs count, its entries follow, and its results (the three orders, the rate constant, the activation energy) are within tolerance of what the model gives for those runs. The lab is complete when all three parts count.

### 19.4 Model

The engine is pure and deterministic and holds whole numbers. It is not this lab's alone: it takes a reaction's record (the rate law's orders, its rate constant at one temperature, its activation energy, and what ends a run) and the iodination of acetone will be a second record for it.

The rate is the rate constant times each concentration raised to its order; the rate constant follows the Arrhenius equation; the time to the change is the bromate that must be used up over the rate, with the concentrations taken as they start, which is the same idealisation the learner's arithmetic makes and is said as one. Room temperature, the baths' temperatures and a small scatter in each time are seeded, the scatter no larger than a bench's, and such that at every seed the orders come out unmistakably and a careful learner's rate constant and activation energy are within tolerance.

The orders, the rate constant and the activation energy are cited where a source that can be read gives them. A figure that cannot be sourced is a model parameter with its rationale, chosen to give the times this experiment is known for, and the lab says of it that it is the lab's own.

### 19.5 Data

The overall reaction and the reaction of iodine with thiosulfate, with the six-to-one count between them; the rate law; molar masses where a note needs them; what the liquid looks like before and after. Hazards by 5.2 for every bottle: potassium bromate above all.

### 19.6 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: how fast (a rate, and its unit); what a rate depends on, and what a rate law and an order are; the clock (thiosulfate, starch, and why the change is sudden); finding an order by comparing two runs; the rate constant and its unit; temperature and the activation energy; reading the graph of part 3; safety.

### 19.7 Acceptance

- For seeds 1 to 100, careful runs of the five mixtures and the three temperatures all count; the orders that follow from them are the stored ones; the rate constant and the activation energy worked from them are within tolerance of the stored values.
- Each error of technique alone makes a run not count, and its cause is named; Guided warns first.
- An entry that does not follow from the learner's own numbers is refused or marked.
- The same seed, actions and times handed in give the same lab; the stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the entries are made; the notebook fills a row only after the learner has worked the row it copies.
- The engine holds nothing of this reaction that is not in its record: a test runs it on a second, made-up record with other orders.
- The descriptions in words of the flasks and of the graph are tested.

### 19.8 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-11, straight through, as the labs before it were (section 13), on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8, 15, 16, 17 and 18. The route is `#/lab/clock-reaction`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it, as "Clock reaction", with its status. Where the page is other than 19.2 and 19.3 have it, or other than the calorimetry lab's page (16.7), which is the nearest to it in kind, the point below says so in its first words.

- **Lab tab: three parts, and one thing at a time.** A navigation named Parts has a button for each part, three to a row at every width, with its number, its name and how far it has come in words beside a mark; the one shown is marked as current. **Other than 16.7,** whose parts are the entries of the shared paged list: here the paged list is inside a part. Of part 1 it has the five runs, each with how it stands in words beside a mark (not started, flasks being made ready, watch running, did not count, counts); of part 3 the three runs in their baths and, after them, the notebook, so the list named Runs ends in an entry that is not a run. Part 2 is one thing, its notebook, and has no list. One entry is shown at a time; Previous and Next are named for where they go; at the last run of part 1 a further way on goes to part 2, and under part 2 are the ways to the parts either side. The list stays behind its button until the page has 928 px, as the flame test lab's does. Until a part is begun a line under the navigation says what it finds and what makes it done; from then a status line says how many of its runs count.
- **A run before its pour is two cards, the mixture and the flasks. Other than 19.2,** which has a third, the watch and the liquid, from the start: a card not yet come is not drawn. The mixture is a table of three columns, each flask heading its own liquids, with what the two come to under it. The flasks are drawn side by side, each named, on a white card in both themes; beside the drawing a status line says in words where they stand and what has been done to them, and a second line what the liquid in them looks like. Under it are the buttons, each a finger tall: the starch, the pour, which is drawn as the main one, and beside the pour, since the review, the choice to have the watch stopped at the change (below), so that it can be made before the first watch is started. A step that is taken reads as done and carries a tick; one that cannot be taken now stays focusable and says why when pressed.
- **A run of part 3** has, between the starch and the pour, the three steps of its bath: stand both flasks in it, let them stand, read the thermometer. The pour is unavailable until the thermometer is read, as the engine has it. **Other than a run of part 1,** its mixture, which is the first mixture again, is behind a disclosure that is closed, so that the five buttons have the room. Once the flasks stand in the bath the drawing shows a trough about their feet, the thermometer in flask A, and what it reads once it has been read.
- **The baths are told apart without colour:** blocks of ice at the surface of the ice water, steam over the warm water, neither for the cool water, and each bath's own name under the trough. The water is one colour in all three.
- **Guided's warning** is the shared group of 16.7, raised in the flasks' card by a pour with no starch or with flasks that have not stood: it takes focus, the way back puts focus on the step that was skipped, and the way on pours.
- **From the pour the run is one card, the watch and the liquid. Other than 19.2's column of three.** The mixture and what was done to the flasks are behind two disclosures that are closed, under the card, 36 px apart (the review, below). The card holds the one flask that now holds the mixture, drawn larger (192 px wide at every width), with the look of its liquid in the record's words under it; beside it the watch, as figures 48 px tall in a frame, in minutes, seconds and tenths; the line that says the page plays the time faster than a clock would, and how: at one speed for all five mixtures in a run of part 1, and each bath at its own pace in a run of part 3; the look of the liquid as a sentence; and a status line that is empty until the liquid changes. Under them are the two ways of stopping the watch, side by side, each 56 px tall or more, and under those what each does.
- **How the time is played.** The page owns the clock and the engine reads none (`src/app/lab/clock/watch.ts`, which reads no clock either: the true time of each frame is handed to it, and it is tested without one). At the pour the page notes the true time. At every frame it works out what the watch reads in the lab's time, asks the engine what the liquid looks like at that time and what the watch shows, and draws both. Until the liquid changes, the lab's time runs faster than a clock's. **Since the review the five mixtures of part 1 are played at one speed,** 14 seconds of the lab's time to each true second, where each had a speed of its own (13, 7, 7, 5 and 14) that made all five take about as long, and so hid what the lab is about. Now a time twice as long on the watch is twice as long to wait for: over seeds 1 to 100 the first mixture changes 7.9 to 10.5 true seconds after its pour, the second 4.0 to 5.2, the third 3.9 to 5.2, the fourth, with twice the acid, 2.0 to 2.6, and the fifth 8.8 to 11.7; at any room and scatter the draw can give, the fifth changes within 11.8. A test holds the one speed, the slowest within 12 true seconds, the fastest in two to three, and the fourth in a quarter of the first's true time. Each bath of part 3 keeps a speed of its own, since ten minutes and forty seconds cannot both be waited for at one: 5 for the warm bath, 30 for the cool and 55 for the ice, so that each changes between 5 and 12 true seconds after its pour at every seed from 1 to 100, which a test holds; the line beside the watch says that each bath is played at its own pace. The figures are the page's own and are not shown. A run mixed before its flasks had stood changes within 20 true seconds, which a test holds too.
- **At the change the watch goes over to true time. Other than 19.2 in what the running watch shows,** which says only that the time is played faster. In the frame that first finds the liquid changed, the page finds the time of the change to the millisecond between that frame and the one before, the watch reads that time, and the true time of the frame is noted. From then on the watch adds true time, a second to a second, and the line about the faster time is no longer shown. So what the watch reads when a hand stops it is the time of the change with the hand's own delay added in true seconds, which is the time the engine records, and the stopped watch reads what the running one read. On Stop the page hands the engine what the watch read and how many true milliseconds had passed since the frame that showed the change; 0 where the liquid had not changed.
- **A watch in whose flask nothing will show** is not waited for at the run's speed. After 20 true seconds the watch runs on to its limit of 30 minutes in 4 more, and the page then stops it, as a hand would at that time, or at the change itself where that was asked for. The run does not count, and says why.
- **The second way of stopping,** "Stop the watch for me at the change", is beside the pour before a watch is started, since the review, and beside Stop from the pour on. Pressed before the pour or before the change, it stays pressed and the watch goes on: the wait and the change are still seen, and the page stops the watch at the change. Pressed once the liquid has changed, it stops the watch at the change at once. It stays pressed for the runs that follow, until it is pressed again, so a learner who cannot or would rather not time by eye says so once; as first built a press after the change stopped that run's watch and did not stay pressed, which the review put right. **Other than 19.2 only in that it is a choice that is kept.** A run timed so counts as any other, and the table and the record say how each run was timed.
- **What is seen and heard at the change.** The liquid is drawn in its new colour in the frame that finds it changed, with no fading; the words under the flask and the sentence beside it change with it; the status line says once that the mixture has turned dark blue, all at once; and, where the reduced-motion setting allows, the edge of the picture is marked for 0.7 s, once, which is the lab's one animation. With motion reduced nothing moves; the figures of the watch still change. The liquid has no transition of its own: with motion reduced the app gives every transition a hundredth of a millisecond, which held the new colour back by a frame until the review. The watch is a timer and not a live region: one that spoke its figures would speak over the change. From the pour, focus is on Stop, so a key stops the watch as a finger does. **For the first half second after a pour the bench takes no press,** since the review: a second press of the key that poured landed on Stop and ended the run before its change, and at 768 by 1024 the watch's card brings Stop, the other way of stopping and a disclosure to where the pour stood. A press is judged by when it was made, as the browser has it, and not by when the page came to it. No run changes within three times that.
- **A run that is left with its watch running is poured again. Other than the engine's own order,** in which the pour is an action of its own: the page keeps the pour to itself while it plays the watch, and hands the engine the pour and the stop together when the watch is stopped. Until then nothing of the pour is stored. Going to another run or part, to another tab of the page, or to another tab of the browser while a watch runs, ends the play; the flasks are then as they were before the pour, starch and bath and thermometer as they were, and the flasks' card says that the run was left and that no time was recorded. A reload does the same without the sentence. No watch runs unseen, and no time is recorded that the learner did not see run. A stored record that is poured with no stop, which this page never writes, is drawn with its watch at nothing and not running: stopping it ends the run, which does not count and is made again.
- **Once the watch is stopped** the card says what it reads and how it was stopped, the liquid is drawn as it was at the stop, and a sentence beside a mark says that the run counts, with the time that goes into the table, or that it does not and why. That sentence takes focus. "Make this run again", with what it does beside it, is there for a run that did not count, and puts focus on the run's heading. **Other than 19.2 and 16.7,** a run that counts has no such button: the engine does not take it (the research record says why).
- **Part 1's notes,** which no notebook carries, are in a disclosure that is closed under the count of its runs, once all five count and not before.
- **The table of times** is a card at the head of part 2, with the five runs, and at the head of part 3's notebook, with the four runs that notebook is worked from; under every run it is behind a disclosure that is closed, with the sources and the bench. Each row has the run, its temperature, its time once it counts, and whether its watch was stopped by hand or at the change.
- **Part 2's notebook is laid out in the order it is worked, with what each stretch is worked from just over it. Other than 16.7's one list under one box.** First a box with the first mixture's own table and, under it, the one line of the table of times that the row is worked from; then entries 1 to 4, the three concentrations and the rate. Then, only once the engine says that row has been worked, a box with the sentence that says the notebook has worked the other four runs and the table of all five: time, the three concentrations and the rate. Under it entries 5 to 7, the three orders. Then entries 8 and 9, the rate constant and its unit; and, only once both are entered, a box with the sentence that says the notebook has worked the other four rate constants, and those with their mean, the first mixture's marked as the learner's own. The three stretches are three lists whose numbers run on.
- **Entries** are typed and checked as 16.7's are, and the typed entry is now one piece of the shell that both labs draw: a field a finger tall, how the entry is written under it, a refusal on that line, the relation in a closed disclosure in Guided only, an entry that is made shown in its disabled field beside "Change", and the calculator over the list. The orders, the unit and the two places are choices in the shared form. **Other than 16.7,** a choice that is made folds to one line beside "Change", as 18.8's do, and its boxes come back at Change, so that the entries under it are not pushed down the page. In Challenge "Check the notebook" is under the list. Guided checks a notebook at its last entry and has no check to press, but for a notebook whose every entry was made in Challenge and not checked before the mode was changed: since the review the check is offered there, where there was nothing to press.
- **Part 3's notebook.** A card named for the graph holds the sentence that says the notebook filled the table, the table (each run's temperature, the same in kelvin, one over it, its rate constant and the logarithm), and under it the graph, explored as 16.7's is: a button over each of the twelve places on the line, a column the plot's whole height; a pointer over one, focus on it or a tap looks at it, rings it and shows its two figures in a callout at the picture's head; one stop of the Tab key, the arrow keys and Home and End moving along the line; two buttons a finger tall under the picture that step one place to the left and to the right; a key in words; and the graph in words behind a disclosure that is closed. A run's point is a diamond and a place a circle, filled once it has been looked at. **Other than 16.7,** the runs' points are not looked at: their figures are in the table over the graph. The notebook's own card is under the picture: the two places, each a choice among the places that have been looked at (the second among the others), which waits and says so until there is one to choose; the slope; and the activation energy.
- **How a notebook came out** is a card of its own once the notebook is checked, which takes focus: the sentence beside a mark, "Work this notebook again" where it did not count, and, once the part is done and not before, a disclosure that is closed with what the result was held to and the record's notes. For part 3 that is the sentence that says the activation energy is the lab's own figure and not a measured one.
- **Under the ways on, on every entry:** 36 px apart since the review, so that the hit area of none lies over another, the sources of the part, closed, and the bench: what stands on it as a list in words, with what the thermometer reads in the room, and a bottle's tile with its hazards closed for each of the five bottles, whichever part is shown. **Other than 16.7,** which has a bench's card in each part.
- **Record tab:** one table with a group of rows for each part: the part with how far it has come; every attempt at each of its runs in order, with what the watch read and how it was stopped (by hand, with how many seconds after the change where the liquid had changed; at the change; by hand alone where there was no change to be late for; or by itself, at its limit, which the record gave as stopped by hand until the review), how it came out, and why where it did not count; and every working of its notebook with what was entered. It shows nothing of what a result is held to.
- **What was made shared.** `src/app/lab/TypedEntry.tsx`, lifted from the calorimetry lab's worksheet, which now draws it with its own words; and `src/app/lab/exploreKeys.ts`, where a key and a step button of an explored picture move to, which the calorimetry lab's own file hands on under the same names. That lab's markup is unchanged, which its own tests hold. Nothing else of the shell is changed.
- **What Challenge is kept from.** Before a part is done: what its result is held to and its notes, in either mode. Before a notebook is checked, in Challenge: whether an entry follows, and the relations. Before a watch is stopped, in either mode: the time of the change. Automated tests draw the Lab tab at every point of a careful Challenge lab, after each run and after each entry of both notebooks, and search its markup for the model's orders and rate constant as the page words them, for the sentence that gives the activation energy, and for every note; none is there before its part is done. The page reads the engine through its one door, which hands a held value over only once its part is done. The speed a run is played at is not shown. Since the review a run of part 1 takes on the screen what it takes on the watch, at one scale, which the watch itself shows from the pour; a bath's wait says nothing of its time, and the page says so.
- **How long things are,** measured in headless Chromium, from the line over an entry's title to the foot of its last card. At 768 by 1024 with the menu open, where the page is 497 px wide and 959 px of the window are under the tab bar: the parts, the count of runs and the list's button stand 194 px over a run, and 270 px on the first screen of a lab that is not begun, which also says what the lab and the part are for. A run of part 1 before its pour is 744 px since the review, where it was 694: the choice to have the watch stopped at the change is a third row of buttons. Its foot is at 938 px of the 959, and at 1,014 on that first screen, where that third row is partly under the window's foot until the first press shortens what stands over the run; with Guided's warning showing it is 871 px. With the watch running it is 517 px, and stopped 385. A run of part 3 is 544 px before anything is done and 594 ready to pour. So a run is one window of an upright tablet at every point but the first screen, which is 55 px over, and the warning, 106 px over. At 1280 by 800, where the list is beside the run and 735 px are under the bar: 667 px before the pour with 126 over it, which is 58 px more than the window, so the third row is under its foot; 517 with the watch running; 482 for a bath's run ready to pour. Part 2's page is 1,220 px when its notebook opens and 2,021 once it is done; part 3's notebook's page is 1,725 px, of which the graph's card is 1,000. No entry needs a figure that is not in one window with it: at 768 by 1024 the first mixture's table begins 267 px over entry 1 and 591 over entry 4; the filled table ends 21 px over entry 5 and begins 591 over entry 8; the first of part 3's entries is 253 px under the foot of the graph, and the slope has its two places in the two lines over it.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is typed and not yet entered, the part and the entry shown, a watch that is running, whether the watch is to be stopped at the change, the place on the line last looked at, whether a disclosure is open, and what the message lines last said. A reload opens on the first part that is begun and not done, at the first of its runs that does not count yet, or at part 3's notebook once its runs count. A notebook stays drawn once it has been shown, hidden while something else is, so that what was typed in it is kept.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the checks were run by script, by keyboard, by mouse and by emulated touch, in headless Chromium, by the AI coding assistant that built the screens and by nobody at a keyboard. `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device. No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer.
- **The review** was held on 2026-10-11 by an AI coding assistant that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium and by reading; the last section of the lab's research record says what it ran, measured, found and left. Where the page is other than it was first built, each point above says so: the one speed of part 1 and the line that says how the time is played; the choice to have the watch stopped, beside the pour, and pressed for good whenever it is pressed; the half second after a pour in which the bench takes no press; the disclosures 36 px apart; the record's word for a watch that stopped itself; Guided's check for a notebook filled in Challenge; and the liquid with no transition. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device, and no chemist or chemistry instructor has read any of it.
- **Changed on 2026-10-11, with the review of the iodination of acetone lab,** in the pieces the two labs share (20.6). A notebook that is worked again gives focus to its heading, where focus was lost to the page with the card that held the button. Once part 1 is done its notes have 8 px more under them, clear of the button of the list of runs at 768 by 1024. The two small lines of the part that is shown, and of one the pointer is over, are in the page's own ink, where grey on the grey ground measured 4.35 to 1 in the light theme. The delay handed to the engine is never more than the watch had left to run at the change, so a stopped watch cannot read more than its limit. On that build the review worked one run of each part and each notebook of this lab, at 768 by 1024, by script in headless Chromium: the five mixtures at one speed (10.35 true seconds from the press that poured to the change for a first mixture whose change the watch had at 144.4 s, which is 10.32 at 14 to 1), a bath's run with the line that says each bath is played at its own pace, the choice to have the watch stopped beside the first pour, a second Enter just after the pour not taken, and the relation of a chosen entry 28 px under Enter with no row of either answered by the other, closed or open, which the change of 20.6 had not been measured for on this page. Nothing else of this lab's page was changed.

## 20. Iodination of acetone lab (added after v1)

The fourth of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used), and the second of the two on how fast a reaction goes. It runs on the engine of section 19, as a second record, and is built like that lab wherever the two do not differ. The directions list the bottles; the procedure is the usual one for them, and each choice in it is the owner's to change.

### 20.1 Set-up

Acetone and iodine react in acid: an iodine atom takes the place of a hydrogen atom of the acetone. Iodine in solution is yellow and nothing else in the flask is coloured, so the reaction can be watched: the yellow fades, and the run ends when it is gone. The time that takes, for a known amount of iodine, is the rate.

What is found is the point of the lab: the rate depends on the acetone and on the acid, and not at all on the iodine. Twice the iodine takes twice as long to go, at the same rate.

On the bench: acetone solution, 4.0 M; hydrochloric acid, 1.0 M; iodine solution, 0.0050 M; distilled water; a flask for the mixture and a second flask of water to compare it with, on white paper; a stopwatch; a thermometer.

Two parts:

1. **Four mixtures at room temperature**, of 50 mL each: the first with 10 mL of each solution and 20 mL of water, and three that double one solution each, with less water. Each is timed.
2. **The rate law.** From the four times: the rate of each run, the order in acetone, in hydrogen ion and in iodine, and the rate constant.

There is no part on temperature: the directions set out no baths for this lab.

### 20.2 A run

As 19.2, with these differences. Nothing is added to show the end: iodine is its own colour. The acetone, the acid and the water are in the flask and the iodine is poured in and the watch started, as one action. The liquid is yellow and fades steadily, and the learner stops the watch when it cannot be told from the water in the flask beside it.

The drawing keeps a faint yellow to the very end and then shows none, so that the end can be seen on a screen; on a bench the last of the colour is hard to see, which is what the second flask and the white paper are for. The primer says this of the drawing. What the liquid looks like is also said in words at any moment, and the change is announced, so that colour is not the only signal. As in 19.2, a learner may have the watch stopped at the change.

There is no error of technique that the bench can make here beyond the watch itself: a run stopped before the colour is gone, or long after, does not count and says why.

### 20.3 The notebook

As 19.3's part 2. The rate of a run is the iodine it began with, in the mixture, over the time: all of the iodine is used up, so nothing is divided by six. The learner works the first run's three concentrations and its rate by hand and the notebook fills the rest; gives the three orders, each from the two runs its question names; then the rate constant of the first run with its unit, and the notebook fills the others and their mean.

The lab is complete when both parts count.

### 20.4 Model

Section 19's engine, given this reaction's record: orders one in acetone, one in hydrogen ion and nought in iodine; a rate constant at one temperature and an activation energy, each cited where a source that can be read gives it and otherwise the lab's own figure, said to be so; a run that ends when the iodine is used up. The engine is made to take a record with no baths and so no part on temperature, and to say how much of the colour is left at a time handed in, for the drawing. The rate is steady from the mixing to the end, which for this reaction is what the rate law itself says while the acetone and the acid are in large excess; how nearly so, by number, is in the notes.

### 20.5 Data, primer, acceptance

Data: the reaction; the rate law; what the liquid looks like before and after. Hazards by 5.2: acetone above all, which burns; and what the sheets say of the product, iodoacetone, for the primer's safety topic.

Primer: the topics of 19.6 without the clock and without temperature, and with two of its own: watching a colour go; and an order of nought, and what it says about how the reaction happens (the slow step does not involve iodine), from a source that can be cited.

Acceptance: 19.7's lines, for four mixtures and no baths; and the engine's own tests still pass for the clock reaction's record, whose output is unchanged.

### 20.6 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-11, straight through, as the labs before it were (section 13), on the pieces of the clock reaction lab's page (19.8), which were first lifted out of that lab's folder so that both labs draw them. The route is `#/lab/iodination-of-acetone`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it, as "Iodination of acetone", with its status. Where the page is other than 20.2 and 20.3 have it, or other than the clock reaction lab's page, the point below says so in its first words. The one deep review was held on 2026-10-11 (section 14), and the points below give the page as the review left it; the last of them lists where that is other than it was first built.

- **What the two labs share** is in `src/app/lab/kinetics/`, and each piece takes the lab as one argument: its own door to the engine, its model, its parts and its wording. `play.ts`, the playing of a run's time, which reads no clock. `Watch.tsx`: the head of a run, the watch's card, and the button that has the watch stopped for the learner; the lab hands in the picture of the liquid, its sentence and what the status line says. `tables.tsx`: a plain table, a mixture's table and the table of times. `Notebook.tsx`: the entries, the check, the card that says how a notebook came out, and the notebook on the rate law. `Record.tsx`, the Record tab. `Bench.tsx`, the Lab tab: the navigation of the parts, one thing at a time, the play of a watch, the half second after a pour, and the sources and the bench under every entry. `Page.tsx`, which reads and saves the stored lab. `Glass.tsx`, a flask with a liquid in it. `text.ts`, how a temperature, a time, a volume and the units are written. No shared piece imports a function of either lab's engine door, and none names a reaction, a bottle or a colour. The clock reaction lab's markup was compared before the lifting and after it, 365 drawings of its page in 28 states, and was the same byte for byte; its own tests pass with no line of them changed.
- **What is this lab's own** is in `src/app/lab/acetone/`: the card of a run with its flask and its fade (`RunCard.tsx`), the picture and its colours (`FlaskDrawing.tsx`), the one speed (`watch.ts`), the lab as the shared pieces take it (`lab.ts`), and the wording (`messages.ts`).
- **Lab tab: two parts, side by side, and one thing at a time.** Part 1 has the four runs in the paged list, each with how it stands in words beside a mark; at the last a further way on goes to part 2. Part 2 is one thing, its notebook, with the way back to part 1 under it. As 19.8 in everything else: what a part is for until it is begun, then how many of its runs count; part 1's notes in a closed disclosure once its four runs count and not before.
- **A run before its pour is two cards, the mixture and the flask. Other than 19.2,** as the clock reaction lab's page is: the watch's card comes with the pour. The mixture is a table in two groups, what is in the flask and what is poured in to start, with what they come to under it. The flask and the flask of water are drawn side by side, small (224 px wide), each named, both with no colour, on a white card in both themes; beside the drawing are the sentence that says how the two stand and the sentence that says what the liquid looks like. Under them, side by side, each a finger tall: the pour, which pours the iodine in and starts the watch as one action and is drawn as the main button, and the choice to have the watch stopped when the colour has gone. **Other than the clock reaction lab's page,** there is no step before the pour and no warning: there is nothing to make ready, and the engine has nothing for Guided to warn of here.
- **From the pour the run is one card, the watch and the liquid,** with the mixture and the flasks in words behind two disclosures that are closed, 36 px apart. **Other than the clock reaction lab's page, the picture holds two flasks,** the mixture's and the water's, each 136 px wide in a picture 320 px wide at every width, each with its name and the lab's words for its look under it. At 768 by 1024 the watch stands under the picture, and at 1280 by 800 beside it. The watch, the line on the speed, the look of the liquid as a sentence, the status line and the two ways of stopping are the shared card's, as 19.8 has them. The line on the speed says what the speed is: "One second here is one minute on the watch, for all four mixtures." **Other than the clock reaction lab's page, the lines keep their room while the watch runs.** At 768 by 1024 the watch and its lines stand under the picture and over the two buttons, so a line that came, went or grew moved Stop under a hand that waited on it: as first built, 40 px down when the first step of the fade was said and the look ran on to a second line, and 8 px up at the end. The look now keeps the room of two lines and the status line of one from the pour, and the line on the speed keeps its room, unseen, once the colour has gone. Measured at the review, Stop's upper edge was at one height from the pour to the stop, at both sizes. It is an option of the shared card that this lab asks for; the clock reaction lab's watch stands beside its picture at both sizes, and Stop did not move there.
- **The fade.** At every frame the page asks the engine how much iodine is left at the time on the watch and draws the mixture in the yellow of that amount: a straight line in the three colour channels from #d9a000 for the most iodine any mixture starts with, 2000 micromoles for a litre, to #f2e596 for the least there can be, and at nought the water's own #e8edf2. So the first three mixtures start at about #e6c34b and the fourth, with twice the iodine, deeper; a faint yellow is kept for any amount above nought, and the end is a step that can be seen, as 20.2 has it. The water is drawn the same from the first frame to the last. Measured from pictures of the screen in headless Chromium, in both themes, since the picture stands on its own white card in both: 6 true milliseconds or less before the end the mixture was drawn in #f2e596 and the water in #e8edf2, which differ by 43.6 in CIELAB (the 1976 colour difference) and stand at 1.09 to 1 as a ratio of luminances; the difference is one of hue and not of light and dark. Colour is not the only signal: the lab's word for the look is under each flask in the picture and in a sentence beside the watch, at every frame, and the status line says the end, "The last of the yellow has gone." Before the end it says a step down in those words only where the end is still 1.8 true seconds off or more when the step comes (`SAY_MS` in `watch.ts`): a status line's sentences are said to a listener one after another, and a step said just before the end would hold the end back, which is what the watch is stopped by. At the one speed pale yellow is said in the first and the fourth mixture, where it comes 2.0 to 3.3 true seconds before the end; in the second and the third, where it comes 1.0 to 1.6 seconds before, the end alone is said; and the faintest yellow, which comes 0.2 to 0.7 seconds before the end in every mixture, is said in none. A test holds that at every seed from 1 to 100. As first built the line said both steps and the end in every mixture, three sentences within two or three seconds in the two fast ones, and the end in thirteen words. The 1.8 seconds is the page's own figure, and no screen reader was listened to in choosing it. **Other than the clock reaction lab's page, nothing is animated,** with motion reduced or not: the edge of the picture is not marked at the end, and the liquid has no transition, so its colour is each frame's own.
- **How the time is played.** As 19.8, by the shared piece. **The four mixtures are played at one speed,** 60 seconds of the lab's time to each true second: a minute on the watch to a second, which the line beside the running watch says. As first built the speed was 56 and the page did not give it; the owner decided on 60 at the review. A time twice as long on the watch is twice as long to wait for. Over seeds 1 to 100 the first mixture has faded 4.01 to 6.48 true seconds after its pour, the second 2.01 to 3.27, the third 1.99 to 3.26, and the fourth, with twice the iodine, 7.98 to 12.96; at any room and scatter the draw can give, the fourth within 13.1 and none in less than 1.99. A test holds the one speed, those eight figures, the fourth in twice the first's true time and the second and third in half of it, and that there is iodine left at every frame before the one that shows the end and none from it on. From the frame that shows the end the watch adds true time, the stopped watch reads what the running one read, and the delay handed to the engine is the true milliseconds from that frame to the press, and never more than the watch had left to run then; a watch stopped more than 5.0 seconds after the end does not count, as the engine has it. For the first half second after a pour the bench takes no press; no run ends within three times that. Measured in headless Chromium at the review, by the page's own clock, from just before the press that poured to the frame whose status line first said the end: 5.72 seconds for a first mixture whose end the watch had at 341.3 s, which is 5.69 at the one speed, and 11.40 for a fourth whose end it had at 682.2 s, which is 11.37.
- **A watch that is left running after the end** adds true time until it reads its limit of 30 minutes and then stops itself; the run does not count, for a watch stopped late. The stopped watch then reads 30:00.0 whenever the frame that finds the limit comes: as first built, a frame that came long after the one before it, as when a computer has slept with the page showing, handed the engine a delay longer than the watch had left, and the card gave a time past the limit (31:52.2 in the review's run, with a clock the script held). Every run of this lab ends, so no watch is run on to its limit as 19.8 has for a flask with nothing to show the change.
- **The second way of stopping,** "Stop the watch for me when the colour has gone", is beside the pour and beside Stop, stays pressed for the runs that follow, and stops the watch at the end itself, as 19.8 has it. **Other than 20.2 only in that it is a choice that is kept.**
- **A run that is left with its watch running is poured again,** with nothing stored and a sentence that says so; a reload does the same without the sentence. Once the watch is stopped the card says what it reads and how it was stopped, the mixture is drawn as it was at the stop, and a sentence beside a mark, which takes focus, says that the run counts or that it does not and why. "Make this run again" is there for a run that did not count. **Other than 20.2,** a run that counts has no such button, as in 19.8.
- **Part 2** is the shared notebook under the table of times: the first mixture's table and its line of the table of times, entries 1 to 4 (the three concentrations and the rate), the table the notebook fills for the other three runs, entries 5 to 7 (the three orders), entries 8 and 9 (the rate constant and its unit), the rate constants the notebook fills with their mean, and how the notebook came out. **Other than 20.3,** the unit of the rate constant is an entry of its own, as the research record has it.
- **One thing was changed in the shared notebook for both labs.** Under an order or a unit that waits in Guided, the disclosure with the relation stood 18 px under the form's Enter. Open, its hit area reaches 24 px over it, and lay over the lowest 6 px of Enter, so that a press there closed the relation and entered nothing. It now stands 28 px under. The clock reaction lab's page was built with the same 18 px, and its markup now differs from what its review left in that one class.
- **Hazards and the bench:** under the ways on of every run and of the notebook, 36 px apart, the table of times where the entry is a run, the sources of the part, and the bench, each closed: what stands on the bench in words, with what the thermometer reads in the room, and a tile for each of the three bottles with its strength as its label writes it and its hazards closed.
- **Record tab:** the shared record: the two parts with how far each has come, every attempt at each run with what the watch read and how it was stopped (by hand, with how many seconds after the colour had gone; when the colour had gone; by hand alone where there was still colour; or by itself, at its limit), how it came out and why, and every working of the notebook with what was entered. It shows nothing of what a result is held to.
- **What Challenge is kept from.** As 19.8. Automated tests draw the Lab tab and the record at fifteen points of a careful Challenge lab, after each run and after each entry, and search their markup for the model's orders and rate constant as the page words them and for every note; none is there before its part is done. The speed is said beside the running watch, a minute to a second, and is the same for every mixture: a run takes on the screen what it takes on the watch at that one scale, which the watch itself shows from the pour. At the review the text of the Lab tab and of the Record tab, with what is closed or hidden in them, was searched at 21 points of a Challenge lab worked by taps, and nothing of what Challenge is kept from was there before its part was done.
- **How long things are,** measured in headless Chromium, from the line over an entry's title to the foot of its last card. At 768 by 1024 with the menu open, where the page is 497 px wide and 959 px of the window are under the tab bar: a run before its pour is 705 px, with 194 px over it, so its foot is at 899; on the first screen of a lab that is not begun, 270 px are over it and its foot is at 975, 16 px under the window's, which is the card's own edge and no button. With the watch running it is 743 px from the pour to the stop, its lines keeping their room, so its foot is at 937; stopped, 571. (As first built: 723, then 763 once the status line had a step to say, and 591 stopped.) At 1280 by 800, where 735 px are under the bar and 126 px over a run: 598 px before the pour, so its foot is at 724, and at 800 on the first screen, where the two buttons are under the window's foot until the first press; 519 with the watch running; 387 stopped. Part 2's page is 1,815 px when the first mixture's row has been worked and 1,885 once it is done, at 768 by 1024.
- **Every control asked point by point.** At 768 by 1024 and at 1280 by 800, in the light theme, a script asked the page what answers at the middle of each control and 21 px from it each way: in a run before its pour, with the watch running, with it stopped, in the notebook with a typed entry and with a choice waiting, and on the Record and Primer tabs, with every disclosure closed and with each open in turn, 27 states at the first size and 23 at the second. Every control answered for itself, after the change to the shared notebook above. A choice was asked at its label; an open disclosure's summary was not asked under its middle, where its hit area does not reach by design; the links in a list of sources are lines of text and were not asked. The review asked again on the build it left, in those states and in 59 more at each size: a run that did not count, with each bottle's hazards and their sources open in turn inside the bench; a choice and the unit waiting in Guided; Challenge's notebook empty, with a choice waiting, full, and checked and not counting; the notebook done with its notes open; part 1 done; and each of the primer's seven cards. It found one fault, at 768 by 1024, in the shared bench: once part 1 is done its notes stand over the button of the list of runs, and a point 21 px under the middle of the notes' line was answered by that button. The notes now have 8 px more under them, and every control answered for itself in every state at both sizes.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** as 19.8, without the graph. A reload opens on the first part that is begun and not done: part 1 at the first of its runs that does not count yet, and part 2 once the four runs count.
- **Where the page is other than it was first built,** all of it from the review of 2026-10-11, which the last section of the lab's research record gives in full: the speed is 60 and the page says it; the status line says a step of the fade only where there is room to say it, and the end in fewer words; the lines beside a running watch keep their room, so that Stop does not move; a notebook that is worked again gives focus to its heading, where focus was lost to the page with the card that held the button; part 1's notes stand clear of the button of the list of runs; the two small lines of the part that is shown, and of one the pointer is over, are in the page's own ink, where grey on the grey ground measured 4.35 to 1 in the light theme; the delay handed to the engine is never more than the watch had left to run; and the primer's card on an order of nought says that the pace is steady if nothing else changes. Four of these are in the pieces the two labs share and change the clock reaction lab's page too, as 19.8 says: the focus, the notes' room, the ink of the small lines and the delay.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge; the checks were run by script, by mouse, by taps and by keyboard presses a script made, in headless Chromium, by the AI coding assistant that built the screens and then by the AI reviewer, which had built none of them, and by nobody at a keyboard. The builder stepped the fade by a clock the script held; the review also watched it at its own pace, by script. `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device, and no chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer.

## 21. Red cabbage pH lab (added after v1)

The fifth of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used). It is an introductory lab, and a short one. The directions list the bottles; the procedure is the usual one for them, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell, the test tube that is explored and the observation form of sections 8 and 17, and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

### 21.1 Set-up

The juice of red cabbage holds a dye that is a different colour at different acidities: an indicator, and one with many colours where most have two. With it a learner can tell roughly how acid or how basic a thing is by eye, and then check with a meter.

On the bench: thirteen buffer solutions, of pH 1 to pH 13; red cabbage indicator solution; a pH meter with its probe, and a wash bottle of distilled water; and fourteen household things, each as it is used or as a dilute solution in water, named for what they are and never by a brand: vinegar, lemon juice, apple juice, household ammonia, shampoo, hair conditioner, laundry detergent, laboratory detergent, mouthwash, an antacid tablet, aspirin, bleach, drain cleaner and Epsom salt. The directions list a fifteenth, buffered aspirin; it is left off the bench, since no document that states its pH in water could be read.

Three parts:

1. **The scale.** The indicator goes into the thirteen buffers, which stand in a rack in order. The learner looks along the rack and answers three questions that the rack answers: the colour at pH 1, the colour at pH 7, and which two neighbouring tubes show the same colour (pH 11 and pH 12, as the supplier's figure has them).
2. **The household things.** For each one tried: the indicator goes in; the learner looks, says which tube of the scale it is nearest, and whether the thing is acid, neutral or basic; then measures it with the meter. The part is done when eight of the fourteen count; the rest are there to try.
3. **What a unit means.** Two questions from the learner's own readings: how many times more hydrogen ion one thing has than another, a whole number of units apart. Where the first eight things to count hold too few such pairs, a pair of buffers of the scale stands in.

### 21.2 A sample

A sample is a short column of cards, with the rack of the scale kept in view beside or above it:

1. **The tube**, drawn and explored as in sections 8 and 17, with the one action, adding the indicator. What is seen is said in words when the liquid is looked at.
2. **By eye**: which tube of the scale is it nearest, a whole number from 1 to 13, or that the indicator cannot say; and acid, neutral or basic, which is not asked where the indicator cannot say. Neutral means the colour of the pH 7 tube, a reading from 6.6 to 7.5; acid is below that and basic above. The estimate can be changed until the meter is read.
3. **The meter**: the probe is rinsed, put in, and read. The reading is to a tenth of a unit. The meter can be read again: after an unrinsed reading the probe is rinsed and the sample read again, and the last reading stands.

An estimate by eye counts when it is within one unit of the meter's reading. Bleach is the exception that the lab keeps on purpose: it destroys the dye, the colour goes, and the right answer by eye is that the indicator cannot say.

One error of technique: **putting the probe into a sample without rinsing it** after the last one. The reading is then pulled toward the last sample's; it does not count, and says why. Guided warns first.

Guided checks each answer as it is given and has a hint in a closed disclosure. Challenge takes what is recorded and checks a sample when asked.

### 21.3 Model

The engine is pure and deterministic. Each household thing has a typical pH with a source, given as a range, since products differ by maker and by batch; the seed picks one value in the range for a lab, to a tenth, and that is what the meter reads from a rinsed probe. The colour the indicator shows at a pH comes from a stored table of colours against pH, with a source; where the sources give a colour for a range and not an edge, the edges are model parameters and say so. What an unrinsed probe reads is the model's own, a stated share of the way toward the last sample's pH.

Each buffer is taken to be at its labelled pH.

### 21.4 Data

The indicator's colours against pH; a typical pH for each household thing, from a source that can be cited (a food regulator's table of the pH of foods, a maker's or supplier's sheet that states a pH, or arithmetic from a cited constant and a stated strength, which is then said to be arithmetic); that bleach destroys the dye. Hazards by 5.2 for what is a laboratory chemical on this bench: the two buffers at the ends of the scale, the indicator, household ammonia, bleach, drain cleaner and the laboratory detergent. Foods, toiletries and medicines are sold under their makers' own labels, which differ; they share one note, which says that nothing on a bench is tasted and that a product's own label gives its cautions.

### 21.5 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: acids and bases in the kitchen and the bathroom; the pH scale, and that a unit is ten times; indicators, and why red cabbage has so many colours; buffers, and why the scale is made from them; the meter and its probe; when an indicator fails (bleach); safety.

### 21.6 Acceptance

- For seeds 1 to 100, every household thing's pH is inside its sourced range, and a careful learner's estimate by eye, taken as the tube of the scale with the same colour, is within one unit of the meter for every thing but bleach, where the indicator cannot say.
- An unrinsed probe makes a reading not count, and its cause is named; Guided warns first.
- Every stored pH and every colour has a source or is marked as the model's own.
- The same seed and actions give the same lab; the stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the learner's answers are recorded: a thing's pH is not on the page before the meter is read.
- Every colour a learner must tell apart is also said in words, and the description in words of every tube is tested.

### 21.7 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-11, straight through, on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8, 17 and 18. The route is `#/lab/red-cabbage-ph`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it, as "Red cabbage pH", with its status. Where the page is other than 21.2 has it, or other than the Le Chatelier's principle lab's page (18.8), the point below says so in its first words. `docs/research/cabbage/README.md` says where the lab itself is other than 21.1 to 21.6. The lab's one deep review, held on 2026-10-12, changed the page in the points that say so; the last point lists them, and the last section of the research record says what the review found, ran and measured.

- **Lab tab: three parts, and one thing at a time.** A navigation named Parts has a button for each part, three to a row, with its number, its name and how far it has come in words beside a mark: for the first and the last, not begun, under way or done; for the second, how many count of the eight ("3 of 8 count", and past the eight "9 count, 8 needed"); for the last, until eight count, that it is not open yet. Only the part shown is drawn. What the lab is and what a part asks for stand over a lab and a part that are not begun, and stay until another part or thing is shown: they do not go at the first press, which would move the rack or the tube from under the hand.
- **Part 1: the rack.** Thirteen tubes in order on a white card, each a button with its pH under it, drawn with no colour until "Add the indicator to the thirteen buffers" is pressed and then each in the drawing's value for its colour word. A pointer over a tube, keyboard focus on it or a tap looks at it: its edge is drawn dashed, and a line under the rack says "pH 3: a clear rose liquid." and stays until another tube is looked at. The rack is one stop of the Tab key; the arrow keys, Home and End move along it, and each tube they come to is looked at. The first look at a rack with the indicator in is stored, and opens the questions. The whole rack in words is in a disclosure that is closed, since it says all of what the rack shows.
- **The rack is two shelves of seven on an upright tablet with the menu open. Other than a rack in one row.** Thirteen buttons of 44 px need 572 px. Where the rack has 576 px it is one row; where it has not, it is two shelves, pH 1 to 7 over pH 8 to 13, read as a line of words is. The rule follows the width the rack has, never the window's. Measured: at 768 by 1024 with the menu open the bench is 497 px wide and a tube's button is 65 by 113 px in part 1 and 65 by 82 beside a sample; with the menu closed, 649 px wide, the rack is one row and a button 47 px wide; at 1280 by 800 part 1's rack is one row of buttons 74 px wide, and beside a sample, in the right of two columns, two shelves of buttons 51 px wide.
- **The three questions come one at a time. Other than the labs before it,** whose form holds all its questions. Each is a form of choices of its own, drawn once the one before it is answered, recorded by itself, and folded to a line beside "Change" (18.8): twelve colours to choose from for the colour at pH 1 and at pH 7, and twelve pairs of neighbours. So the rack and the question in hand stand in one window. Guided refuses an answer that is not right, says so and never which is, checks the part by itself as the third is answered, and has a hint, closed, under the question that is open. Challenge records what it is given; "Check the three answers" then marks each, and "Answer the three again" sets them up again. The part's notes are shown outright under the questions once the three are right.
- **Part 2: choosing a thing.** The shared paged list, named "Choose a thing to try", has the fourteen things, each with how it stands in words beside a mark (not begun, under way, counts, does not count); it stays behind its button until the page has 928 px, and is beside the thing from there. Previous and Next are named for where they go. How many count is on the part's button and in the line over the thing's name. Until the rack is made the part says to make the scale first.
- **A sample's head.** Which part, how many count, how this thing stands, and which tube of it this is where it has been set up again; its name; what kind of thing it is and how it is on the bench.
- **The tube** is the shared tube of 17.8, with the one action beside it, "Add the indicator", which carries a tick once it is done. **One part of it is looked at, not two: other than 17.8 and 18.8.** The engine has one look for a tube, so the picture has one part, the liquid, and what the look says in its callout is the whole tube: "A cloudy blue liquid over a white solid at the bottom." The callout goes when the pointer or the finger moves to the scale, so the same words stay in a line under the picture's directions, once the liquid has been looked at as it now is (the review's; as built nothing else that is drawn said what is seen). Nothing on the page says it before the look. A cloudy liquid is drawn with faint wisps through it, so that it is told from a clear one of the same colour by more than the word; a solid has its edge, its grain and its bracket. Where the card has 416 px the heading and the action stand beside the picture, which is then 240 px wide; in the narrow left column of a wide page they are over and under it and the picture is 320 px.
- **The scale stays in view, and is what the estimate is chosen on. Other than 21.2 in how the tube is named,** which has a whole number entered. The rack, small, is the card straight under the tube's, or beside it where the sample has 704 px for two columns. Its tubes are looked at as in part 1. Once the sample has been looked at, the card is headed "Which tube of the scale is it nearest?", and a press on a tube chooses it: the tube is then drawn with a whole edge on a grey ground, its number in bold, and its button says that it is pressed. "The indicator cannot say" is the fourteenth place of the two shelves, and stands under the one row. Under the rack, acid, neutral or basic is three choices, with the line that neutral here means the colour of the pH 7 tube; where the indicator is said not to say, the three are not asked and the line says so. One button records the two. Guided takes an estimate that is near enough and then judges acid, neutral or basic against it, and says of whichever is not taken how to look again, never what is right; Challenge records what it is given.
- **The estimate folds to a line in the heading's row,** "By eye: pH 3, acid", beside "Change" until the meter is read, with a mark and its word once it is judged. The row is as tall without "Change" as with it, so that nothing under it moves up when the meter is read (the review's). This form is the lab's own and not the shared form of choices, since one of its two questions is answered on the rack.
- **The meter comes once the estimate is recorded.** The reading stands large and alone, to a tenth, as tall before the meter is read as after; "Rinse the probe" and "Put the probe in and read the meter" are straight under it; and under them is everything that comes and goes with the probe: where it stands (rinsed, or last in a named thing and not rinsed since), Guided's warning, and the sentence on a reading that does not count. So the two buttons are at one height at every press (the review's: as built the line on the probe stood beside the reading and the rest over the buttons, which moved 20 px up when the meter was read and 48 px down after a reading taken anyway). The reading is also a sentence in a status line. A button that cannot be used now stays focusable and says why when pressed. In Challenge "Check this sample" is there once the meter is read.
- **Guided's warning is a line under the two buttons, not the shared group of warnings.** The engine holds the warning in the stored record: the first press of the meter's button with an unrinsed probe reads nothing and sets it, and a second press reads. So the page draws the warning's sentence in a frame under the two buttons, which are its two ways on, and it is there again after a reload. As built it stood over them and moved them down, 80 px at 768 by 1024 and 100 px at 1280 by 800, so that a second press in the same place landed on its words; the owner decided that the buttons stay where they are. Since the second press now lands on the button, a press that comes within half a second of the one that raised the warning is that press come twice and reads nothing, as the rate labs' watch takes no press for half a second after a pour (19.8): a double click raises the warning and reads nothing, and a press a second later reads. While it is up the line that says where the probe stands is not drawn, since the warning says it. A reading taken with an unrinsed probe says, once that is judged, that it does not count and why.
- **How a sample came out, and its note, are under the meter. Other than 18.8,** which has a disturbance's notes first. The reading that settles a sample is the last thing done and is at the page's foot, so the outcome is drawn there: that the sample counts, or that it does not with each cause named and "Set it up again in a fresh tube"; and once it counts, the thing's note, shown outright. Focus goes there when the button that was pressed has gone, and it is brought into view otherwise. A thing set up again is a card of its own, and focus goes to its heading (the review's: it was left on the page's body).
- **Part 3** says what opens it until eight things count. Then its two questions come one at a time, each a form of two questions of choices: which of the two holds more hydrogen ion, whose own sentence gives the two readings it is drawn from ("The vinegar read pH 2.9. The apple juice read pH 3.9."), and how many times more, from ten times to a million million. Each folds to a line; Guided has one hint, closed; the check, the marks, answering again and the notes are as in part 1.
- **Under every part and thing:** its sources, closed, and the bench, closed, 56 px apart from line to line and 16 px under what is over them. The bench is one place: what stands on it in words; a tile with its hazards, closed, for each of the seven bottles that are laboratory chemicals; and the one shared note for everything else, headed by the thing's own name where the thing shown is one of those. **Other than the lab's messages foresaw,** which word the hazards for a heading on each thing's card: the card has none, and the bench is one press away from any of them.
- **A disclosure inside a card,** a hint or the rack in words, stands 24 px under the button over it: the hit area a finger tall over an open one reaches that far up.
- **The colours of the scale** are the drawing's own values for the record's words, in `src/app/lab/tubeDrawing.ts`, chosen so that each step along the rack differs from its neighbours in how light it is or strongly in hue. pH 7 is the darkest, and lightness rises from there to pH 13. The table gives each value, and for each pair of neighbours the colour difference (CIEDE2000) and the ratio of contrast, worked out from the values by script; an automated test holds every pair of neighbours that differ in word to 1.4 to 1 or more and to 0.14 of lightness or 25 degrees of hue (OKLCH), and the two that share a word to one value. The review read the colours from the pixels of the page, in the rack and beside a sample, in both themes at both sizes: each is the value below.
