It changed two, the dark pink (#99186a as built) and the purple (#742bb0 as built): held against all thirteen tubes as a sample is, the purple came out as the blue of pH 8 with protanopia simulated and the dark pink near the teal of pH 9 with deuteranopia; a second test now holds every two of the twelve apart, neighbours or not, as drawn and with each of three colour vision deficiencies simulated, and the research record has the figures before and after. The blue, the yellow and the liquid with no colour are the values the Le Chatelier's principle lab already draws, unchanged; no other lab's picture changes.

  | pH | Word | Value | Against the white card | To the next: CIEDE2000 | To the next: contrast |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 1 | red | #e02424 | 4.72 | 24.7 | 1.65 |
  | 2 | dark pink | #992154 | 7.78 | 33.8 | 3.29 |
  | 3 | rose | #f089ae | 2.36 | 21.7 | 1.44 |
  | 4 | lavender | #d2c2f7 | 1.64 | 39.9 | 3.76 |
  | 5 | purple | #920de4 | 6.18 | 19.6 | 1.58 |
  | 6 | blue-violet | #6f72f2 | 3.91 | 35.4 | 4.29 |
  | 7 | dark blue | #0e1650 | 16.81 | 16.0 | 1.83 |
  | 8 | blue | #1d3ea6 (as it was) | 9.16 | 28.3 | 1.67 |
  | 9 | teal | #0d7480 | 5.49 | 29.7 | 1.62 |
  | 10 | emerald | #16a05c | 3.38 | 20.6 | 1.48 |

  Before the indicator goes in: no colour, #e8edf2 (as it was), 1.18 to 1 against the card; pale yellow, #fbf3bd, 1.13; and white, the card's own #ffffff, which is told from no colour by the wisps of a cloudy liquid and by the word. The least difference between any two of the fifteen is that one, 4.8. None is left to its colour: the word is said at every look, in the rack and in the sample. The tube and the rack stand on white cards in both themes.
- **Record tab:** how each of the three parts stands; and a table of the things that have been begun, in the bench's order, with what was said by eye, acid, neutral or basic or that it was not asked, the meter's reading once it is read, and how the sample came out, with each cause where it does not count. A thing set up again is listed as its fresh tube. Once the lab is complete the line that says so is over it.
- **What Challenge is kept from.** Before the meter is read in a thing, in either mode: its pH, anywhere on the page or in the record. Before a sample counts: its note. Before a sample or a part is checked, in Challenge: whether an estimate, acid, neutral or basic, or an answer is right, and the hints. Before a tube is looked at: what is seen in it, outside the closed disclosure of the rack in words. Automated tests draw every one of the fourteen things at three points before the meter is read, in each mode, and search the markup for the reading a rinsed probe would give, for the thing's note and for a mark. What the page stores is the engine's record, which holds no pH.
- **How long a page is,** measured in headless Chromium on the production build, by a script that worked the whole lab in each mode, from the top of the parts' buttons to the foot of the last card. At 768 by 1024 with the menu open, where the bench is 497 px wide and 976 px of the window are under the tab bar; the parts' buttons and the list's button are 122 px of each figure for a sample. Part 1: 669 px before the indicator and 649 with it in; 945 in Guided and 897 in Challenge with the first question open; 1,115 and 1,067 with the third open over the two that are folded; 1,005 done, with its notes. A sample, over the fourteen things, as the review left the page and measured again (the meter's card is 28 px longer while it has its buttons, and the estimate's row 20 px taller once the meter is read): 702 to 754 before the indicator; 942 to 994 in Guided and 894 to 946 in Challenge with the estimate open; 992 to 1,048 and 924 to 980 with the estimate recorded and the meter open; 1,056 to 1,112 in Challenge with the meter read and the check to come; and 1,004 to 1,049 and 984 to 1,029 once it counts, with its note. The higher end of each is the first thing, which has what the part asks for over it, 52 px, and the two things whose line on how they are on the bench takes two lines. So from the line over a thing's name a sample is 882 to 927 px at its longest in Guided, inside one window, and the tube and the scale are both in view at every point; in Challenge with the check to come it is 934 to 990, which for the longest things is 14 px more than the window. Part 3: 644 px at its first question, 730 at its second, 436 done. With the menu closed, 649 px wide, the rack in one row: part 1 503, 503, 769, 939 and 794; a sample 665 to 717, 905 to 957, 955 to 1,011 and 943 to 995 in Guided. At 1280 by 800, where the sample is 753 px wide beside the list, in two columns, and 752 px are under the bar: part 1 503, 503, 769, 889 and 794; a sample 584 to 636, 665 to 717, 785 to 821 and 727 to 795 in Guided, and 869 to 925 in Challenge with the check to come; part 3 474, 560 and 396.
- **Focus** is not left on the page itself. Recording an estimate leaves it on "Change", and "Change" on the button that records; a check, or a reading that settles a sample, puts it on how the sample or the part came out; the parts, the list, Previous and Next move it to the heading of what is shown, and so does setting a thing up again in a fresh tube.
- **Nothing of the lab's own moves.** Its stylesheet has no animation and no transition, which a test holds. The app's buttons keep the short transition of the app's own style, which the reduced-motion setting stops, as on every page.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** the part and the thing shown, a tube chosen and not yet recorded, the callout of a look, whether a disclosure is open, and what the message lines last said. **Other than 18.8,** only the part and the thing shown are drawn, so a tube chosen and not recorded is dropped when another thing is shown. A reload opens on the scale until the rack is made; then on a thing that is begun and does not count yet, the earliest on the bench; where none is, on the first part that is not done, and in the second on the first thing that does not count; in a lab that is complete, on the last part.
- **What was made shared, or changed in the shell.** The drawing's table of colours has the twelve new values above. The shared tube takes a liquid that is cloudy and draws the wisps, as one path: drawn as a pattern they came out, in the one browser tried, as a white band across the window at 1280 by 800. The shared form of choices hands on the class one choice is drawn with, which this lab's own form uses for its three. No other lab passes a cloudy liquid, and no other lab's markup changes. `vite.config.ts` lists the lab's stylesheet among those a test may read.
- **What was changed below the page.** One note of the record: the hair conditioner's said that it is more acidic than its maker's shampoo, and the shampoo's figure on this bench is another maker's; the note now gives the conditioner's figure and sets it beside nothing, and a test holds that. The lab's messages have the page's fixed words that were missing (how a part stands, how a picture is looked at, the names of the buttons that record, an estimate as one line, the heading over the shared note), each with its test. No engine file, stored chemistry value or model parameter was changed. The review changed one more note of the record, the lemon juice's, which called it the most acidic thing on the bench where a seed can draw the vinegar lower: it now says that its range is the lowest. It changed the primer's example of two juices, from about two units and about a hundred times to one to two units and ten to a hundred times, as the table's ranges have them; and in the messages the sentences that said an unrinsed probe was pulled where it may not have been, the refusal and the hint on acid, neutral or basic, the refusal of a probe last in the thing shown, and how many count past the eight.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge. Everything above was run by script, by keyboard, by mouse and by emulated touch, in headless Chromium on Linux, on the production build, by the AI coding assistant that built the screens, and by nobody at a keyboard. `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device. The colours are values worked out and drawn, not seen on a bench. No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer.
- **The review.** Held on 2026-10-12, when the screens were whole, by an AI reviewer that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium and by reading, and by no person. Where the page is other than it was first built: the meter's card, with the reading over the two buttons and all else under them; the warning under the buttons and the half second after it; the estimate's row; the line of a look that stays; focus after a fresh tube; "9 count, 8 needed"; the sentences named in the point before this one; and two of the scale's colours. The research record's last section has each finding with how it is known, the colours as measured from the screen, what was run and measured, what was left for the owner, and what nobody checked: no person, no screen reader, one browser, no real device, no chemistry instructor.

## 22. Measurement and density lab (added after v1)

The sixth of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used). The directions have two first-week labs, one on laboratory techniques and one on density; the second needs exactly what the first teaches, so they are built as one lab in three parts, which the owner can have split. The directions list what is set out; the procedure is the usual one, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell, the notebook and typed entries of sections 7 and 16, the burette's drawn scale of section 7, and the rule for hazards of 5.2.

### 22.1 Set-up

Every instrument is read to one figure beyond its finest mark, and that last figure is an estimate; a number worked out from measurements can be no better than the worst of them. The lab has the learner read real scales to the right last figure, and then use two of them to find a density and with it what an unknown is.

On the bench: two rulers, one marked in centimetres and one in millimetres, and a long paper note to measure; a 50 mL graduated cylinder, marked each millilitre; a thermometer, marked each degree, with a beaker of ice and water; a balance that shows hundredths of a gram; an unknown liquid; pieces of an unknown metal; distilled water.

Three parts:

1. **Reading instruments.** The note's length and width with each ruler; a volume in the cylinder; the thermometer in ice water; a mass on the balance. Then two things worked out: the note's area from each ruler's pair of readings, which shows what the better ruler buys; and how far the thermometer is off at the one temperature that is known.
2. **The density of a liquid.** The empty cylinder is weighed; some of the unknown liquid goes in; its volume is read and the cylinder weighed again. Density is mass over volume, and a table of liquids says which one it is.
3. **The density of a metal.** The dry pieces are weighed; the cylinder holds water, and its level is read; the pieces are slid in, and the level read again. The rise is the metal's volume. Density, and from a table which metal.

The unknown liquid is one of two and the unknown metal one of three, by the seed, each named only by a letter until it is found; the masses, volumes and readings are seeded too.

### 22.2 A reading

An instrument is drawn large enough to read, with its scale as a real one has it, and is explored as section 7's burette is: the part of the scale that matters can be looked at closely. The learner types what they read.

- **The right last figure.** A reading must have the figures the instrument gives and one estimated figure more: to a tenth of a centimetre on the centimetre ruler and a hundredth on the millimetre one; to a tenth of a millilitre in the cylinder; to a tenth of a degree on the thermometer; every figure of the balance, a final zero included. A reading with too few figures or too many is refused with the reason, as section 7 does for the burette.
- **Near enough.** A reading counts when it is within a stated allowance of what the drawing shows, since the last figure is anybody's estimate. As built the allowance is two of the reading's last figure, counted from where the edge, the level or the thread stands; the balance is copied exactly.
- **Eye level.** A liquid's level is read at the bottom of its curve, with the eye level with it. The drawing has the eye above, level or below, moved by the learner, and shows the level as it looks from there. The eye begins above, as a learner stands at a bench, and only the cylinder has one: the rulers and the thermometer are read as they stand. A level read from above or below is the lab's one error of reading; Guided warns of it and Challenge does not.

### 22.3 The notebook

As section 16's: entries each of which must follow from the learner's own earlier entries, and which can be made, in any order, once the entries it is worked from are in; Guided checks each as it is made and has its relation in a closed hint; Challenge checks them together. What differs is the rounding, which here is the lesson and not a house rule:

- a sum or a difference is given to the decimal place of its least exact term;
- a product or a quotient is given to as many significant figures as its factor with the fewest;
- an entry rounded to more figures than that, or to fewer, is refused or marked, with which rule it broke.

As built, the zero that ends a whole number is counted where a rule asks for it: an entry takes no power of ten, so an area of two figures that comes to 70 cm² is typed "70", and is taken (the review of 2026-10-12; 22.7).

The amounts are seeded so that the unknowns can be told apart at the figures the instruments give.

Two errors of technique in part 3, each real and each its own cause: **weighing the pieces after they have been in the water**, so that the water on them is weighed too; and **dropping the pieces in**, so that water splashes out and the rise reads low, where sliding them down the tilted cylinder loses none. Guided warns first. A run that does not count says why, and a part can be run again with new numbers. As built, a step of the bench that would lose a reading not yet typed is refused (the liquid poured before the empty cylinder's mass is in, the pieces moved before a level is), so that no run is worked into a corner; and pieces weighed wet leave the density inside the tolerance, so that run does not count for its technique and not for its number.

A part counts when its readings are right, its entries follow, its technique was right, and its result is within tolerance of the stored figure; in parts 2 and 3 the unknown named must also be the one it was. The lab is complete when all three parts count.

### 22.4 Model and data

The engine is pure and deterministic and holds whole numbers. What a scale shows is the seeded true value; what the learner makes of it is theirs. An eye above or below the level shifts what is seen by a stated amount, the model's own. The thermometer reads a seeded few tenths off in ice water.

Data: the densities of the two liquids and of the candidate liquids in their table, and of the three metals and the candidate metals in theirs, each at a stated temperature from a source that can be cited; that ice and water together are at 0 °C. As built the liquids are at 20 °C and the metals "near room temperature", which is all their source says; the table of liquids has five rows, the two unknowns, the same two liquids at another strength, and water. A solution's density depends on how its strength is defined: the record says which definition its source uses, which is by mass. Hazards by 5.2 for the unknown liquids and the metals. As built no record is shared: the other labs' aluminium, zinc and sodium chloride are the same substances in another form, amount or strength, so each of the five records is this lab's own, on the documents those labs read. The lab's research record, `docs/research/density/README.md`, lists every place the lab is other than this section.

### 22.5 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: what a measurement is, and why the last figure is an estimate; reading a scale between its marks; reading a liquid's level; significant figures, and which zeros count; rounding what is worked out, for sums and for products; density, and why it tells one substance from another; volume by the water a thing pushes aside; safety.

### 22.6 Acceptance

- For seeds 1 to 100, a careful learner's readings count, their entries follow, and each unknown is found, with the result within tolerance of the stored density; the candidates in each table are far enough apart to tell at the figures the instruments give.
- A reading with the wrong number of figures is refused with the reason; a level read from above or below does not count.
- An entry rounded by the wrong rule, or not following from the learner's own numbers, is refused or marked with which.
- Each error of technique alone makes a run not count, and its cause is named; Guided warns first.
- The same seed and actions give the same lab; the stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the entries are made, and nothing on the page says which liquid or metal an unknown is before it is named.
- The description in words of every instrument's reading is tested, so that the lab can be done without seeing the drawing.

### 22.7 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-12, straight through, as the labs' screens before it were (section 13), on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8, 15, 16 and 21. The route is `#/lab/measurement-and-density`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it with its status. Nothing was lifted into the shell and nothing in it was changed. Where the page is other than 22.2 or 22.3 has it, or than the calorimetry lab's page, the point below says so in its first words. Every figure of a size was measured in headless Chromium on the production build by the AI coding assistant that built the screens, and those of the cylinder's closer look and its pages again by the lab's review. The one deep review was held on 2026-10-12 by an AI reviewer that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium and by reading; a point it changed says "the review's", and the last point lists them.

- **Lab tab: the three parts in a row, and one page of a part at a time. Other than the calorimetry lab,** whose parts are the paged list: here an instrument is the thing shown one at a time, so the parts are three buttons in a row over the part shown, each with how far it has come in words beside a mark, as the red cabbage pH lab has them, and the shared paged list holds a part's pages: what is set out, each instrument the part reads, and the notebook. Part 1 has seven pages (the bench, the two rulers, the cylinder, the thermometer, the balance, the notebook) and parts 2 and 3 four (the bench, the balance, the cylinder, the notebook). The list says of each instrument how many of its readings are taken. Previous and Next are named for where they go. The list is behind its button until the part is 58rem wide, which it is at 1280 by 800 and is not on an upright tablet.
- **A part's head.** Which run of the part this is and how far the part has come, the part's title, and what the part is for, in both modes: the sentence gives no relation and no rule.
- **The bench's page.** What is set out, a line for each thing. For an unknown, under a heading that says why, a tile for each thing it may be, which is its name, its formula and its hazards, closed: both liquids and all three metals until a run of the part counts, and then the one it was. The whole bench in words is behind a closed disclosure.
- **An instrument's page.** How the instrument is marked and what it is read to; the steps of the bench that are worked at it; the instrument drawn; a button that opens the closer look of its scale; each reading taken from it, typed beside it in the shared typed entry; and what it shows, in words, behind a closed disclosure. A reading must have the figures its instrument gives: one with too few or too many is refused under its field with the reason, and a field that takes none says what brings it. **Other than a field for every reading:** a reading the bench does not show yet is its name and what brings it, with no field, and one whose instrument has moved on (the empty cylinder's mass once the liquid is in, the water's level once the pieces are) is read and not typed, with why it cannot be changed. The page then keeps to about a screen.
- **The steps of the bench are where they are worked. Other than one row of steps:** the weighings are on the balance's page, and the pouring, the sliding in and the dropping in on the cylinder's, so that each press shows in the picture beside it. A step that is taken carries a tick; one that cannot be taken stays focusable and says why when pressed. In part 3 a line says where the pieces are.
- **A scale is drawn by one piece,** from the engine's view of it, in the colour of the page's text, so that it follows the theme: a long mark at each numbered value with its number, a mark of middle length halfway between two numbers where ten marks stand to a number, and shorter marks between. A mark is 1, 1.5 or 2 px thick at every size. The picture holds the numbers the scale prints and its unit, and nothing else in figures. A ruler is drawn as clear plastic lying across the paper note, the note's near edge on the zero mark, so that its far edge runs down across the marks; it stops short of the numbers. Each ruler's page has two such pictures, the note's long side along the ruler and its short side. The cylinder is drawn with its liquid, the curve of its surface as a darker band whose lower edge is the level, and the pieces of metal at its foot once they are in, drawn alike whichever metal they are. The thermometer stands in a beaker of ice and water, its thread drawn up its stem; the picture shows it from 10 below zero to a little over 50, and its scale goes on above. An unknown liquid is drawn as water is.
- **The closer look** is a second picture of the same piece, opened and closed by a button over it, which is reached by the keyboard and by touch and does not move when it is pressed: the stretch of the scale the engine hands over, two marks on either side of the two an edge stands between, with half a gap of the scale beyond its first and last marks. The cylinder's is 400 px tall, where the thermometer's is 420, with 25 px beyond its first and last marks and its five gaps as far apart as the thermometer's (the review's: it was 420 px tall with half a gap, and the cylinder's page of part 3 was longer than a screen). **Other than the engine's stretch, for the millimetre ruler:** that ruler is numbered each centimetre, so five of its marks can stand between two numbers with neither in sight, and a mark would be counted from nothing; where the engine's stretch holds no numbered mark the page shows as much more as reaches the nearer one, which is at most two marks more. No other scale needs it. At 768 by 1024 with the menu open, where a page's card is 497 px wide, two marks of a closer look are 76.8 px apart on the centimetre ruler; 76.8, 65.9 or 57.6 px on the millimetre ruler, as its look has five gaps, six or seven; and 69.5 px in the cylinder and on the thermometer. With the menu closed, and at 1280 by 800, the rulers' five gaps are 85.0 px each and the upright scales the same 69.5 px.
- **The eye. Beside the cylinder,** in its picture on the bench, an eye is drawn over, level with or under the level it looks at, with a dashed line to the level; under the picture three buttons, one over another in the eye's own order, move it, the one where the eye is pressed and marked with a filled dot. The eye begins above. The level is drawn where the engine has it from there, in both pictures, and from above or below the closer look shows the rim of the surface as a ring. A sentence under the pictures says where the eye is and does not say which way the level is off; under an empty cylinder it says that the cylinder is empty (the review's: it spoke of the liquid's surface). The three buttons and the button that records a reading were measured at one place through every move of the eye.
- **The balance** is its display, every figure of which is the reading, and what stands on its pan: the object, the cylinder, empty or with the liquid, or the pieces, with drops beside them when they are wet. It has no closer look. **Other than the engine's view,** which has no figure for an empty pan: the display then shows 0.00 g, as a balance does, and the reading has no field until something is weighed.
- **Guided's warning** is the shared component, drawn under the buttons of the step that raised it, or under the readings for a level: before a level is recorded with the eye above it or below it; before the pieces go in unweighed, or are dropped in, one group for both where both apply; and before wet pieces are weighed. It takes focus, and again when the same press raises it again. "Go back" puts focus on "Bring your eye level" for a level and on the button that was pressed for a step; the way on does what was asked. The field of a level held back by the warning is emptied: from where the eye is brought to, the level is read afresh. Any other action drops the warning, and it is not stored.
- **The notebook's page.** The readings as they were typed, each with its unit and, in Guided, how many significant figures it has and whether it is as shown; then what is worked out, each entry in the shared typed entry, open once the entries it is worked from are made, with how it is typed under it and, in Guided, the relation and the rule in a closed disclosure. In Guided an entry rounded by the wrong rule is refused with which rule it broke. A whole number that ends in zeros is taken with those zeros counted where the rule asks for it, so the centimetre ruler's area is typed "70" where it comes to that (the review's: it was refused as one figure, and part 1 could not be finished at about one seed in ten). For an unknown, the table of what it may be, with what its figures are for, stands between the learner's own density and the choice of its name, which is the shared form of choices. The calculator is offered at the head of the page. Challenge has "Check the notebook" under the entries, which says what is missing until every entry is made.
- **A checked run.** When the notebook is checked, at the last entry in Guided or when Challenge asks, the notebook's page is shown and how the run came out takes focus, wherever the last entry was made. A card named "This run" says whether the run counts and, where it does not, why, with "Run this part again with new numbers"; once a run of the part counts, and not before, a closed disclosure has what the unknown was and the part's notes. A new run opens on the bench's page and the part's heading takes focus.
- **Record tab.** For each part, how far it has come and every run of it in order, each with whether it counted, what was read and entered, and, where it did not count, why. An unknown is named there only as the learner named it.
- **Where the page opens, and what is not stored.** The stored record is the engine's. A reload opens the part the learner was in the middle of, and of that part the bench of a run not begun, the first instrument with a reading that can be taken and is not, or the notebook once every reading is in or the run is checked. The part and the page shown, what is typed and not yet entered, a closer look that is open, and a warning that is showing are the page's and are not stored: what is typed and not entered is dropped when another page is shown, and a closer look is closed.
- **Nothing on the page moves.** The lab has no animation and no transition of its own; with reduced motion asked for, no element of the page had one longer than a millisecond at three points of a lab.
- **How long a page is.** At 768 by 1024 with the menu open, 976 px of the window are under the tab bar. With its closer look open, the card of the centimetre ruler's page is 879 px, the millimetre ruler's 899, the cylinder's 778, the thermometer's 746 and the balance's 485. In part 3 the cylinder's page is 952 px with the pieces in and its last level to type, and 964 with the closer look open as well, 12 px inside the window (the review's: it was 984, 8 px over). Part 1's notebook is 880 px, with 686 px from the first reading to the last field; part 2's is 1,017 px in Guided, with 364 px from the first reading to the field of the density and 391 px from the top of the table to the last choice of a name. With the menu closed the rulers' pages are 918 px, their pictures drawn up to 512 px wide. At 1280 by 800, where 752 px are under the tab bar, the rulers' pages are 918 px, the cylinder's 778 and the thermometer's 726: the rulers' and the cylinder's longer than that window.
- **Every disclosure has the room its hit area needs.** A closed disclosure's hit area reaches 12 px over and under its line, and an open one's 24 px over it. At 768 by 1024, on the bench of parts 1, 2 and 3, on the cylinder's page of parts 1 and 3, on the notebooks of parts 1 and 2 in Guided and in Challenge, and on a run that counts, with every disclosure closed and each open in turn, a script asked the page what answers at points 3 px apart over each disclosure's area: none answered for another control. Two places did as first built (the words under a reading's field, and the bench in words under the tiles) and were given room. No button or field of those pages was under 44 px either way but those that carry the frame's own 44 px area.
- **What the tests hold,** without a browser: the marks of every scale; that every closer look holds the engine's stretch and a numbered mark, and how far apart two of its marks are drawn, at seeds 1 to 100; that an edge is drawn where it stands between its two marks; that a picture prints no number but the scale's own, and no page a reading of a scale before it is typed, at seeds 1 to 30; that the pieces are drawn alike for all three metals; that every page of parts 2 and 3 names each thing an unknown may be as often at every seed from 1 to 30; that Challenge says nothing of a reading or an entry before the check and has no relation to open; where the page opens; and the markup of each page, the notebook and the record. What a press does was run in the browser.
- **The review of 2026-10-12.** One pass, by an AI reviewer that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium and by reading. It worked a whole lab in Guided by the keyboard alone (1280 by 800, dark) and one in Challenge by emulated touch (768 by 1024, light), each in stages over the stored record, reading every scale from a picture of its closer look and typing what it read: 50 readings of the four scales at seven seeds, all within the allowance. Its points above: the cylinder's closer look and the length of its pages; the line under an empty cylinder; a whole number of tens in the notebook. Besides: a first level that can no longer be changed says that the pieces have been put into the water, where it said that they are in it, which was false once they were out; the metals' hazard notes no longer say how many grams of each metal the bench holds, which named the metal from the balance; and the primer's card on significant figures has a paragraph on a whole number of tens. It measured the contrast of the text (4.7 to 1 or more in the light theme, 6.2 to 1 or more in the dark, but for controls that cannot be used, the frame's, at 3.7 and 4.9 to 1) and of the scales (marks and numbers 19 to 1 or more; the curve of a liquid 4.4 to 1 or more against the picture and 3.5 to 1 or more against the liquid). It left the millimetre ruler's closer look widened, since a mark must be counted from a number. The lab's research record has the review whole, in its last section.
- **Not checked.** No person has used the page, by hand, by keyboard or by touch: every reading of a scale was made by an AI, the builder's by script from the engine's figures and the reviewer's from pictures. No screen reader has been used on it. It was run in headless Chromium on Linux only, at 768 by 1024 and 1280 by 800, in the light and the dark theme, on no real device. No chemistry instructor has read the record, the primer, the notes or the hazards.

## 23. Freezing point depression lab (added after v1)

The seventh of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used). The directions list what is set out; the procedure is the usual one for those things, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It is built on the calorimetry lab's pieces (section 16): the foam cup, the logger and its graph that is explored, the worksheet, an unknown named from a table.

### 23.1 Set-up

Something dissolved in water makes the water freeze at a lower temperature, and how much lower depends on how many dissolved particles there are for each kilogram of water, not on what they are. So the fall in the freezing point counts particles: it gives the molar mass of an unknown, and it shows that a salt comes apart into ions.

On the bench: a foam cup with a lid, a stirrer and a temperature probe read by a logger to a hundredth of a degree; ice and distilled water; a balance that shows hundredths of a gram; a wire strainer and a second cup; an unknown liquid, which is one of acetone, ethanol and 2-propanol, by the seed; and urea, sodium chloride, potassium chloride and sodium bromide.

Three parts:

1. **The freezing point of water.** Ice and water are stirred in the cup until the logger is steady. That reading is this probe's freezing point of water, and every fall is measured from it.
2. **Molar mass of an unknown.** A weighed amount of the unknown liquid is stirred into ice and water; the logger falls and steadies; the ice is strained out and the solution weighed. From the fall and the masses, the unknown's molar mass, and from a table which of the three it is.
3. **How many particles.** The same with urea and with one of the three salts, of the learner's choosing; the other two salts are there to try. From each fall, how many dissolved particles each formula unit gave: close to one for urea, and close to two, a little under, for a salt. The part is done when a run with urea counts and a run with a salt does, and it stays open once the lab is complete, so that the other salts can still be tried; parts 1 and 2 are then read and not worked.

### 23.2 A run

A run is a short column of cards, as section 16's:

1. **The bench**: what is used, with its hazards, and the balance's readings as they are made. Until the unknown is named the bench shows the tiles of all three liquids it may be.
2. **The cup**: drawn, with its state in words. Ice and water go in; the lid goes on and the stirrer is started; the logger is started; the weighed solute is added; "Run on" lets time pass. No clock runs.
3. **The graph**: temperature against time, explored as section 16's. The curve falls when the solute goes in and levels off. The learner looks at the reading the solute went in after, which ends the level stretch before it, and at the logger's last reading, taken as the cup is strained: the solution that is weighed is the one in the cup at that reading, since the ice goes on melting slowly after the fall.
4. **Straining and weighing**: the cup is poured through the strainer into the second cup, which holds back the ice, and the solution is weighed. The water in it is its mass less the solute's.
5. **The notebook**: in part 1, the one steady reading; in parts 2 and 3, the two temperatures and their difference; the mass of water, in kilograms with five decimals, which is every figure the balance gave; then for part 2 the molality from the fall, the moles of unknown, its molar mass, and which liquid it is; for part 3 the moles of solute from its molar mass, the molality, the fall that molality would give if each formula unit were one particle, and the ratio of the fall found to that one.

Entries are typed and checked as section 16's: each must follow from the learner's own earlier entries, rounded as the primer says; Guided checks each as it is made and has its relation in a closed hint; Challenge checks them together.

Three errors of technique, each real and each its own cause: **reading the temperature before it has levelled off**; **letting all the ice melt**, so that what is read is no longer a freezing point; and **weighing the solution without straining out the ice**, so that ice is counted as water. A lid left off and a stirrer left still are not errors of their own: they are how the first two come about, and Guided warns of each by name. Guided warns first. A run that does not count says why, and can be made again with new numbers.

A part counts when its technique was right, its readings are the logger's, its entries follow, and its result is within the tolerance of the stored figure, which is 1.5% for a molar mass and 3% for a count of particles; in part 2 the liquid named must be the one it was. The lab is complete when all three parts count.

### 23.3 Model

The engine is pure and deterministic and holds whole numbers. While ice and liquid are both in the cup and it is stirred, the temperature goes to the freezing point of what is dissolved at that moment; ice melts as the solute goes in and as the cup takes heat from the room, slowly with the lid on, and the solution grows a little more dilute as it does, which the graph shows as a slow creep after the fall. If the ice runs out the temperature climbs toward the room's.

The freezing point's fall is the constant for water times the molality times the particles each formula unit gives. For the unknown liquids and for urea that is one, the ideal, said as one. For each salt it is a measured figure at about the strength used where a source that can be read gives one, and otherwise two, the ideal, said to be the ideal and to be more than a real salt gives. The amounts are seeded, and chosen so that the three liquids can be told apart at the figures the instruments give.

### 23.4 Data

The freezing point constant of water; the molar masses of the three liquids, urea and the three salts; the measured freezing points of solutions of each where a source gives them. Hazards by 5.2 for the three liquids, urea and the three salts, sharing other labs' records for the same thing in the same form.

### 23.5 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: freezing and melting, and why ice and water together sit at one temperature; what dissolving something does to that temperature; molality, and why it is counted for each kilogram of water; the freezing point constant; from a fall to a molar mass; salts and ions, and the count of particles; reading the graph; straining and weighing; safety.

### 23.6 Acceptance

- For seeds 1 to 100, a careful run of each part counts; the unknown's molar mass is within tolerance of the stored one and names the right liquid of the three; urea's count of particles is close to one and each salt's close to its stored figure.
- Each error of technique alone makes a run not count, and its cause is named; Guided warns first.
- An entry that does not follow from the learner's own numbers is refused or marked.
- The same seed and actions give the same run; the stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the entries are made, and nothing on the page says which liquid the unknown is before it is named.
- The descriptions in words of the cup and of the graph are tested.

### 23.7 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-12, straight through, on the shell and the pieces of sections 7, 8, 15 and 16. The route is `#/lab/freezing-point-depression`, loaded lazily; Home, the list of labs and the command palette show it, eleventh of the labs, with its status. Where the page is other than 23.2 or than the calorimetry lab's page (16.7), the point below says so in its first words.

- **What is shared with the calorimetry lab.** Three pieces of that lab's page were lifted into the shell, and each lab now hands its own to them. The foam cup's drawing (`src/app/lab/FoamCup.tsx`): the two cups, the lid, the stirrer, the probe and the logger, with how high the liquid stands, what lies in it and what stands beside the cup handed in. The logger's graph that is explored (`LoggerGraph.tsx` with `graphGeometry.ts`): the readings in the lab's own units, how the temperature axis is cut for them, the lines that mark what was done to the cup, and the lab's words for a point and for its callout. The worksheet (`Worksheet.tsx`): the numbered list with one entry waiting, the typed entry, the choice, the row that is read and Challenge's check. The calorimetry lab's markup is unchanged: its Lab tab at every step of a careful run of each part, in both modes, at two seeds, with and without reduced motion, and its Record tab beside each, were written out before the change and after it and were the same byte for byte; its page tests pass with two lines changed, which are where a test reads the stylesheet and the geometry from; and its part 1 was worked in a browser after the change as far as the first entry of its worksheet. **Left unshared:** a part's card, the list of parts, the Record tab and the page itself. Each is this lab's own copy of the calorimetry lab's pattern: what they hold differs at every line (the steps of the cup, when a card comes, six warnings where that lab has two, the tiles, a part that wants two runs), and a shared form of them would be mostly arguments.
- **Lab tab: one part at a time,** as 16.7: the paged list of the three parts, each with how far it has come in words beside a mark; part 3 has a word of its own for one of its two runs counted. A part's head says which run this is, what the part finds, in both modes, what part 3 still wants (a run with urea, a run with a salt, or neither), and that the part is done.
- **A run is a column of six cards at the most,** each there when the engine has a use for it: the bench and the cup from the start; the graph once the logger is started; straining and weighing once the solute is in; the notebook once the solution is weighed, and in part 1 with the logger; and "This run" once the logger is started. **The review's:** once the solution is weighed the bench, the cup and the straining are what was done, and each is drawn as its heading over a closed disclosure whose summary says it in one line: the solute's mass and the solution's as the balance read them; that the cup was poured through the strainer and is empty, or was left as it was; and that it was strained and weighed, or weighed without straining, with the balance's reading. Everything a card held is still in it. The graph and the notebook stay open. The press that weighs is made in the straining's card: as the cards fold, the window is scrolled by as much as that card moved, so that it stays where it was, and focus goes to its line. A run opened already weighed is drawn folded and nothing is moved; part 1 weighs nothing and folds nothing. **Other than 16.7,** whose notebook is drawn from the start of the change with a sentence that says what opens it: here it is not drawn until it can be written in.
- **The bench.** What is used and the balance's readings as they are made, as a short list. Parts 2 and 3 have the freezing point constant and under it a table of molar masses: the three liquids the unknown may be, or the four things part 3 can dissolve. Part 2 has, under a heading that says why, a tile for each of the three liquids, which is its name, its formula and its hazards, closed; once a run of the part counts the heading names the liquid and one tile is left, and the table keeps all three. Part 3 has the choice of what this run dissolves as four buttons a finger tall, the one chosen pressed, which is said to assistive technology, and ticked; it can be changed until the solute is in the cup. Its tiles are of all four until one is chosen and of that one after. The water and the ice have no tile.
- **The cup.** The shared drawing with this lab's own. Once the cup is filled the ice is afloat in the water: six cubes while there is plenty, two small ones when a little is left, and none when none is, so that the three are told apart by a count and by the words beside the drawing, and by no colour. Beside the cup the second cup stands on the balance with the wire strainer on its rim. Once the cup is poured through the strainer it is drawn empty with its lid on the bench, the strainer holds the ice that was left (five cubes, two, or none), and the solution stands in the second cup. The solute is not drawn; the words say that it is in. Beside the drawing a status line says the cup's state, the ice among it. Under it are the steps as the engine offers them, each ticked once taken: the ice and water, the lid, the stirrer, the logger and, in parts 2 and 3, the solute. **The review's:** the stirrer's step is "Stir", as 16.7's, where it was "Start the stirrer": the five steps are then two rows at every width that shows a bench. A cup whose ice has all melted is said to hold water, and one weighed with its ice to have been weighed without straining.
- **Run on is over the graph,** as 16.7, with what one press does beside it. Nothing over it changes height as it is pressed: the cup's words stand beside the drawing and are shorter than it, and the line that says why a press did nothing is under the button. Measured in the browser before and after every press of each run of presses, in the three parts in each mode, the button stood at one place.
- **The graph** is the shared one with readings in hundredths of a degree. **Its scale is this lab's own.** A cup starts a few degrees over zero, settles a little under it and falls one to two degrees, so the axis ends on half degrees close about the readings, with three tenths of a degree of room or more over and under, and is marked in whole degrees while it spans less than eight. Over seeds 1 to 100, with the unknown and with each of part 3's four solutes, the axis spans 4.5 to 7.0 degrees and a degree is 28 to 44 units of the picture's 280; the fall from the reading the solute followed to the last reading is 37 to 80 units, and its first step alone 26 to 58; the three readings that end a level stretch are at one height. A hundredth of a degree is under half a unit and is not read from the axis: a point's callout gives its time and its reading to the hundredth. A dashed line marks where the solute went in and a dotted one where the cup was strained or weighed, each just after its reading and each named in the key. Pointer, keyboard, touch and the two buttons that step are as 16.7's. The graph in words is behind a disclosure that is closed, since it names the two readings the notebook asks for; no reading is printed beside the picture, and the logger's drawn screen shows none.
- **Straining and weighing** are a card of two steps, under the graph. The balance's reading is not said in that card: it comes to the bench's list and to the head of the notebook, which is drawn under the card as the solution is weighed.
- **Guided's warnings** are the shared group, raised in the card of the button pressed, for each of the engine's six: the lid off and the stirrer still (at the logger and at the solute), the temperature not level or no ice left (at the solute, at the strainer, and in part 1 at the reading entered), a little ice left (at Run on; **the review's:** once in a run, at the first press made with little left, and not at each press as built), and the cup not strained (at the balance). The warning takes focus, and again when the same press raises it again; "Go back" puts focus on the step that was skipped, or on the button pressed where none was; "Go on anyway" does what was pressed. In part 1 the reading stays typed in its field, with the warning's title under it, until the warning is answered. A warning is not stored.
- **The notebook** is the shared worksheet under the calculator's button and the bench's figures, which are said again at its head: the two masses, the constant, and the molar masses (in part 3, that of the solute chosen). Part 1 has neither: its one entry is a reading. The first temperature waits for a look at the reading the solute went in after and the second for a look at the last reading, each with the reason under its field. The liquid of part 2 is chosen from three boxes. **The review's:** a notebook filled in Challenge and left unchecked when the mode is changed to Guided has the button that checks it, in the shared worksheet and so in 16.7's page too; as built, Guided had none, since it checks at the last entry.
- **This run** is as 16.7's, with "Run this part again" under it; in part 3, once a run counts, the line beside the button says that the other solutes are there to try. Once a run counts, a closed disclosure holds what the result was held to (for part 3, a sentence for each solute a run has counted with), the record's notes, and how each molar mass of the table was worked out.
- **A lab that is complete** says so over the list. Parts 1 and 2 are then read and not worked; part 3 is worked as before, and a page opened on a complete lab with a new run of part 3 under way opens on part 3.
- **Record tab:** as 16.7's, with what a run of part 3 dissolved over its entries. It shows nothing of what a result is held to. **The review's:** a run that did not count because an entry did not follow has a sentence of the Record's own, which says so and sends the reader to no notebook; as built it was given the Lab tab's.
- **How long a run's page is,** measured at 768×1024 with the menu open, where the window has 959 px under the tab bar. **As the review left it:** the cup's card is 328 px in every part, its five steps in two rows; the graph's is 584 px until the cup is strained and 605 px after, when its key has a line more. So the two, with the 12 px between them, are 924 px, and 945 px once the cup is strained: inside one screen. Until the solution is weighed the bench is 134 px in part 1, 557 px in part 2 and 600 to 620 px in part 3, and straining and weighing 120 px; from the weighing on, the bench, the cup and the straining are 90 px each. The notebook is 618 to 638 px at its first entry and 1,022 to 1,084 px at its eighth. The whole page is 2,268 to 2,288 px as the notebook opens, 2,672 to 2,714 px with the eighth entry waiting and 2,246 to 2,326 px when the run is finished: two screens and a little. Through the fold the straining's card stood at one place in the window in every run worked, to the pixel here and within one at 1280×800. **As built** the cup's card was 378 px where the step that adds the solute took a row of its own (part 2, and part 3 with a salt), so that the two cards were 974 px in part 2, 15 px over one screen; the bench grew by a line, 44 px, as the solution was weighed, which moved the card under the hand; and the whole page of a finished run was 3,003 to 3,198 px as the review measured it, a little over three screens. The bench's figures at the head of the notebook end 21 px over the first entry and 637 px over the eighth, and from their top to the foot of the entry that waits is 913 px at the most, so that both are in one screen at every entry. The graph is not: from the top of its picture to the foot of the second entry is 1,017 px (1,047 px as built), a short scroll between the look and the typing, with the folded card of straining and weighing between the two; to the foot of the first entry it is 863 px, inside one screen.
- **Hit areas.** Every button, field and choice box is 44 px or more each way, or carries the frame's own 44 px area. The disclosure of the notes has 8 px of room over it, and a part's Sources 16 px over and 8 px under, so that an open disclosure's area, which reaches 24 px up from its line, lies over no other control. Measured point by point in the browser with each disclosure open in turn, at both sizes, on five states of the page: no point of any control's area answered for another control, and the least room between a laid area and the next control was 4 px. **The review's:** measured again on the page as it now is, on six states, with every disclosure open in turn and the ones inside another among them (the sources inside an open hazard tile, and the tiles inside a folded bench), which the first pass had not opened: the same, with 4 px the least room. A link in a list of sources is the height of its text, 31 px where the citation is one line.
- **What Challenge is kept from, and what nobody is told.** Automated tests search the Lab tab's markup at every step of a careful run of each part up to the check, and after a check that does not count, for the figure a result is held to, the notes, a relation and whether an entry follows. A test draws part 2 at four steps up to the naming for twelve seeds that between them have all three liquids, in both modes, and holds that with the pictures, the graph in words and every number taken out, the markup is the same for all twelve: nothing in the words, the names or the order of the page differs with which liquid the unknown is.
- **What the page keeps and does not store:** what is typed and not yet entered, the part that is shown, the reading last looked at and its callout, a warning that is showing, whether the calculator is open, and what the message lines last said.
- **Not done by a person:** how it looks is the owner's to judge. The lab was worked by script in headless Chromium by the AI coding assistant that built the screens, from Start to the line that says it is complete, in Guided at 768×1024 and in Challenge at 1280×800, each entry typed from what the page showed; `docs/for-institutions.md` says what was run and what was not. No person has run the lab, no screen reader was used, one browser was tried and no real device. No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes or the primer.
- **The review of 2026-10-12,** by an AI reviewer that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium and by reading. The points above that are its own: the fold of the bench, the cup and the straining once the solution is weighed, with the window held where it was and focus on the straining's line; "Stir", and the cup's five steps in two rows; the cup's words for a cup with no ice and for one weighed unstrained; Run on's warning of little ice once in a run; the button that checks a notebook filled in Challenge once the mode is Guided; the Record's own sentence for an entry that does not follow; the lengths measured again; and the hit areas measured again with the disclosures inside others. Besides these it changed four sentences that said the cup had been strained, or that ice had been counted, where it might not have been. It worked the lab from Start to complete four times, in Guided by the keyboard alone at both sizes and in Challenge by emulated touch, with each of the three salts carried to its end, and worked the calorimetry lab's part 1 to its end by the keyboard alone. It changed no stored value and no parameter of the model. What it found, measured, looked at and left, and what nobody checked, is the last section of `docs/research/freezing/README.md`.
- **Room over the hint of the entry that is chosen, since 2026-10-12.** Measured by the reviewer of the endothermic and exothermic lab, by script in headless Chromium at 768×1024 and at 1280×800, on the entry that names the liquid: with its hint open, the hint's hit area answered for the lowest 7 of the 44 rows of pixels of "Enter" above it, as on the calorimetry lab's page (16.7, last point). The page now asks the shared worksheet for the same 12 px of room, and no point of "Enter" answers for anything else.

## 24. Types of reactions lab (added after v1)

The eighth of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used), and the first of three short ones from an introductory course. The directions list what is set out for five reactions, named A to E; the procedure is the usual one for those things, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell, the test tube that is explored and the observation form of sections 8 and 17, and section 8's builder of equations from tiles.

### 24.1 Set-up

Most reactions a first course meets are one of four kinds: two things join into one (combination); one thing comes apart into two (decomposition); an element takes the place of another in a compound (single replacement); two compounds trade partners (double replacement). The lab shows one or two of each, and has the learner see what happens, say which kind it is, and write its equation.

Five stations:

- **A. Zinc in copper(II) sulfate solution.** A grey piece of zinc in a blue solution: a red-brown coating forms on the zinc and the blue fades. Single replacement.
- **B. Magnesium burned in air.** A strip of magnesium held in a flame burns with a white light too bright to look at and leaves a white powder. Combination.
- **C. Three metals in hydrochloric acid.** Magnesium, zinc and copper, each in its own tube of acid: magnesium bubbles fast, zinc slowly, copper not at all. Single replacement, twice, and no reaction once; and from the three, which metal is the most active.
- **D. Calcium chloride and sodium carbonate solutions.** Two clear, colourless solutions give a white solid. Double replacement.
- **E. Hydrogen peroxide with a little iron(III) chloride.** The peroxide alone does nothing that can be seen; with a few drops of iron(III) chloride it bubbles and the tube grows warm. Decomposition, and the iron chloride is not used up. As stored, the station asks whether there are bubbles and whether the tube is warmer, and for no rate and no colour, since no source that was read gives either.

The directions name iron(III) chloride for reaction E and nothing to go with it. Hydrogen peroxide is this lab's reading of that, since it gives the one kind of reaction the other four stations lack; it is the owner's to correct.

### 24.2 A station

A station is a short column of cards:

1. **The bench**: what is used, with its hazards. As built it is at the foot of every station's page, behind a disclosure, with a tile for each of the lab's nine bottles and metals (24.6).
2. **Before and after**: the tube, the dish or the flame, drawn and explored as in sections 8 and 17: what is there before, the one action (put the metal in; hold the strip in the flame; mix the two solutions; add the drops), and what is there after. Bubbles, a coating, a solid, a colour that goes, light and warmth are each shown and each said in words. As built, a strip that has burned is two pictures, its light and what is left of it; and warmth, which cannot be drawn, is said by the picture once the tube has been felt (24.6).
3. **What is seen**: choice boxes, opened by looking.
4. **Which kind**: one choice of the four, or that nothing reacted (station C's copper).
5. **The equation**: built from tiles with section 8's builder, balanced; for station C one equation for each metal that reacts. As built, a learner who says that nothing reacted is asked for no equation, whatever the tube, so that the page does not show which tubes react; and a whole multiple of an equation is taken as right.

Station C adds one question: the three metals in order of how active they are. As built it is asked as two choices, the most active and the least, and the third follows.

Guided checks each answer as it is given and has a hint in a closed disclosure. Challenge takes what is recorded and checks a station when asked. A station counts when what was seen matches what the station showed, the kind is right and the equation is right and balanced. No slip of technique spoils a run. The lab is complete when all five stations count.

### 24.3 Model and data

The engine is driven by its record, as section 8's and section 18's are: each station holds what is seen before and after, the right answers and the tiles its equation is built from, each with a source. Nothing is drawn by chance.

Data: what each reaction gives and what is seen, from sources that can be cited; that magnesium is more active than zinc and zinc than copper, from a cited activity series or from the standard potentials in the tables the other labs use; that iron(III) speeds the decomposition of hydrogen peroxide and is left unchanged. Hazards by 5.2 for every bottle and metal, sharing other labs' records for the same thing in the same form. Burning magnesium is in the primer's safety topic: its light harms the eyes and water makes the fire worse.

### 24.4 Primer

Short topics, those to read first marked: what a chemical reaction is, and the signs of one; the four kinds, each with a pattern in letters, and an everyday case of three of them (none was found in a source for a single replacement); an equation, and what balancing means; metals that push other metals out, and the rules their order is found by (the order of the bench's three is an answer the lab asks for, so the primer does not give it); a catalyst; safety.

### 24.5 Acceptance

- For each station, what is shown before and after is what its stored record gives and nothing else, and the station counts when what was seen, the kind and the equation are right.
- Every equation of the record balances in atoms and in charge, by a test; the builder refuses or marks an unbalanced one.
- Every stored observation and rule has a source.
- The stored record round-trips; no state a learner can reach leaves them unable to go on.
- Challenge shows nothing of what is expected before the learner's answers are recorded: a station's kind and its equation are not on the page before they are given.
- The description in words of every picture is tested.

### 24.6 The page as built

Built on 2026-10-12, straight through, on the shell and the pieces the eleven labs before it made, and reviewed that day; what the review changed is marked "(the review)" and listed in the last point. The route is `#/lab/types-of-reactions`, loaded lazily; Home and the list of labs link it with its status, and the lab is the twelfth of the activities. Where the lab as stored is other than 24.1 to 24.5, its research record says so (`docs/research/reactions/README.md`); where the page is other than 24.2, the point on that here does.

- **What is shared with the aqueous reactions lab, and what was changed in the shell.** The builder of an equation from tiles was that lab's own and is now the shell's (`src/app/lab/EquationBuilder.tsx`, with the row of places and what is done to it in `equationRow.ts`, which is pure). Each lab gives it its tiles and the formula each is drawn as, the states it offers, the count of places, every word it says, and what it finds of an equation that is entered. Three things were added for this lab, none of which the aqueous lab uses: an equation that is taken and not yet judged, which folds to its line as a right one does (Challenge here); a sentence or two under the heading; and the bank of tiles over the row of places and not under it, with the line of a term's controls under the tiles and over the row too (`toolsFirst`; the controls' line was moved there by the review). The aqueous lab keeps two small files that hand the shell's pieces its record, its three forms and its words, and its page is as it was: the markup of its Lab tab at each of its ten stations in six states, and of its Record tab in each, 66 renders, was written out before the lift and after it and after the three additions, with the generated ids numbered, and the files are the same byte for byte; its page tests pass untouched; and one equation was then built there by taps and one by the keyboard, in the browser, and each was found correct. After the review's change to the builder a test holds that lab's order (the row, the controls' line, the tiles; since 2026-10-17 that lab asks for `toolsFirst` too, see 8.3), and the three equations of its first station were worked to their end in the browser, one by emulated touch and two by the keyboard alone. The paged list takes a mark to stand before an item in place of its number: a station's letter. The drawing's table of colours has the paler blue, the three metals and the coatings. The picture of a vessel is this lab's own, drawn on the shared tube's glass and contents and the shared way a picture is explored.
- **Lab tab: one station at a time.** The five are listed by letter, with how far each has come in words beside a mark; where the tab is narrow the list is behind a button. "Station A", which run, and how far it has come are over the station's heading, which is what is used and never the kind of reaction. Previous and Next are named for the stations they go to. A station that has been shown stays in the page, hidden, so that what was chosen or built there and not yet recorded is still there on coming back; a new run of a station is a card of its own. Under the ways on, in one place every station has: the station's sources, with those of what is seen afterwards, of the formulas on the tiles and of the answers listed only once the station counts (the review kept back the first two, whose pages' names can say what a reaction is), and the bench, which is what the station uses in words and a tile with its hazards for each of the nine bottles and metals.
- **A station with one vessel is one card:** its picture; what is done to it (what is to be done, the one button with the picture in words behind a closed disclosure beside it where there is the width, once the button is pressed that it has been, and which parts have been looked at); what is seen; which kind; and the equation, which has the card's whole width. In a card 464 px wide or more what is done stands beside the picture; in one 704 px wide or more the picture is on the left for the whole height with what is done, what is seen and which kind on its right. An entry that is recorded folds to one row: its lines, what is said of them (that it is what the vessel showed, that it is the kind, or in Challenge that it is not checked) and "Change" (the review; as built the sentence had a line of its own). What is seen opens once the action is done and every part has been looked at since; the kind once what is seen is recorded; the equation once a kind is said that is not "No reaction".
- **The pictures.** A picture is 240 units wide, on a white card as every lab's tube is, with a button over each part that can be looked at. A pointer over a part, focus on it or a tap outlines it, and what is seen there is said in the room under the drawing, which is a status; while nothing is looked at the same room says what to do with the picture. Nothing beside a picture says what it shows. Once what is seen of every vessel of the station is recorded each picture is drawn short, the drawing alone with no room under it, 200 units tall: what a part shows is then said in a box that hangs under the drawing, over what stands there, for as long as the part is looked at, a press goes through the box, and what to do with the picture is a screen reader's alone (the review). The tube is the shared tube's glass, liquid, cloudiness and spread solid. A piece of metal is a bar in its stored colour, lying beside the tube before and standing in the liquid after, and drawn in its coating's colour under a grain once it is coated. Bubbles are told apart with nothing moving: none drawn; three small ones over the metal; fourteen larger ones about the metal and up to the surface; or seven of a middling size through the liquid where the record gives no rate. The burner has tongs that hold the strip beside it, and until the strip is held in the flame its two parts are named the strip and the tongs (the review; as built they were named for what they show afterwards); a strip that has burned is two pictures side by side, "While it burns" with the light as a star with rays and "Afterwards" with a heap of powder in the tongs, since the two are one stored state and are not seen at one moment. Nothing on the page moves, with reduced motion asked for or not. Warmth is not drawn: the tube is felt by a part with a hand on it, and once it has been felt and is warm the words stand by the hand.
- **The equation.** The station's tiles, each named in full for a screen reader, stand over the row of places, and under the tiles, still over the row, the line of the term last filled or pressed: its number in front, its state and its way out. At an upright tablet's width the row takes a second line as it is filled, and whatever is under it moves down; over it, the tiles and the buttons that raise and lower a number keep their place, and only the button that records moves, which is pressed once. As built that line was under the row, and in a narrow card the row had the height of two lines from the start so that the buttons would not move; the review moved the line and took that height away. In Guided the row has the places the engine gives; in Challenge each side is its terms and one empty place. A tile that is pressed goes into the place with the thick outline and the next empty place is outlined; a press on a place chooses it. Guided refuses an equation with something wrong in it and says what kind of thing; Challenge takes it and says that it is recorded.
- **Station C.** The three tubes stand in one row at every width, each drawn 160 units wide with its piece close beside it, each named by its metal, with what is seen of it under it: once recorded, a word and its choice ("Bubbles: fast") with the tick of an answer found right before it, the sentence that says so being there a screen reader's alone (the review). The kind and the equation of each tube and the order of the three are asked once what is seen in all three is recorded (the review; as built, as soon as one was, which put an open question under three full pictures), one at a time, chosen from a row of four that is a word each to the eye and the whole name to a screen reader, and that marks which are recorded: a filled circle for what is recorded, and a tick only for what has been found right, which in Challenge is after the check (the review; as built a tick for both). Each keeps what is chosen or built for it while another is shown.
- **A run that is checked, and a station that counts.** Guided checks a station by itself at its last entry; Challenge has "Check the station". From then on what was recorded is lines, each with its mark, and how the run came out is under them, with why where it does not count and "Work the station again", which asks first. A station that counts shows its notes outright and first, with each finished equation; at station E the catalyst's formula is over the arrow, and a sentence under the equation says so in words. Focus goes to the notes, or to how the run came out, when the form that was pressed goes.
- **The Record tab** lists every run of each station: whether it counts, what was recorded of each vessel, the kind said, the equation built, the order, and why a run does not count. Nothing is marked in a run that has not been checked. A run left behind is not said to be worked again (the review).
- **Where the page is other than 24.2, and other than the labs it builds on.** The bench is at the foot of each station and not a card at its head, so that a station's picture is the first thing on its page. What a part shows is said under the drawing and not beside the part, since three pictures in a row leave no room beside one. A strip that has burned is two pictures. Warmth is said only once it is felt, so that the page gives away no answer that a look has not found. The tiles and a term's controls are over the row. Station C asks its kinds, equations and order one at a time, once its three tubes are recorded. The order is two choices. The builder takes a tile into the outlined place at one press, and a place is chosen by a press on it; it has no step in which a tile is first picked up and a place then pressed, and dragging, which it also takes, is not needed.
- **What was measured as built,** in headless Chromium on the production build, at 768×1024 with the menu open, where a station is 497 px wide and 976 px of the window are under the tab bar; the lengths as the review left them are in the last point. From a station's head to the foot of its last card before the one that closes it, at each stage (before; what is seen open; the kind open; the equation open; the equation built; done, with its notes): station A 467, 795, 910, 1,001, 1,021 and 1,023 px; station B 467, 853, 1,026, 1,117, 1,137 and 1,062 px; station D 467, 613, 910, 1,051, 1,071 and 902 px; station E 467, 695, 910, 1,001, 1,021 and 1,028 px. Station C is 673 px before, 869 px with what is seen open, 1,281 px with the kinds open, 1,422 and 1,442 px with an equation open and built, 1,178 px at the order and 1,268 px done. The card that closes a station is 134 px more. So stations A, D and E are inside a screen until the equation is asked, 25 to 95 px over one while it is built, and from 74 px inside one to 52 px over once done; station B, which has two pictures, is 50 px over at its kind and up to 161 px over at its equation; station C is inside a screen until its kinds are asked and longer than one from there, by up to 466 px. At 1280×800, with 752 px under the tab bar, stations A, D and E are 394 to 809 px, station B up to 933 px and station C up to 1,280 px. A picture is 240 px wide, the strip's two 208 px each, and station C's three 148 or 149 px each at 768 and 160 px at 1280; the least part of any picture is 49 px each way, and no button, choice, tile or place of a station was under 44 px either way. At station D, the widest equation, the bank of tiles, the button that raises a number and "Record the equation" each stood at one place through four tiles and eight presses that took a number to 9 (after the review the first two do, and the third moves once, by 52 px, as the row takes its second line). At the foot, with Sources, the bench, each of the nine hazards and the sources inside each open in turn, 22 states at each size, no point of any control's box answered for another control. With reduced motion asked for, no element of the page had a transition or an animation over a millisecond; without it, 23 did, all of them the frame's buttons.
- **What was run.** The whole lab from Start to the line that says it is complete, in Guided, every look by a press on the picture, every answer by its box, every equation tile by tile: by the pointer at 768×1024 and at 1280×800 in the light and the dark theme, by the keyboard alone at 1280×800, and by emulated touch at 768×1024, six runs, with no error reported by the browser; in each, an equation with every number 1 was first recorded at station B and refused with the sentence that the atoms do not balance; a reload then opened the Lab tab on the complete lab. In Challenge, station A recorded wrongly, with "No reaction" said, which asked for no equation and showed no mark until the check, did not count and said why; worked again, right, it showed "Recorded." with no mark and counted when checked.
- **What nobody checked as it was built.** No person has worked the lab. No screen reader was used. One browser, headless Chromium on Linux, and no real device. No contrast was measured. The builder looked at four sheets that hold the picture of every station before and after its action and the notes of station E, at both sizes in both themes, and at three whole pages of the build as it was (an equation refused, in the dark theme; the order at station C; the Record tab) and six of builds before it; the other pictures were taken and not looked at. No chemistry instructor has read any of it.
- **The review of 2026-10-12,** the lab's one deep review, held by an AI reviewer that had built none of it, by script in headless Chromium and by reading; its record is the last section of `docs/research/reactions/README.md`. What it changed, each marked above: an entry that is recorded is one row; a picture is drawn short once what is seen is recorded; the line of a term's controls is over the row, and the row no longer keeps the height of two lines; the picture in words is beside the one button; station C asks its kinds, equations and order once its three tubes are recorded, with a word under each tube and a word on each of the four buttons; a tick in that row of four is for an answer found right; under a checked tube of the three each line has its own tick or cross, where as built a sentence kept on one line ran out of its column into the next tube's; the Record tab's two sentences for a run; the sources of what is seen afterwards and of the tiles are kept back until a station counts; two hazard notes no longer say what a station shows; an open hint stands clear of the button over it; the strip's two parts are named for what they are before it burns; and the primer's sentence on the two cards that can wait. The lengths at 768×1024 as it left them, in Guided, at the six stages of the point on what was measured: station A 467, 795, 828, 839, 859 and 951 px; station B 467, 825, 907, 918, 938 and 972 px; station D 467, 613, 810, 871, 891 and 830 px; station E 467, 695, 810, 821, 841 and 956 px; station C 645 px before, 841 px with what is seen open, 1,048 px with the kinds open, 1,109 and 1,129 px with an equation open and built, 945 px at the order and 1,059 px done. So four stations are inside a screen at every stage, and station C is inside one until its kinds are asked and at most 153 px over one from there. It measured contrast (the least text 4.7 to 1 in the light theme and 6.9 in the dark; the words of a button that cannot be used now 3.7 and 4.9; the ink that edges everything drawn 4.4 to 10.4 against what it stands on), worked a whole lab in Challenge by emulated touch and one in Guided by the keyboard alone with every kind of wrong answer the engine names, changed the mode in the middle of a station, reloaded in the middle of one, and loaded the page on stored records that do not fit. What nobody has checked still: no person, no screen reader, one browser, no real device, and no chemistry instructor.

## 25. Polarity and mixing lab (added after v1)

The ninth of the labs taken from the owner's department's lab directions (section 17 says how those are used), and the second of three short ones from an introductory course, where it is called polarity, solubility and miscibility. The directions list what is set out; the procedure is the usual one, and each choice in it is the owner's to change. It uses the lab shell, the test tube that is explored and the observation form of sections 8 and 17.

### 25.1 Set-up

Some liquids mix into one and some stay as two layers; some solids dissolve in a liquid and some sit at the bottom of it. One rule covers most of it: like dissolves like. Liquids whose molecules have a positive end and a negative end, as water's have, mix with each other and dissolve salts and sugar; oily liquids, whose molecules have no such ends, mix with each other and with little else.
